is the gateway illuminating the nature of reality of the self-accomplished. hūṃ is the gateway illuminating the nature of reality of the root. rmūṃ is the gateway illuminating the nature of reality of things just as they are.

Lord of Laṅkā, these are the illumination of phenomena through the miracle of letters. Why is that? Lord of Laṅkā, by way of letters, all phenomena come to be understood as being like space. One comes to obtain dhāraṇī, sound, mindfulness, intelligence, realization, and clarity. In the same way, letters also open the gateway to all phenomena. They also indicate the meaning. They also bring realization of the intrinsic nature. Lord of Laṅkā, these are the illumination as the nature of reality by way of the miracle of letters.

Lord of Laṅkā, what is the miracle in which phenomena appear uncontrived? It is as follows:

The natures of phenomena that appear as signs
Appear within the state of the nature of reality, free of signs.
The natures of sentient beings that appear as things
Appear within the state of mind itself, free of things.
The natures of all that appears in its multiplicity
Appear within the state of suchness, just as it is.
The natures that appear as the five poisons of affliction
Appear within the state of the five wisdoms of liberation.
The natures that appear as the root, the three poisons,
Appear within the state of the three bodies, spontaneously present.
The natures that appear as concepts of apprehended and apprehender
Appear within the state where expanse and wisdom are not two.
The natures that appear as act and agent
Appear within the state where means and wisdom are joined.
The natures that appear as the aggregates of the perishing collection
Appear within the state of the undefiled aggregates of liberation.
The natures of saṃsāra, thoroughly afflicted,
Appear within the state of nirvāṇa, completely purified.
The natures of the world, its moving dust and its unmoving,
Appear within the state of the forty-two victors.
The natures of nirvāṇa and of saṃsāra
Appear within that very state, unsought and uncontrived.

Thus it resounds everywhere and becomes renowned. Lord of Laṅkā, that is the miracle of the appearance of the Dharma, and these too are fully explained as one, each distinct and unmixed."

This concludes the seventeenth chapter, the teaching of the miracle of the appearance of the Dharma, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Lord of Laṅkā, what is the miracle of expressing all? It is as follows:

All phenomena, unmixed and fully complete,
The meaning of how they definitively are,
Expressed just as it is, without distortion:
That itself is the great path of utter purity.
The intrinsic nature of body, speech, and mind
Is nowhere at all; it is just that.
Though the victors are immeasurable and infinite,
They are nowhere else; they are that itself.

Delusion, unmixed, is total bewilderment;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Aversion, unmixed, is the tormenting mind;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Pride, unmixed, is a great mountain;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Desire, unmixed, is clinging to everything;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Jealousy, unmixed, is total turmoil;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Form, unmixed, is fear itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Feeling, unmixed, is experiencing itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Perception, unmixed, is grasping itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Perception, unmixed, is making manifest;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Consciousness, unmixed, is knowing things as distinct;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Suffering, unmixed, is torment everywhere;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The elements, unmixed, are the seed itself;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The sense sources, unmixed, are the door of arising;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Cause and condition, unmixed, are gathering and dispersing itself;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Non-attachment, unmixed, is giving itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Purity, unmixed, is free of torment;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The unmoving, unmixed, is undisturbed;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Diligence, unmixed, is unsurpassable;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Contemplation, unmixed, is gathering itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Cutting through, unmixed, is free of attachment;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The immeasurable intentions and the four absorptions,
Being unmixed, undisturbed, and fully complete,
Are the empty, selfless nature of reality,
Uncontrived suchness alone.
All things, too, unmixed, are arising itself;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The liberation of emptiness, unmixed, is solitude;
Signlessness, unmixed, is peace itself;
Wishlessness, unmixed, dispels suffering;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless,
Uncontrived suchness alone.
Liberation, unmixed, is complete unfolding;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The powers, unmixed, are beyond doubt;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Fearlessness, unmixed, is supremely steadfast;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Compassion, unmixed, is protection itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Loving-kindness, unmixed, is bringing benefit;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Joy, unmixed, is without regret;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Equanimity, unmixed, is spaciousness itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Form, unmixed, is connection itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Meditative absorption, unmixed, is undistracted;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Unmixedness itself cannot be stolen away;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Total liberation, unmixed, is directly evident;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Phenomena, unmixed, are manifold;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Wisdom, all-knowing, is without arising or ceasing;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The expanse itself, though nothing at all, is inexhaustible;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
Emanations arise from nothing, their stream unbroken;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The enjoyment body, unmixed, is perfectly endowed;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
The dharmakāya is utter purity itself;
Being fully complete, it is empty and selfless.
When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
All things, unmixed, are distinct;
Being fully complete, they are empty and selfless.

When that very thing, unmixed, is fully complete,
It is uncontrived suchness alone.
From the miracle of expressing all phenomena
Uncontrived suchness appears.
Unobservable as the middle, it is secrecy itself,
The domain of experience of sublime beings.
This great vehicle of the vajra secret mantra
Is clear as the Tathāgata himself.
Why? Because the vajra secret mantra,
Sentient beings, and buddhas are not distinct.
The yogin of the vajra secret mantra
Is a being who has perfected means.
If one does not possess vajra yoga,
One is parted from means and wisdom.
Therefore the great yogin
Should never transgress
The nature of the four miracles
Of the sublime vajra secret mantra.

When this had been said, it resounded everywhere and became renowned: "Unmixed and fully complete, all phenomena are Samantabhadra himself." Lord of Laṅkā, these are the miracle of expressing all, and these too, each distinct and unmixed, are fully explained as the one reality.

This concludes the eighteenth chapter, called the teaching of the miracle of expressing all, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Lord of Laṅkā, what then is the miracle of instruction? The explanation of calm abiding as fully complete is as follows: everything, in all its multiplicity, is simply fully complete as things just as they are. How is that? It is as follows:

In that very unmoving sky,
However many distinct moving particles there are,
The great ocean of nonconceptual wisdom
Has no aspects whatsoever apart from one.
In the great maṇḍala, spontaneously present by nature,
Even the names of the lesser, of the bodhisattva,
And of the yoga of conduct do not exist.
From the spontaneous, equal heart of awakening,
Having found the equality of the definitive intention,
Just as it is, uncontrived, suchness itself,
The profound, sublime, definitive meaning,
By the great and sublime wonder
In the vajra essence itself, within itself,
Was explained thus as the meaning of the intention:

How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From the unborn, all things are born;
In being born itself, there is no birth.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From the unceasing, all things cease;
In ceasing itself, there is no cessation.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From the unobservable, all things are observed;
In observing itself, there is no observing.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From non-abiding, all things abide;
In abiding itself, there is no abiding.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From no coming and going comes coming and going itself;
In coming and going itself, there is no coming and going.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Uncontrived, unadulterated, nothing made,
Just as it is, the intrinsic nature is utterly pure.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From the nature that has no nature
Great bliss is spontaneously present.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Mind itself, without ground or root,
Is the root of all phenomena.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Neither empty nor not empty,
The middle is not something to be observed.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
When definitively examined, it is permanence itself;
In permanence and annihilation themselves there is no antidote.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
When definitively cut off, there is no annihilation;
It is awakening itself, the stream of error cut off.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
The intrinsic nature of each and every phenomenon
Is buddhahood itself, the stream of error cut off.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Any phenomenon other than buddhahood itself
Is not found by buddhahood itself.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Nowhere in the boundless and infinite
Is the perfect buddha to be sought at all.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Mind itself, conceptual thought, is the buddha;
It does not exist anywhere other than that.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
The three realms of existence are primordially the essence itself,
Unmistaken, uncontrived, spontaneously present.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
The expanse, spontaneously present by nature,
Is perfect buddhahood without any searching.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From every side and in every way, the state is all-good;
All is primordially all-pervading, marvelously renowned.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Apart from that very essence of awakening
Nothing whatsoever exists.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Though all are buddhas,
Being obscured by concepts, they do not know it.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Because in conceptual thought itself there is no conceptual thought,
The essence of the sugatas comes from nowhere else.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
In awakening, the essence of all,
The intention is primordially spontaneous.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
In the vajra secret essence
There is no accomplishing and nothing to be accomplished.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
In the primordial, sublime result,
How could there be anything to attain?
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
This secret essence of secret mantra
Is the secret of all buddhas and sentient beings.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
The secret gathering, the marvelous source,
Is the vajra essence, the gathering itself.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
This essence of the sublime secret
Is the certainty of all buddhas.

How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From the secret moon
The profound bindu of speech arose.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Through union in equality with all buddhas,
Appearances of form are clear like rainbows in the sky.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From the illusory wisdom of appearance and existence
The net of emanations arose like banks of cloud.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
From the net of emanations and means
The noose that seizes the two extremes arose.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The speech of all perfect buddhas:
Yoga free of activity, concordant engagement, conduct,
Union, means, and action: from these the tantras arose.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Insight and calm abiding,
The two pure extremes, definitely arose.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
The sublime scriptures of the collections and treasuries
Are definitively established in their source.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
However many the domains of those to be trained,
So many doors of speech arise.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
This, marvelously renowned throughout infinity,
Is the sublime lineage of the Word itself.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
Reality just as it is
Is the supreme essence from which the teaching arises.
How wonderful! The amazing, marvelous Dharma,
The secret of all perfect buddhas:
This reasoning of the profound gathering
Is the mother, the essence from which buddhas arise.
It makes manifold aspects appear:
The vajra mind, unchanging and unentering,
Immeasurable, its speech infinite,
From which manifold precious qualities arise,
All spontaneously present, beyond doubt.
In the vajra essence itself, within itself,
The sun, the maṇḍala of enlightened mind, has risen.

Beyond thought and inconceivable,
Are the immeasurable eighty-four thousand doors;
Though the renowned enumerations are beyond conception,
There is nothing but the one definitive vehicle.
So long as one has not entered the secret itself with certainty,
The doors of the vehicles are inconceivable;
As soon as the secret itself appears with certainty,
There is no door of any vehicle beyond it.
All enumerations of expression and teaching
Are provisional meaning, not that itself.
To find the meaning by uttering words and signs
Is like searching for gold in a dark pit with a lamp.
Breaking open the treasury of the secret essence,
One brings forth the great treasure of the sublime ones.
By a single bindu all is pervaded;
All the great scriptures are definitively taught.
If these are liberated in the state of that itself,
To see the secret mantra is to be buddha,
And all is great bliss, the mind of awakening,
Free of concepts, stainless, utterly pure.
In great bliss itself great bliss is perfected;
The essence, the sun of wisdom, has risen.
All without exception is sealed by the bindu;
Primordially unsought, it need not be sought.
Cause and result, indivisible, are that itself;
Yet in what is unmoving, nonconceptual, and beyond objects,
Through conceptual grasping at cause and result,
All are bound by illusion and delusion.
When the sun of self-arisen wisdom itself
Abides uncontrived, just as it is,
There is no going astray within the essence itself.
Since all things are the essence of awakening,
All abide in bliss without seeking.
Though one passes along the course of saṃsāra, of appearance and existence,
One goes nowhere from the expanse of the essence.
Though one traverses grounds without end,
One never moves from that essence.
Though all the immeasurable victors are brought together,
The essence does not increase at all.
Though endless sentient beings arise,
That itself does not diminish from it.
Whatever appears in the four modes of birth and the five streams
Is buddhahood itself, the body of wisdom.
Since all are one in that itself,
Primordially buddha, great bliss itself,
It is the essence without arising or ceasing.
The three maṇḍalas, without act or agent,
Are primordially accomplished, spontaneously present.
Whoever abides in this practice,
That yogin is fortunate.
However one meditates, practices, or acts,
It is that itself, the supreme meaning.
From the very essence of all victors
Wondrous blessings arise in vast measure.
The mother of the ocean of victors,
Buddha, great bliss, arises from it.
This essence of all without exception,
Abiding as the unelaborated bindu,
For the sake of the victors' heirs and all sentient beings
Teaches itself and explains itself.
Calm abiding is nonconceptual, pervading space;
Insight is undeluded, clear as the colors of the rainbow.
From the unmixed and the fully complete,
The definitive explanation of the intention is taught.

Lord of Laṅkā, since such an echo arose from emptiness, the explanation of all things as unmixed and fully complete is Samantabhadra himself. Lord of Laṅkā, thus all those definitive explanations of the intention should be understood as ground, entry, application, and root; as having come about from certainty alone; and as appearance, expression, and teaching.

When he had said this, the Lord of Laṅkā and the other bodhisattvas of inferior bodies rejoiced. Understanding all things as unmixed and fully complete, they were liberated without center or edge, and found relief.

This concludes the nineteenth chapter, the explanation of what is fully complete, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

This concludes the fifth teaching on how it came about, the full deliberation on wisdom, the seal of Dharma relating the account of the definitive explanation of the intention of all buddhas, from the Way of Going Forth in the Great Vehicle.

Then again the Lord of Laṅkā, that bodhisattva great being, put a question to the Lord of Secrets about the sixth of the twelve phrases given earlier, the one that says, "It occurred when the occasion for those to be tamed had arrived." He asked: "O Lord of Secrets, when you yourself told the account showing how the supreme secret of the Great Vehicle came about, you set down the words, 'It also occurred when the occasion for those to be tamed had arrived.' Which worldly being is renowned as having been fully tamed when the occasion for his taming had arrived?"

He said: "Lord of Laṅkā, the special one to be tamed, who is the cause for the arising of secret mantra, is the wrathful one of terrifying form who burns up the world. When the time for his taming had definitely arrived and he was fully tamed, all the buddhas of every direction and time gathered at the place of the essence of awakening and urged on the holy beings. And so, in just the ways it happened, he was tamed by terrifying clouds of wrathful ones, as many as there are atoms, like the Complete Array of the Lamp, the emanation of Vajrasattva. It is renowned that this occurred at that very time, at that very moment."

He said: "Lord of Secrets, where did that one to be tamed come from? What were his actions and his births? How did he become the cause for the arising of secret mantra?"

When he had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said: "O Lord of Laṅkā, it was like this. In a time gone by, immeasurably beyond memory, there was a buddha called Akṣobhya, who appeared in the eon called Complete Joy, in the buddhafield called Manifest Joy. Within his teaching there appeared a monk who had gone forth, called Youthful Hard to Subdue, who turned the wheel of the Dharma of the Great Vehicle just as it is, who dwelt in yoga that was unfabricated and unadulterated in each and every respect, and who had come to be a holy being. He saw all sentient beings as children of the Victor and devoted himself solely to urging them on.

At that time, at that period, the actions of beginningless and endless saṃsāra, of beginningless and endless births, and the immeasurable births, could never be fully told. But if we take one portion of birth brought about by one occasion of karma, it was like this. Nearby there lived a householder called Keukaya, who had a son called Black Liberation, and that son had a servant called Denbak.

At that time, at that period, the people who had learned the path of yoga from the monk Youthful Hard to Subdue all abided on the path of yoga and practiced yoga. At that time, at that period, Black Liberation thought this over. He had heard that this monk Youthful Hard to Subdue teaches everything just as it is, and he wondered, 'Are there teachings with a path in which one practices everything just as one pleases?' He asked various people, and all of them said that it was so.

Then, together with his servant, he went to where the monk was. Arriving at his dwelling, with earnest longing he made this request: 'Great monk, I have heard that you are skilled in the means of teaching a path in which one acts in everything just as one likes. Is that certainly so?'

When he had asked this, the monk said: 'Son of good family, it may well be that there are things that happen in just that way.'

When he had said this, Liberation and his servant were delighted, glad and overjoyed, and said: 'Please accept me and my servant as subject to your command. I too wish to take from you the path in which nothing whatever is blocked, and to abide in yoga, so please definitely accept me as your foremost disciple.'

So he requested, and the monk said: 'Yes, excellent! Let it be so.' Having given his consent, he let Black Liberation go forth and took him as his foremost disciple. Denbak he took on as the keeper of the hours, and made him the servant of the monk Liberation.

Then the monk Liberation asked: 'Great monk, what is that supreme path in which all things are just as they are?'

He said:
'When suchness itself is left unfabricated,
Even if one dwells in and practices the four things,
It is like clouds in the sky:
This is the path of supreme yoga.'

When he had said this, Liberation and his servant rejoiced and said: 'How wonderful! You are skilled in means! It is certain! So it is! I entrust myself! I shall abide fully in that in everything.'

Then, because he practiced according to the words exactly as they sounded, the monk Liberation did not find certainty of understanding. Turned toward the words alone, he practiced only the four things and clung to them; he dressed his body in the attire of a holy one, but with his mind he entered the path to the lower realms. The servant Denbak, having found certainty about the meaning on which the words rest, was joined to supreme understanding. Turned toward the meaning alone, he practiced only suchness and held to it; being a thorough yogin, he dressed his body in the attire of a lowly person, but with his mind he entered the path of certainty in what is supreme.

Then Liberation and his servant disagreed in their views. Liberation said: 'We two have one master, and the same words came to us both. How is it that we disagree with each other about this?'

Denbak said: 'I have heard nothing other than this very conclusion that I now hold.'

Liberation said: 'I too have now found certainty, and I have heard nothing other than this conduct.'

When he had said this, it turned into a great dispute. Because their views disagreed, he parted from his servant, swore that they would never see or meet each other again, drove the servant away to a distant land, and cast him off.

Then a mountain of wrong, overweening pride arose in the monk Liberation, and he asked the master which of them had found the right conclusion. The monk Youthful Hard to Subdue, practicing the natural state unfabricated and unadulterated, without pretense, speaking straightforwardly of the nature of things, declared: 'It is as Denbak holds.' At this Liberation was utterly enraged and said: 'If the master were impartial, he would have said we are both alike. But not treating us as equals, he has surely humiliated and refuted me. So I will not let this master stay in this land either; I will make these lands my own domain.' Having vowed this, great pride arose in him, and he drove the master too away to a distant land and cast him off completely.

Then, because the enumerations of the Tathāgata's speech are many and vast, and because the Teacher's way of teaching in every respect is hard to fathom, that Liberation did not fully comprehend it. Without weighing it with the two measures, he drew it toward his own measure of what was valid and clung to it, and so he twisted everything into falsehood. He himself plunged into an ocean of wrongness and took up fierce, wrong austerities and a great, wrong discipline. He took human corpses from the charnel grounds and made them his food, flayed their skins and wore them as his garments. As his companions he kept many living creatures: black jackals, foot-stamping wolves, hand-striking bears, predatory killer birds, charnel-ground kaṅka birds, devouring charnel pigs and the like. He took the life of every creature and feasted on flesh and blood. He gathered a great crowd of prostitutes and indulged in great passion. Never departing from the four things, he passed entirely beyond the suchness that is the true nature of those four things.

Then that birth came to an end. In the births that followed, he took five hundred births in the body of a black jackal, engaged only in the action of killing living beings, and ate their flesh and warm blood. In the births after that, he took five hundred births in various bodies, such as dogs and the like, whose action is vicious killing and whose food is flesh and blood; he was killed by others, and passing from birth to birth he became nothing but flesh. In the births after that, he took various bodies, such as the flesh-eating nele bird, the blood-drinking red-beak, the charnel-ground kaṅka bird and the leg-licking rutsé bird; he was killed by others, and passing from birth to birth he became nothing but flesh. In the births after those, he took births in the bodies of various insects and very large insects, such as flies, puka worms and corpse-eating leg-suckers; these too were killed by their enemies, and passing from birth to birth he became nothing but flesh. In the births after that, he took five hundred births as a creature living in a swamp of filth and vomit; he was killed by enemies, and passing from birth to birth he became nothing but flesh. In the births after those, he took five hundred births as a creature that lives in the bodies of living beings and also feeds on them; he was killed by enemies, and passing from birth to birth he became nothing but flesh. Again, in the births after that, he became a creature living inside the flesh of the slain, the corpses of the dead, putrefying matter, the stones of various valleys, cliffs, great tree trunks and the like; dwelling there and living on them as food, he was killed by enemies, and did nothing but pass from birth to birth. In the births after that, he dwelt five hundred births as a creature living in the city of the womb, and made his dwelling and his livelihood from it.

Again, in the birth after that, he dwelt in the abode of Avīci, in what is called the Vajra Hell, for eighty thousand of its own lifespans. The measure of the suffering in that hell cannot be told. Why is that? If one were to tell the measure of that suffering before anyone, whoever heard it would faint. At that time, at that period, in the single instant in which he cried, "Alas, what wrong is this?", he passed through that hell. When Vajrasattva, the lord of sages, the king of Dharma, taught him the Dharma, saying, "This is your very own karma," he brought that karma to mind, fully understood it, and was filled with remorse. By that remorse alone he passed from that place, and then dwelt in the abode of the hell Pratāpana, again for eighty thousand of its own lifespans, just as in Avīci. Then, passing on again, he dwelt in the abodes of the hells Lesser Heat, Mahāraurava, Raurava, Saṃghāta, Kālasūtra, Blazing, Saṃjīva and the rest, again for eighty thousand of their lifespans each, just as in Avīci and Pratāpana. Whenever those came to an end, he was likewise thrown into the hells of other world systems. Passing from those as well, he was born wherever there was an eon of destruction by famine, disease or weapons. Whenever those destructions had emptied such places, he would again take birth wherever else there was a time of destruction. Freed even from those, for eighty thousand eons he took birth and died in species that are killed for their flesh and bones. Freed from that, he was tormented for five hundred births in the world of Yama. Passing from there, he took birth in the race of pūtanas. In the birth after that, he took birth in the race of piśācas. In the birth after that, he took birth in the race of pūtanas, born as the pūtana called Desiring Inclusion, son of the pūtana called Stiff Neck.

In the birth after that, he was conceived in the womb of a prostitute in the land called Laṅkāpura, and ripened toward birth. He was born there, and in the ninth month his mother died. People then said among themselves, "If we raise this orphan bastard, the whole land will be defiled, so it is right to put him in his mother's armpit." There was a poisonous tree called Nalabi; at its root a charnel pig of delusion had its nest, in its middle a venomous snake of hatred had its coils, and at its top a poison bird of desire had its nest. At its foot they all built a tomb for the mother. They carried her corpse on a bier, placed it inside, and put the child too in his mother's armpit.

That orphan clung to his mother's corpse and sucked at her breasts, drawing out lymph, and lived on that for seven days. Then, sucking out blood, he lived on that for seven days. Then he ate her breasts and lived on that for ten days. Then he ate her entrails until they were gone, and lived on them. Then he ate her flesh until it was gone, and lived on that for seven months. Then that youth grew strong and lived on the corpses of the charnel ground. Then, when he had grown very strong, he ate corpses as his food and wore shrouds and skins. Through the power of the nature he had gained by eating the raw flesh of human corpses, the mātṛkās of the charnel grounds, the piśācas, the hosts of pretas and the many hosts of nonhumans came under his control. He became lord of all the vicious beings of the charnel grounds, a savage of utterly terrifying form who stole the splendor of others, devouring people alive, whoever they were, and stealing their vitality. Because he had eaten his mother's raw flesh, people everywhere knew him by the name Mother-Eater Mataṅka.

From the breath of that great preta's mouth came fevers of heat, which spread through the elements, so that every embodied being was tormented by fierce, piercing pain. From his nostrils came diseases of cold, which spread through the elements and afflicted everyone. Anyone struck by his malice was certain to die. By reciting the nail mantras of Rudra, he brought everything under his power. His huge body was ash-colored and bluish gray, adorned with dots of blood and fat. He wore a garland of skulls as a necklace, and was clad in skins smeared red with blood. He constantly drank from a skull ladle filled with blood. His matted hair was dun and black, bristling and split in two. He ate oil, grain and skull bones as his food. His body was adorned with ashes, red lac and collyrium. Bird wings grew from his body, so that he moved through the sky like a bird. Naked and unhindered, he moved without obstruction, like a fish in water. Whoever saw him, his eyes glaring and flickering, was struck with fear and fainted. His body was rough, with the bristles of a pig; his limbs had become snakes, and he was adorned with snakes. His nails had grown into the claws and beaks of kaṅka birds, so that he crushed the flesh and bones of any embodied being he seized. Through the power of past habituation, he set his mind on killing every kind of embodied being. He was attached to gathering in every woman's sense faculties. He terrified all embodied beings, overpowered them, and left them trembling and quaking. The worldly mātṛkās, bhūtas and powerful yakṣas bowed to him and regarded him as their chief. All the vicious ones served him as his agents, messengers, servants and under-servants, slaves and under-slaves, and he became Maheśvara, the great lord who wields power over all.

This concludes the twentieth chapter, the teaching of the impure line of births, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then that fierce preta let out a savage cry of pride, and loudly proclaimed in a voice of arrogance: "How wonderful! I am the great lord of all! If there is any lord or domain other than me, that too shall become mine. I have become the one and only great and mighty one, without rival!"

At that time the ways of beings came to be like this. All their understanding was dulled, and because Māra had blessed them, all of them became entirely void and cut off, nothing but those who fall to the extreme of nihilism. Clinging to a self, they were nothing but eternalists. Entering cessation, they were nothing but those who are stopped and gone beyond. Becoming vicious and savage, they were nothing but those who fall into Raurava. Holding wrong views, they were nothing but those who fall into Avīci. Becoming greedy and miserly, they were nothing but those who fall into the world of Yama. Becoming greatly distracted, they were nothing but those who fall into the states without leisure. Becoming deluded by great bewilderment, they were nothing but those who fall into the state of animals. Becoming intoxicated with great passion, they were nothing but those who go into the realm of the womb. The tree of awakening had withered, and they were nothing but those in whom the root of mind was burnt.

Lord of Laṅkā, this too was renowned, renowned again, and became renowned:

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings
Strove exceedingly at emptiness.
Through annihilation into emptiness,
They became the great realm of annihilation.
Then, when they passed on again,
The path of freedom and liberation was cut off.

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings
Strove exceedingly at the view of a self.
Through the great self, enduring and eternal,
Everything became eternal.
Then, when that came about once more,
They became deluded about the holy.

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings
Came to strive exceedingly at cessation.
Through the practice of extreme cessation,
They came to be stopped and wholly gone beyond.

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings
Became exceedingly wrathful, savage and vicious.
Being exceedingly wrathful, savage and vicious,
They fell into Raurava.
Then those beings, passing on from there,
Linked to rebirth by the karma of their habitual tendencies,
Became exceedingly vicious.
Their minds too were blessed by Māra:
Poisons, diseases, fevers and sorceresses
Shot forth harmful, piercing pains and stabbing arrows.
Māras and vināyakas harmed liberation.
Exceedingly savage, fierce and brutal,
They destroyed the three worlds,
Devoured the sacred commitments,
Turned back the river of the holy at its source,
And caused the worlds of gods and humans to pass away.

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings
Came to abide in wrong view.
Through exceedingly wrong view,
They fell into the great hell of Avīci.
Then, if they came forth again,
The holy path itself was cut off.

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings
Became exceedingly greedy and miserly.
Through being exceedingly tormented, miserly and greedy,
They fell into the realm of the pretas and Yama.
Then, when they came forth again,
They became exceedingly withered and poor.
Exceedingly tormented by hunger and thirst,
They came to kill and destroy one another.

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings
Became exceedingly distracted by laziness.
Through the laziness of great distraction,
They came to fall into the states without leisure.
Then, when they came forth again,
They were caught in the eon of the three eons.

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings
Became exceedingly bewildered by delusion.
Being exceedingly bewildered by delusion,
They fell into the state of deluded animals.
Then, when they came forth again,
Even the path of seeing and appearance was cut off.

In times gone by, long ago,
In the scriptures of the Tathāgata,
The great being, the eternal one,
The way of beings was once like this:
At that time all sentient beings

Became exceedingly intoxicated with passion.
Being exceedingly intoxicated with passion,
They fell into the very city of the womb.
Then, when they came forth again,
They were tormented by the fire of desire.

So it resounded everywhere.

This concludes the twenty-first chapter, called "What Happened in the Past," from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then the Lord of Secrets said: "Lord of Laṅkā, in this way that fierce preta crushed the greatness of every person who came under his power, and, deeming himself supreme, said, 'How wonderful! Who is there greater than me, the lord? Who is there at all?'

Now in the land of Laṅkā, in its town, on the island of Tsoko Katangmar, on the slopes of Kinnara's Leap, at the peak called Where the Sky Iron Is, there was one called the Lord of Laṅkā, king of the rākṣasas, a disciple of the Buddha called Munīndra, the Lord of Sages. His power and prowess were extraordinary and most wondrous; his understanding was complete, and his skillful means, like an ocean, arose like clouds. He was renowned in the world as a great one. When that winged preta heard of this, he became utterly agitated and furious, blazed up as a mass of Rudra's fire, and by his own miraculous power and skill in flight he alighted on the summit of the mountain of Kinnara's Leap. When he proclaimed in a savage voice of pride, 'rutra matra maratra!', the towns of the land of Laṅkā shook and shook, shook violently and shook, and swayed back and forth. All the rākṣasas and rākṣasīs of Laṅkā were frightened and terrified. 'How wonderful! What is this?' they cried, their eyes flickering, trembling with fright. They saw and heard that a savage, fierce and furious winged yakṣa was settled in their lair.

Then the great Lord of Laṅkā and the host of rākṣasas gathered an army and strove and strove to overthrow him. But that winged rākṣasa displayed a miraculous feat called Cowing the Rākṣasa Host, and proclaimed in a savage voice of pride:

'I am Maheśvara, the Great Lord.
The Four Great Kings are my servants;
The eight classes of gods and spirits are my slaves.
Since I am the master of the supreme secret,
Who is there who could rival me?
rutra matra maratra!'

At this the rākṣasas were overpowered and cowed. Some proclaimed, 'This is one who makes the realm of the rākṣasas victorious; he is worthy to be our chief.' Some proclaimed, 'This is one who destroys the world; he will be our adversary.' Some proclaimed, 'This is a great worldly lord.' Some proclaimed, 'This is the Gaṇapati of the hosts of ḍākinīs.'

The king of the rākṣasas, the Lord of Laṅkā, ordered them: 'This is the son of a bhūta, called Desiring Inclusion. He terrifies the three worlds; apart from suchness, he ruins everything and brings it under his power. He is a perverse lord of hosts, and no one else can tame him. The buddhas, the blessed ones, will be the ones to counter him: their emanation, the Vajra Holder, will tame him and establish him in the supreme certainty. Since this has been truly prophesied, it is certain that through him, as the cause, the wondrous Dharma of the Great Vehicle, the certainty of the supreme secret mantra, will arise in the world. We too, by becoming his servants, will see the face of the Buddha Vajragarbha and will find the certainty of the supreme secret mantra. So you must definitely set your hearts on him.'

The hosts of rākṣasas rejoiced, and, not disregarding whatever their own commander said, they set their hearts on him in that way. All of them went to become servants of that winged rākṣasa, and all of them said, 'We too set our hearts on the Great Lord himself.' So they declared, and they held him as their supreme chief, and he took them as his retinue.

Then, again, that winged rākṣasa, the one renowned as the leader of the rākṣasas, called Mataṅka Rudra, was crushing others with the greatness of his pride, crying out, 'Who is there greater than me? Who could it be? rutra matra maratra!' It was then reported that the leader of the gods, called Mataṅka Runa, was outstanding in power, miraculous ability, skillful means and greatness, and could match him. At this the black māra Rudra became utterly furious and enraged and proclaimed a savage cry of pride. By his own miraculous power and skill in flight he alighted at the abode of Mataṅka Runa and challenged him again to a trial of strength. Since they were not alike, like tea and butter churned together, and since both were embodied beings, they roared and came to blows. In the onslaught, Rudra blew out hot and cold breath and sent forth the weapons of disease, which spread through that other one's whole body. His body was covered with pustules of black flame, and tormented by unbearable piercing pain, that very being was killed in the fight. His corpse fell upon Jambudvīpa and appears there as the eight lands of the abodes of virtue.

The wives, children and retinue of the one Rudra had killed in the fight went over to become the retinue of that winged yakṣa. The asuras, piśācas and vetālas all went over to that māra's side. The four kings, though they ran to all sorts of refuge, were not freed, and became his slaves. Śakra, Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Rudra and all the rest, though they ran to all sorts of refuge, were not freed, and became his slaves. He seized all their wives as well: some he made his wives, some his handmaidens, some his concubines, some his mistresses, some his slave women, and some his messengers and envoys. He utterly ruined the existence of gods and humans, and everything became a realm of lusterless black.

And this too was renowned, renowned again, and became renowned.

Black, with garuḍa wings and skill in flight,
Savage, fierce and utterly brutal,
With ugly face and ugly form,
With harsh and grating cries,
On heaps of flesh, heaps of bones, worms and pus,
On blood and the fluids of excrement and urine
He feasts, and lives on them always.
That fierce leader of the perverse
Ruined the existence of gods and humans here.
He slew the very person of Karuna,
The leader of the gods,
And made his retinue his own encampment.
Through the blazing fire of vicious, piercing pain,
All of them went to the four leaders.
Those, in turmoil and great terror,
Went to the Indras for refuge.
Then the gods together with the Indras
Went to Brahmā for refuge.
Brahmā too, with gods and Indras,
Went to Desiring Inclusion for refuge.
Brahmā, Desiring Inclusion, gods and Indras
Went to Rudra for refuge.
Brahmā, Desiring Inclusion and the Indras,
Utterly frightened and terrified,
Went to the Vajra Holder for refuge.
Likewise, though they ran to whoever
Might offer refuge, they were not freed,
And became the slaves of that terrifier.

So it became renowned everywhere.

Then, behold the black māra Rudra! He brought all moving gods and humans under his power, and made his lair in the land of Laṅkā, in its town, on Tsoko Katangmar, on the mountain of Kinnara's Leap, at the place called Corpse Mountain, Skull Fortress. He planted the banner of Māra in the cave, raised the battle standard of Māra at the door, and surrounded himself with his own encampment. Fierce Rudra produced his miraculous power, and by that power he set Mount Meru on the tip of his finger and twirled it arrogantly above his head, proclaiming his savage cry of pride like this:

"rutra matra maratra! In the realms of the earth, who is greater than me? Who is there? How wonderful! Long ago, when the glorious one called Śākyamuni tamed the māra Lord of Death, the great supreme śrāvakas who appeared in his teaching spoke with words meant to pacify everything, saying, 'Listen to my command!' But I told them, 'Little boys with your robes slung over your shoulders, stay at ease in your own austerities!' I did not listen, and so even he could not bring me under his power. In his presence a god, the glorious Vajragarbha, lord of speech, emanated in various forms and, crying 'hri', tried to bring me under his control. But I myself snapped my fingers at him and commanded, 'Little rival, you too stay at ease!' So he too cut the cord and went back. In the fourteen tiers of the heavens I heard of a god, the glorious Vajragarbha, who had no rival, and him too I brought under my power. I have gathered them all in and laid them bare!"

So he proclaimed, and regarded himself as the one and only sovereign lord, without rival.

In just the way that the one who was Black Liberation ripened into Black Rudra, at that time, at that period, the one who was Youthful Hard to Subdue became, at the essence of awakening, the suchness of Vajrasattva. The one who was Denbak at that time, at that period, became the holy being of great and perfected prowess called Vajradhara.

This concludes the twenty-second chapter, called "The Teaching of the Sphere of Black Rudra," from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Here begins the fourth fascicle.

Then all the tathāgatas gathered at the essence of awakening, dissolved into emptiness, and made the holy being appear within emptiness. The sound of suchness itself resounded to him: "Holy being, why do you think of becoming completely and fully awakened when the work of your buddhafield is not fully complete? Why do you think of awakening when the deeds of a buddha are not complete? Why do you think of becoming completely and fully awakened without making diverse manifestations?"

When this had resounded, from that emptiness these words resounded: "O all tathāgatas, by the force of what aim will the work of the buddhafield be fully completed, and likewise the deeds of a buddha and the making of diverse manifestations?"

When this had resounded, there resounded again, from the suchness of all, the first of the three exhortations, in these words:

"You who have gone to bliss, unshakable,
In the unmoving sky of emptiness,
Suchness without reference or concept:
Please give rise to manifold conceptions!"

So it resounded. To that, from the holy being himself, these words resounded:

"From the state of nonconception, constantly,
Whatever emptiness arises through examination
Is itself the great conception:
Nonconception is the cause of conception."

So it resounded. Again, from the suchness of all, these words resounded:

"From the essence of the sphere of space,
The light of the nature appearing everywhere,
All-pervading, the domain of compassion:
Please give rise to boundless kingdoms!"

So it resounded. Again, from the holy being himself, these words resounded:

"The origin of all, the ground of all, the dharmadhātu,
Unborn, unceasing, appearing everywhere,
Inexpressible, beyond the reach of speech and thought,
Is the mother who gives birth to the boundless, the boundless."

So it resounded. Again, from the suchness of all, these words resounded:

"When you make diverse manifestations,
From the wisdom of the seal of speech
The victors appear as letters:
Please give rise to the wisdom of letters!"

So it resounded. Again, from the holy being himself, these words resounded:

"Suchness is the nature of letters:
Self-arisen wisdom shines as muṃ.
Mind itself is the nature of letters:
The manifold letters appear as hūṃ.
The nature of the letters of the victors
Is the seal letters, oṃ and the rest.
All things are the nature of letters:
The unborn secret letter a."

So it resounded. Again, from the suchness of all, these words resounded:

"Through wisdom perfected beyond,
Indicating the door of the awareness of yoga,
Making the signs of mind appear,
Please give rise to the wisdom of liberation!"

So it resounded. Again, from the holy being himself, these words resounded:

"The wisdom of all the buddhas
Always arises from nonconceptuality.
The nonconceptual mind of liberated wisdom
Appears to conception as conceptual signs."

So it resounded. Again, from the suchness of all, these words resounded:

"From the nonconceptual mirror of emptiness
Appear forms, unborn and unceasing.
Like a rainbow appearing in the sky,
Please arise from the expanse as a mudrā!"

So it resounded. Again, from the holy being himself, these words resounded:

"Though nothing is apprehended in suchness,
The expanse in which the nature appears everywhere
Is unmixed and fully complete,
And so the supreme form of the mudrā appears."

So it resounded.

When that first exhortation had exhorted him completely, the holy being, suchness itself, from that expanse of emptiness, appeared as a body of emptiness, just as the disc of the moon appears in water or a rainbow appears in the sky. As he abided in the abiding of a wisdom fixed nowhere, all the tathāgatas exhorted him decisively with this second exhortation:

"Nature unarisen and uncontrived, suchness,
State of great bliss, unmoving from the very beginning,
In which there is nothing at all to seek or to stir!
'Expanse of wisdom' is only a verbal convention;
That very thing is the source of things and of non-things.
Unassailable by anything, uncuttable, indestructible,
Primordial just as it is, unmoving supreme bliss,
Supreme Vajrasattva, great Ākāśagarbha,
Spiritual friend of the sugatas of the three times,
Noble lord, source from which many emanations arise,
Great luminous mirror, the nature of all,
Treasury, father and mother, that gives birth to infinite victors,
Equal to all who are the essence of awakening,
Mind of awakening unmoved from the self-pure expanse!

"There, in the beginning, all the pure buddhas,
Arisen from the three realms, the body of Vajragarbha,
Emanated manifold letters from infinite conceptions;
At the center, beautifully encircled by assemblies of heirs,
Please show yourself as a form body and purify the afflictions!

"There, in the beginning, Vajragarbha,
Arisen from the three realms, spontaneously present great bliss,
In order to liberate through skillful means the fierce, the hot-tempered and the malicious,
Emanated as hūṃ with a stainless, manifold intelligence;
At the center encircled by garlands in the manner of light rays,
By the power of your former commitment and aspiration,
Please reveal the form body that nothing can shake!

"There, in the beginning, Vairocana,
Arisen from the three realms, made all things appear;
In order to liberate through skillful means the darkness that deludes,
He emanated as oṃ with a pure awakened intelligence;
At the center encircled by garlands in the manner of many forms,
By the power of your former aspiration and commitment,
Please reveal the form body that appears as anything at all!

"There, in the beginning, the Source of Qualities,
Arisen from the three realms, the wish-fulfilling jewel,
In order to liberate through skillful means those deluded by desire,
Emanated as svaṃ with a pure intelligence that is a source;
At the center beautifully encircled by garlands of jewels,
By the power of your former aspiration and commitment,
Please reveal the form body from which all that is desired arises!

"There, in the beginning, Amitābha,
Arisen from the three realms, excellent speech, infinite light,
In order to liberate through skillful means those deluded by attachment,
Emanated as aṃ with a pure and stainless intelligence;
At the center encircled by garlands of melodious syllables of speech,
By the power of your former aspiration and commitment,
Please reveal the form body that displays all variety!

"There, in the beginning, Amogha,
Arisen from the three realms, accomplished all manner of things;
In order to liberate through skillful means those deluded about action,
He emanated as haṃ with a pure and diligent intelligence;
At the center encircled by garlands of deeds as a dance,
By the power of your former aspiration and commitment,
Please reveal the form body that accomplishes everything!"

When that second exhortation had exhorted him completely, from the illusion of the nature of reality there appeared the illusory body of wisdom, just as the disc of a face appears in the expanse of a mirror. As he abided as the self-appearance of wisdom, all the tathāgatas exhorted him with this third exhortation to manifest perfection, the perfection of enjoyment:

"You who have gone into suchness,
Please extend manifest perfection to the world,
And, just as the essence of awakening is,
Please appear for the sake of sentient beings!
In immeasurable world systems,
All the emanated buddhas
Of however many sugatas there are
Shine forth for the sake of every being.
For the sick of saṃsāra in the three realms
There is no other medicine that helps.
Compounding the medicine of genuine amṛta,
You purge saṃsāra's chronic disease.
Great and supreme physician who dispels
The sickness of suffering's piercing pain, come forth as the chief!
You who have become the enemy of every disease,
With an ocean of the medicine of amṛta
Please raise up all the multitudes of the sick
Tormented by the enemy, perverse disease!
You, the perfected being of great prowess,
Turn your mind to the essence of awakening,
Spread wide the radiance of your light rays,
And resolve to dispel the darkness!
Ah, being who has accomplished the aim,
Who has done what was to be done and completed the task,
Who has laid down the burden and attained his own aim,
The time has come to tame those who are here!
In the celestial palace Seen Through the Dharma,
In Akaniṣṭha, supreme among the Pure Abodes,
Come forth as the body of perfect enjoyment
And perform the deed for which the essence is exhorted!"

When he had been exhorted in this way,

Just as the disc of the sun
Rises through an opening in the clouds,
From the very expanse of the unmoving
The sun of the form body rose,

and he appeared in the celestial palace called Height of the Dharma.

This is the twenty-third chapter, on the exhortation of the miraculous display, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then that Dharma king of perfect enjoyment made the sign of the vajra opening that gathers all together, and from the boundless buddhafield Ghanavyūha, the Densely Arrayed, immeasurable victors gathered from every side.

At that time the assembly of buddhas of the playful display of compassion deliberated in unison:

"O, O, assembly from which buddhas of many kinds arise,
Appearing everywhere and fully arising here!
The malicious one, perverse in existence,
Propelled by the harsh seeds of saṃsāra,
And the hungry ghosts of great suffering
Have arisen in this world system through karma.
Rudra, through perverse austerities,
Has ripened into lord of the three realms of existence.
With the malicious splendor of arrogant Rudra,
This one who utterly crushes and destroys beings
Has become a wondrous, supreme field for taming.
Lords of compassion, all gathered together,
It is fitting that we cut the stream of his evil karma
And place him on the ground of great bliss."

So they deliberated. The assembly of the buddhas of the vajra lineage and their hosts deliberated as one:

"Ah! Assembled buddhas,
As happened before in times past,
So it has happened now, in this very time.
Burned upon the mountain of manifest perverse scripture,
Corrupted, his own being scorched,
Empowered by Māra,
A great and fierce maddened elephant,
With exceedingly unbearable great poison
He kills and destroys, bound for Raurava,
Clinging to the very end to corpse-lands and charnel grounds:
By what means can he be helped?"

So they deliberated.

The assembly of the buddhas of the jewel lineage and their hosts deliberated in unanimous accord:

"If this mad and malicious black one
Is left to do as he pleases,
With the waves of his unbearable ocean of poison
He will spread through the black realm;
He will cut off the lineage of the holy ones,
And the sun and the lamp will be destroyed.
Then, through the darkness of karma,
All will wander in pitch-black gloom."

So they deliberated. The assembly of the buddhas of the lotus lineage and their hosts deliberated in accord with a single voice:

"Just as what happened in times past,
When that Rudra of perverse understanding,
Propelled by the wheel of karma,
Arose from the expanse of the essence,
And through the application of the holy being
Māra was tamed by māra and set in order,
So what has arisen now, at this time,
This mountain of heaped-up, transforming poison:
The time has come for it to be destroyed and crushed
By the great hammer of the wrathful vajra."

So they deliberated. The assembly of the buddhas of the youthful lineage and their hosts deliberated in accord with a single voice:

"Great poison coiled within him,
Seized by an unbearable disease from which there is no release,
A corpse dead in the realm of eternalism and nihilism,
This blind man who has strayed from the path and lost it:
In order to crush the great gloom and pitch darkness
With the great, blazing, radiating and turbulent nails
Of the miraculous display of wrathful wisdom,
The ocean of victors has gathered, all of them,
From the domain of those who possess wisdom.
Here, where the cloud banks of the miraculous display
Of the wrathful vajra of wisdom have massed,
If the great ocean of poison is not dried up
By sorcery of manifold play,
In the guise of a child who enters in accord,
Is compassion here suited to the conditions?
For the sake of the holy commitment
Of the great compassion of the nature of reality,
Here the teaching of the four things is given."

So they deliberated. This is the twenty-fourth chapter, on the deliberation of the great assembly, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

When the deliberations of the great assembly had thus been fully concluded, they considered that the occasion had come to tame him by one of the four kinds of activity. In order to examine which one it should be, from the light rays of the Youthful Hard to Subdue, who had transformed into Vajragarbha, an emanation came forth as Munīndra, and all the tathāgatas commanded that emanation:

"Munīndra, this is the time!
By the blessing of truth just as it is,
Turn back the mass of fire of the four things,
And rouse the essence of awakening!"

Having been fully exhorted in this way, Munīndra appeared at the encampment of that lord of the charnel ground and spoke words that pacify everything. At that the lord of the charnel ground retorted scornfully and spoke these words: "Little boy, you have not tamed me. Formerly, when I was the yakṣa general called Rudra, many like you came and said, 'Enter my teaching! Listen to my command!', but even then I did not listen. So how should I now come under your power? Cast off your robe and your necklace, and then go happily about your own austerities!"

Having said this, and being utterly overwhelmed by the four things of Māra, Munīndra fell into the conceptions of the noble ones of the Pure Abodes and turned back.

Then all the tathāgatas again fully commanded Avalokiteśvara, the all-pervading emanation of the buddhas of the lotus lineage and their hosts:

"Lotus lineage, dharmakāya of all the victors,
Dharma lord Amitābha, Vajradharma,
Emanation of Avalokiteśvara, from hri display the great ferocious one!
Vāgīśvara, Lord of Speech, subdue him with the face of a horse!"

Exhorted thus, the emanation, the great Vajra Horse-Face, appeared at the abode of the great lord of the charnel ground and let out the cry of a fierce horse, piercing and shrill: "ha ha hi hi hri hu lu hu lu hu lu." At that, the encampment of the lord of existence was completely cowed and became somewhat timid. Yet that lord of the charnel ground himself proclaimed in the fierce voice of his pride, "ru tra ma tra ma tra," puffed himself up with ostentation and swaggered, and was not overpowered. Then he in turn commanded: "Little Neck, before this too those like you did not overpower me, so you will not subdue me. Go happily back to wherever your own abode is!" So he scolded him.

Again, being utterly overwhelmed by the four things of Māra, the conceptions of the noble ones of the Pure Abodes arose in him, and he thought of turning back. But since for a tathāgata, who is like space, there is no clean or unclean, he did not turn back but pressed in again. That one himself sank down, and the Tathāgata Vāgīśvara entered him in accord, dissolving into his place of pleasure.

Then that Tathāgata made his head emerge at the crown of that one's head and his feet at the soles of that one's feet, and expanding with "Vajra hūṃ" he swelled through the whole of his body. Unable to bear it, that one at last let out a wailing roar: "Ah, the agony! Whatever you are doing, do it quickly!"

Then that Tathāgata purified that one's obscurations and, in bliss, emerged from the crown of his head and neighed a horse's cry into the sky. At this the hosts of rākṣasas were scattered in confusion, and overpowered and cowed they said, "We become subjects of the great hero. Please command us what to do." When they had made this request, that Tathāgata said: "I have not tamed you. Your antidote will arise, called Vajra hūṃ, empowered with the empowerment of all the tathāgatas: extremely fierce, ferocious, turbulent, savage and unbearable, a great māra of māras, the terrifying Blazing One. Go to him for refuge!" Having said this, he went, beyond all reference, to the palace of the dharmadhātu.

Then all the tathāgatas of the Guhyasamāja deliberated in great assembly. When they examined through which door of the four activities the occasion had come to tame him, since one who is tamed by pacifying and enriching is of a different kind, they decided definitively, in sacred deliberation, to tame him by magnetizing and wrathful activity, and they fully proclaimed the sūtras of their decision, as follows.

The assembly of tathāgatas whose conduct is liberation proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For one pacified by complete cessation,
Wrath will bring no benefit.
From that very uncontrived yoga,
All you tathāgatas, act in peace!
If even by peaceful ones in the manner of yoga
The sprout of existence can be stopped,
What need is there to speak of rousing from cessation
By the power of a yoga empty of existence and nonexistence?
As if moistening utterly dry rocks,
In order to rouse those in cessation there,
From the nature of reality, unceasing emptiness,
Act just as it is, without obstructing variety!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then the assembly of tathāgatas without essential nature proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For one utterly empty, cut off in annihilation,
Things will bring no benefit.
From emptiness that is continuous awakening,
All you tathāgatas, act as nonexistence!
If even the empty, in the manner of emptiness,
Can come to abide in nothing at all,
What need is there to speak of turning back the extreme of utter extinction
By the power of a yoga arisen from nonexistence?
To turn back utter annihilation,
And since things there arise from nonexistence,
Within emptiness that possesses continuity
Expound the greatness of continuity!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then the assembly of the unchanging essential nature of the tathāgatas proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For one utterly fixed in permanent eternalism,
Nonexistence will bring no benefit.
From that very essence of awakening,
Make everything into the great self!
If even the permanent, in the manner of the unchanging,
Can make things stable and firm,
What need is there to speak of turning back eternalism
By the power of spontaneous presence as the great self?
To turn back clinging to a self,
And to divert the channel of grasping at things,
From the nature of reality, permanent selflessness,
Act as the very nature that is without agency!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then the assembly of tathāgatas of the vajra lineage and their hosts proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For the utterly malicious and fierce,
Peace will bring no benefit.
From the essence of wisdom and means,
All you tathāgatas, act as wrathful ones!
If even the wrathful, in the manner of wrathful ones,
Can terrify the three worlds,
What need is there to speak of the three realms without exception
Before the wrath of the buddhas' compassion?
To tame all the hard to tame without exception,
And to purify those hostile to the commitments,
From the nine dances of the great Blazing One,
Act as the mudrā of the charnel-ground dance!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then the assembly of tathāgatas called Vajragarbha, Certainty of Suchness, proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For one utterly attached to what is wrong,
Truth will bring no benefit.
From the hidden intent of the great secret union,
Make everything entry in accord!
If even conduct, in the manner of enacted conduct,
Can make all defy the command,
What need is there to speak of the sage on the wrong path
Before the enacted conduct of compassion that enters in accord?
To free things by means of things,
And to cure the disease of poison by poison,
Act as the mudrā of the dance of deeds,
Of supreme means beyond all measure!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then the assembly of tathāgatas of the jewel lineage and their hosts proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For the utterly miserly and greedy,
Giving will bring no benefit.
From the source of all, inexhaustible even in want,
Make everything an inexhaustible jewel!
If even ascetics, restrained in the manner of austerity,
Can sustain a single life,
What need is there to speak of the torment of the three realms
Before that which yields every wish amid want?
To satisfy the miserly,
And to dispel all torment,
From the enjoyment of the secret Great Vehicle,
Act as the mudrā of the sky treasury!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then the assembly of tathāgatas of the youthful lineage and their hosts proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For the utterly lazy and distracted,
Urging will not bring diligence.
From the nature of reality, with nothing to do or to entrust,
Make everything free of action!
If even one who abides in the manner of the unmoving
Cuts off the path of holy action,
What need is there to speak of the distracted world
Before great unmoving bliss, free of activity?
To cut off the paths and continuums of the unfavorable states,
And to conquer the enemy of laziness and distraction,
From the great yoga of equanimity,
Act as the mudrā of abiding without action!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then the assembly of tathāgatas of the elephant lineage and their hosts proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For the utterly deluded and bewildered,
Teaching will not bring understanding.
From the sameness of all, without any distinction,
Make everything great delusion!
If even the bewildered, in the manner of delusion,
Can make everything dark,
What need is there to speak of bewildering the three realms
By that which does not discriminate, with nothing to accept or reject?
To destroy the sense sources of darkness,
And to turn bewilderment into space,
From the wisdom that is delusion purified,
Act as the unmixed and fully complete mudrā!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then the assembly of tathāgatas of the lotus lineage and their hosts proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For the utterly attached and clinging,
Antidotes will bring no benefit.
From the wisdom of great and supreme passion,
Make everything great passion!
If even one who practices in the manner of attachment
Can turn the three realms to blood,
What need is there to speak of the clinging attachment of the three realms
Before passion practiced as desire purified?
To dry up the lake of blood of desire,
And to cut off passion through passion,
From the wisdom of desire purified,
Act as the mudrā of the dance of desire!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

Then all the tathāgatas proclaimed the sūtra of the decree:

"For one utterly attached to things,
Prohibition will bring no benefit.
From the essence of wisdom and means,
Practice everything as entry in accord!
If even the accordant, in the manner of entry in accord,
Can pervert the three realms,
What need is there to speak of the perverted minds of the world
Before the enacted conduct of compassion's dance?
These, and others beyond number,
All worlds, the endless worlds,
Are drawn in by various means of taming
And joined to the genuine secret.
For the wrathful, act with love and in a wrathful manner;
For the peaceful, peace and the conduct of rousing;
For the violent, fierceness itself;
For the lustful, act with lust and with its antidote;
For the attached, attachment and renunciation;
For the distracted, gathering the mind and distraction itself;
For the deluded, pervasive sameness;
For the mountain, the very mountain of samādhi;
For the jealous, the manner of jealousy:
These, and others beyond number.
To the boundless limits of the expanse of space
Of the all-ground, the equal and the unequal,
For embodied beings who wish for distinctions,
The hero within the expanse of all, the all-ground,
Vajragarbha, fully abides.
The expanse of all sentient beings and buddhas
Is Vajrasattva, suchness.
When it is ascertained that this is so from the very beginning,
For sentient beings without limit,
Just as they wish for what delights them,
Unobstructed, uncontrived, just as it is,
Through the cloud banks of dance-methods of enacted conduct,
Supreme means beyond all measure,
That have become certainty itself,
However one acts, the hero within the expanse
Is unmoved from that very nature and unstained.
From the essence ascertained as that very nature,
Through endless and measureless doors,
Without obstructing anything,
Leading beings to bliss in whatever delights them,
Act as the certainty of the holy ones!
Here, with diligent violence,
From the inexhaustible blazing wisdom,
Act as the mudrā of the great and supreme Blazing One
Who utterly terrifies the lord of the charnel ground!"

Thus they decided, definitively and sacredly.

This is the twenty-fifth chapter, called the Sacred Decision of the Deliberation, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Lord of Laṅkā, at that time I, who had come together in that very assembly, knowingly, in order to bring about full awakening, questioned a bodhisattva also seated in that assembly, called Armor of Exhortation, Intelligence Endowed with Means: "O great being, from what cause did this great preta arise? From what condition did he arise? Into what result does he ripen? To what family does he belong? What conduct of his karmic lot does he engage in? If he is not tamed, what harm will there be? If he is tamed, what need will it serve?"

When I had asked this, that great Armor of Exhortation said: "Great being, Lord of Secrets, this great preta did not arise from any good causes and conditions; he arose from causes and conditions of wrongdoing alone. His cause arose from the mountain of darkness. His condition arose from fierce austerities that perverted the command. His result ripens into a mountain of suffering. As for his family, he belongs to the commoners, to saṃsāra, to the caṇḍālas, to the bhūtas and the pretas. As for the conduct of his karmic lot, he indulges unrestrained in the four things of saṃsāra. If he is not tamed, the fault will be that the essence of awakening of embodied beings is long eclipsed. If he is tamed, the maṇḍala of light, the essence of all, will dawn manifestly and near at hand."

When he had said this, I spoke these words to him: "How wonderful, great being! Then this time is the time that has come. This activity is the supreme of activities. This secret is the sacred secret of secrets. Tame him, tame him utterly, everywhere!

At this great occasion when the time has come,
For this being whose karma has fallen due,
To transform perception through compassion that has come to the destitute
Is the supreme of compassion.
Teaching the whole trichiliocosm without exception
Does not match even a hundred-thousandth part of it."

Thus I exhorted him.

Then all the tathāgatas fully commanded the holy being whose negative tendencies had been transformed, appearing as Vajradhara:

"Holder of the secret treasury of all the victors,
Lord of mind, Vajradhara,
You of great and fierce discipline,
Hero victorious over the three worlds,
By the power of your great armor of old,
Buddha of all the buddhas,
Who tames the fierce ones hard to tame,
Māra of māras, who conquers the māras,
Being with a blazing blue body,
Bearing a terrifying, heroic guise,
With your fierce, ferocious and turbulent essence
Utterly vanquish the lord of the charnel ground!"

Thus fully exhorted, that being Vajradhara knew that

This world with its gods
Had been brought under the power of the king of bhūtas;
By that extremely fierce, ferocious and turbulent one
The world of gods and humans was all but destroyed.
Faces and complexions were no longer clear;
All had grown dim and murky,
And untimely darkness too
Had turned to pitch-black gloom and covered all.
Heat and cold, disease and fever,
And every kind of misery spread everywhere;
Gales, thunder and hail,
And rains of pus and blood, of stones and of weapons,
Came everywhere out of season.
Even the livelihood of lowly beings had gone wrong.
It was a protectorless world,
Utterly exhausted and tormented
By malicious beings of every kind.

Knowing this and tormented by compassion, with utterly ferocious discipline he spoke these words in vajra speech to Vajradharma's Horse-Face:

"Vāgīśvara, Vajra Face,
