Endowed with the supreme essence, the meaning of primordial wisdom,
Scripture of the yoga of the Great Vehicle, gathering all vajras:
We pray you, explain it extensively to this assembled gathering!

"Aho!" they said, and bowing down with certainty they made supplication.

Then, when the Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, had been supplicated with that song of Vajra Play Suchness, he gazed on the whole retinue and smiled. From the expanse of his utterly pure mind, suns of awareness, beyond telling, beyond even what is beyond telling, pervaded everywhere and in every way with boundless light rays. From each of those light rays arose various forms, such as vajra yakṣas and vajra rākṣasas. From each of these, in turn, immeasurable hundreds of thousands of buddhas, each with their own retinues gathered around them, appeared, pervading infinite buddha-fields. From that, the light of the sun of awareness arose, pervading the realm of space, and every darkness, every deep and dense blackness, was completely dispelled. There arose the thunder of the Dharma of awareness, the rain of Dharma, various songs of Dharma, various dances of Dharma and various offerings of Dharma, and in boundless space they appeared vividly and unmistakably, pervading every direction, this way and that, above and below, interwoven, and so on. The inconceivable light rays of the wondrous body blazed, radiated and churned, vivid and distinct. The wondrous mind, too, was vast and clear. The wondrous, inconceivable speech also proclaimed, "Aho!" The immeasurable buddhas, too, with the single sound of inconceivable speech, proclaimed, "How wonderful! The meaning of the wondrous Dharma of play has come forth!" Wonder was born in the hundreds of thousands of the retinue and the various beings to be tamed, filling them with delight. Their skill in awareness was trained, the greatness of the meaning was brought out, and they were struck with awe and amazement. All of them remained close by. They remained at peace. They remained filled with joy.

Then that Lord of the Supreme Secret entered into equipoise in the meditative absorption called Vajra Stream, which purifies the retinue, and from the long hūṃ of his nostrils there came forth these beings possessed of manifest vajra might:

hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ Those of the lineage of the sacred Great Vehicle,
Gathered in the vajra gathering,
Are to be placed in vajra secret rows,
According to their order, higher and lower.
Those not of the lineage become lineage-less.
The sacred commitments of those of the lineage
Do not descend upon the lineage-less.
Whoever transgresses this will be destroyed.

hūṃ krodha jvala samaya rakṣaśka jvala samaya yakṣa jvala samaya citta jvala samaya

When this sound was sent forth, from the mass of light of his tongue faculty there came, in a whirling succession, blazing garlands of light of the wrathful ones, radiating garlands of light of the rākṣasas, churning garlands of light of the yakṣas, and garlands of light of the devourers. Thereby the rudras, the māras, the rākṣasas, the yakṣas, Entering Craving, Craving-Free, Īśvara, Balin, monkeys, goats, lu-de creatures, bees, crows, fire-bearers, saṃsāra, water, earth, space, fire, sound, conceptual analysis and the rest no longer remained as signs, and were pacified into the nature of reality.

Then, as those light rays swirled in the sky, that great retinue was terrified and made this supplication: "How wonderful! Alas! Blessed One, we pray you, bless us so that we become fit vessels! We pray you, accept us into the lineage of the Great Vehicle!" When they had so supplicated, the Blessed One let the radiance of his face shine forth. Those light rays became the light of primordial wisdom, peaceful and swirling, beautiful and blissful, delighting everyone; and having purified the vessels, they dissolved back into the source of the light.

Lord of Laṅkā, then that Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, arose decisively from the expanse of great nonabiding primordial wisdom, and with a sound like the roar of a victorious lion, there arose this sound of the great treatise: the array of meaning, the play of signs, the certainty of the secret, the commentary on the word, the mirror of Dharma, the source of reasoning, the Gathering of All Intentions, the vajra expanse. Having said, "oṃ hūṃ mūṃ," he said nothing at all. He did not speak at length. He did not deny what had been spoken. He did not negate. In the very state of not having spoken, he remained as the essence of extensive explanation.

Lord of Laṅkā, these are called the branch of the root application.

From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, the forty-eighth chapter, which teaches the branch of the root application.

Lord of Laṅkā, then what is the branch of connection? It is that to which each thing is connected. It is to be stated purposefully, as going purposefully, thus.

Lord of Laṅkā, at that time, on that occasion, I had become the great samaya, Vajrasattva. The Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, intending that I myself should purposefully proclaim at length the secret meaning of the Great Vehicle, looked at me for a moment with his face inclined toward me, and urged me on with a smile. Then I, who appeared as the one called Vajrasattva of the Great Samaya, realizing that I had been truly urged, rose from my seat, touched my own marks at the five places, and, in order to proclaim at length the suchness of the Great Vehicle, placed my tongue upon a lotus. With great reverence I made a prostration in the yogic manner, bringing the four limbs of the body to the ground, gazed at the face of the Teacher with the gaze of a lion, and spoke these words:

Vajra King, Lord of the Assembly, please consider me!
King of Dharma, unmoving, you act in the way of the victors.
Supreme Victor, abiding firmly on the ground of great bliss,
With the blazing vajra tongue, supreme in the act of speech,
May the kings of sound and melody bless me!
This king of secret mantra, this mirror of Dharma,
By the power of the Supreme Victor, the King of Dharma himself,
It is fitting to proclaim at length to the assembled gathering.

"Aho!" So I made supplication. Then, in order to bless me, whom he had charged with the command of vajra activity, as a great vajra proclaimer, the Lord of the Supreme Secret entered into equipoise in the meditative absorption called Vajra Radiance, and from the light rays of his tongue came vajra karma satva cakra ha ha haṃ. A blazing wheel of crossed vajras, the being who is vajra alone, fell into my hand. I gained mastery over the vajra of activity, was blessed with the eloquence of a great expounder, utterly unconstrained, and became one of great conscientiousness, never careless.

Then I, Vajrasattva, took that emblem of activity in my hand and, with various vajra words, turned it five times, that is, in twelve ways, and changed it into two and five. Thereby the buddha-fields shook to their utterly pure limits: they shook, shook strongly, shook strongly everywhere; they moved, moved strongly, moved strongly everywhere; they rocked, rocked strongly, rocked strongly everywhere; they churned, churned strongly, churned strongly everywhere; they roared, roared strongly, roared strongly everywhere; they quaked, quaked strongly, quaked strongly everywhere. When the edges rose, the center sank; when the center rose, the edges sank; when the east rose, the west sank; when the west rose, the east sank; when the south rose, the north sank; when the north rose, the south sank. All the buddha-fields became as though they were nothing but me, Vajrasattva, and resounded with the sound that proclaims at length the essential nature of secret mantra in an unerring way. I, Vajrasattva himself, in a terrifying manner, remained in a majestic posture on a throne marked with lions and vajras, in front of the Teacher.

Then the great assembled gathering and retinue spoke thus to the Blessed One: "How wonderful! Blessed One, this Vajrasattva, so mighty, so greatly blessed, of such great cause, so learned, so clear and profound, is inseparable from the mind of the Blessed One and born from that mind; he is connected with suchness and born from suchness." So they said, praising me. Lord of Laṅkā, the Blessed One then declared to them: "Great assembled gathering, this Vajrasattva is the very essence of the awakening mind, the lord of all compassion, the supreme of all skillful means, the mighty sovereign of all buddhas, the father of all bodhisattvas, the yoga of all yogas, the guide of all nirvāṇas, the great māra even of all māras, the tamer of those hard to tame even among all buddhas, the treasury of all Dharma, the lord of all secrets, the great source of all primordial wisdom. Why is this so? In the time of equality without beginning or end, in boundless time, immeasurable and inconceivable, beyond even the furthest limit of the furthest limit, he did what was to be done, performed the act, and became manifestly and perfectly awakened. Because he is joined to the intrinsic nature of all phenomena and connected with the intrinsic nature of all phenomena, he is born from the mind of all the victors and connected with that mind, and has become the father of all. Such is he." So he declared, speaking in praise of me.

At this the whole assembled gathering and retinue was amazed, and they said, "Blessed One, this is truly wondrous! Sugata, this is truly wondrous!" Then they bowed separately to the sacred Vajrasattva, made prostration in the yogic manner, bringing the four limbs of the body to the ground, offered wondrous flowers and various offerings of meditative absorption, and said:

You who are so marvelous and wondrous,
Born a victor from the mind of the buddhas,
How you appear here in this buddha-field!
Father of yogis, heir of the victors,
Turning the wheel of the word
That is one in connection with all intrinsic natures,
Make clear the sacred, utterly pure, supreme path,
Illustrating it through words!

"Aho!" So they made supplication.

Lord of Laṅkā, then I, the sacred Vajrasattva, turned and twirled the vajra, and spoke majestically in vajra words:

oṃ hūṃ muṃ a ho
The heir of the victors says nothing at all.
Because he does not speak, he explains at length.
This nature of reality, which cannot be explained,
The victors have not explained.
Thus even the Teacher, the Supreme Victor,
Does not wish to give an exposition;
His intention is to explain at length.
The nature of reality, which is unborn,
Because it is unborn, is explained as the essence.
That meaning which is the essence
Is not something that can be spoken in words.
In elaboration itself one goes astray:
So the victors have rightly explained.
Therefore the Vajra King himself
Intends what he has not spoken.
Whatever brings out the meanings of the intention,
Like a reflection in a mirror,
Appears to awareness as the meaning of the intention.
Though, by the meaning of the unborn essence,
It is not elaborated in words,
Yogis of signs and words,
Subtle in words but coarse in meaning,
Those weak in skill whose minds cling to words,
In order to satisfy their minds by guiding them,
Must have it illustrated by words of miracle,
Words that from the unobservable make it observable.
The mind of all the sugatas and victors,
That very secret, the meaning of the essence,
Equality, awakening, the Great Vehicle,
Clearly pervading and appearing in all,
Must be seen just as it is by all.
I am the essence of all the victors
Who abide in the three times;
In this meaning hidden in emptiness,
With the key illustrated by symbol and example,
I open the door to the awareness of equality.
The lineage that illustrates and brings forth the hidden meaning
Is said to be taught to those who have not seen.
Seeing it, it accords with suchness itself.

Thus the Lord of Secrets set out the rite in which all phenomena are gathered into the four branches of purpose and connection. Then the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, beings of inferior bodies, also came to comprehend all phenomena completely by way of the four branches of purpose and connection.

From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, the forty-ninth chapter, which teaches the branch of connection and purpose.

Then again the Lord of Secrets called to the Lord of Laṅkā and said: "Lord of Laṅkā, what then is the branch of guiding? It is guiding fully from what to what. It should be discussed thus, discussed in full detail. Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this:

The branches of full guiding
Are guided by a set of ten,
And so the branches of the words of utterance and expression are complete:
The words of looking to what, of opening the door,
Of gathering, of leading to accomplishment,
Of condition, and of holding another object in focus,
The unfabricated, the mirror, and the interweavings.
By applying them in harmony with the ten mirrors,
Words and meaning are joined completely.

Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this:
As for looking to what, and looking:
One who travels here through the sky
Divides the arc of the sky into stretches by his skill;
Likewise one who goes here into that very thing
Divides the unmixed range of view into stretches.
The stages of ground to be traversed
Are divided to the measure of one's strength;
Likewise the junctures of meaning and of words
Are divided into sections and groups of meaning.

Just as under someone's dominion
Many lands are contained,
So all the many enumerations of expression
Are gathered into the single essence of something.

Those that go in this way or that
Appear directly connected, following one upon another.
The junctures of words of expression and expressed
Are here joined in connection without confusion.

One traveling to the place one is going
Goes by momentum, with no visible movement;
By uttering the letters and by one's own effort,
They are joined in accordance with the essence of something.

Whatever goes in whatever way,
What is unfitting appears fitting there:
Words that are coarse, harsh and faulty,
What is unfitting, are joined there fittingly.

Foods that are imagined in the mind
Draw out from within the nourishment one desires.
By holding something as the object of focus,
What is unclear is shown manifestly.

Just as when an echo arises in succession,
At the moment it arises one gains conviction,
Likewise when the object of valid cognition arises,
One is certain that it is the unfabricated itself.

Just as here a pure mirror
Shows the face and its reflection,
So by the mirror of close illustration
What is unseen is made clear.

As in interweaving, scattering and churning,
The junctures of twisting, of casting and gathering, and of scattering are joined,
Like the great power of the play of great skill,
So that very thing is joined as awareness itself.

Lord of Laṅkā, these
You should know to be only the branches
That guide into utterance and expression."

When the Lord of Secrets had spoken thus, the Lord of Laṅkā and the other beings of inferior bodies came to open fully the doors of speech concerning all phenomena.

From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, the fiftieth chapter, which discusses the branch of full guiding.

Then again the Lord of Secrets called decisively to the Great Being, the Lord of Laṅkā, and spoke in detail: "Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this:

The branch of certainty
Is what is expressed, by whom, to whom:
The four connections, explanation and application, the doors of expression,
Utterly pure and vividly clear.
To this, the Lord Avalokiteśvara
Sees all the beings without end
Here as only four vessels:
Those with clinging attachment, those with wrong craving,
Those with lineage, and those with intelligence.
All of them appear in four parts:
Desire, guidance, application and certainty appear.
These, at times, are four parts:
What is needed where, what is certain,
From the essence along with the object of focus,
In the four of scripture, meaning, logical reason and reasoning,
Joined as consistent with appearance, extensive and sound.
All the causes of nonexistence, imagined and clung to,
What is established and accomplished, appear according to how they are viewed.
Whoever, communicating to whomever through symbols,
Is certain in taking up the meaning of something."

When he had discussed this as the sacred certainty, the beings of inferior bodies found the certainty of all phenomena through mere symbols.

From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, the fifty-first chapter, which discusses the branch of sacred certainty.

Then again the Lord of Secrets, by the means of bringing the mind to full engagement, called to the Great Being of inferior karma, the Lord of Laṅkā, and said:

"Lord of Laṅkā, the very means
Of entering realization exhaustive of the branches is this:
Through the door from which intrinsic nature arises, the application of empowerment,
And the comprehension of certainty,
One should settle definitively upon the true meaning.
Knowing, entering and the result
Are the characteristics of the spontaneously present.
Its connection, its purpose, it must be illustrated,
And one must follow after it.
Hearing a little, familiarization, and becoming certain;
Opening, seeing, entering and attaining;
Seeking it, finding it, entering, making it manifest;
Approach, close approach, accomplishment and attainment;
Likewise becoming equal, so that for the yogi
It is spontaneously present and fit for activity.
This is the sequence of definite entry.
For the sake of others there are four stages of arising:
Moving, miracle, and causing to appear;
They are applied in sequence, just as they are.
Knowing that, knowing it as unborn,
At that time knowing absence of entity,
And knowing it primordially: such is the sequence.
All equality, suchness itself,
Accomplishment, not transgressing, and the substances in accord with conduct:
Intrinsic nature appears in five aspects.
Whatever is skilled, great, many, boundless and vast,
Transforming, of great waves, showing as anything at all,
Containing all, enjoying all, entering into bliss.
Destroying, pacifying, expanding the limits,
And gathering into completeness: these are the four activities.
By this branch of application,
All the branches are complete.

Lord of Laṅkā, these are the branches. You should know that by these branches every expression, in whatever way it is made, is fully distinguished."

When the Lord of Secrets had spoken thus, the beings of inferior bodies came to comprehend all phenomena completely.

From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, the fifty-second chapter, which explains the means within the branch of the means of entry.

Then again the Lord of Laṅkā asked the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, what is that which is called the full unraveling of the sūtra?" When he had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said: "Lord of Laṅkā, as for this sūtra, all that is expressed here, wherever and in whatever way, comes down to just these four sūtras. Lord of Laṅkā, what are the four? They are: release through hearing something; release through seeing something; arising as direct perception through something; and the absence of any release from anything into anything."

"Lord of Laṅkā, of these, release through hearing something is this:

The intrinsic nature of all is suchness.
In that very thing which one hears,
All is pervasive and vast, without entity.
Knowing that, one comprehends it as suchness.

Lord of Laṅkā, release through seeing something is this:

When one sees that just as it is,
By that seeing, appearance and existence are utterly pure.
There is no fear of anything at all.
In that suchness, all are buddhas.

Lord of Laṅkā, release as direct perception through something is this:

When one knows that emptiness,
There, all things, directly perceived,
Are definitively exhausted in emptiness.
That itself, directly perceived, is buddhahood.

Lord of Laṅkā, the absence of any release from anything into anything is this:

All things, in the expanse of suchness,
Are buddhas from the very beginning, without conceptual thought.
How could they become buddhas only now?
It is simply suchness itself.

Lord of Laṅkā, these are the sūtras. The infinite expressions, by whomever, to whomever, in whatever way they are made, come down to these four sūtras alone. They untie the knots of everything, and you should know that they are the complete liberation and the extensive commentary of all phenomena."

When the Lord of Secrets had spoken thus, the beings of inferior bodies came to comprehend all directions completely and clearly.

From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, the fifty-third chapter, the extensive commentary on the sūtra.

Then again the Lord of Laṅkā, the bodhisattva, the Great Being, put the root questions in full to the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, what is the root of everything? What is it that brings release? Lord of Secrets, please make it understood. Lion of speech, please make it fully understood." When he had spoken thus, the Lord of Secrets said: "Lord of Laṅkā, this question about the root of everything is excellent. It is most excellent indeed. It is like this: the root is the mirror, the discordant, and certainty.

"Lord of Laṅkā, the mirror is this: taking up an example that shares the qualities of space. Just as space is, so one views certainty about anything in the same way. Lord of Laṅkā, what then is space? It is this: it is of one intrinsic nature, unstained, making room, unborn, undestroyed, without shape, without color, unconditioned, without anything grasped, without grasping, without limit, without center, without increase, without decrease, without going, without coming, without moving, without stirring, without formation, without abiding, without destruction, without being empty, without change, without anything it does not pervade, without anything that does not arise, without anything it does not encompass, without weariness, without setting, without characteristics, without cause, without condition, without result, without effort, and without conditioning activity.

"Lord of Laṅkā, space does not turn from existence into nonexistence, nor from nonexistence into existence. Likewise, there is in it no turning from clear to unclear, from unclear to clear, from distinct to one, from one to distinct, from what is to what is not, from what is not to what is, from near to far, from far to near; likewise no turning into large and small, good and bad, happy and suffering, pervading and not pervading, arising and not arising, pure and impure, established and unestablished, high and low, wide and narrow, shape and shapelessness, color and colorlessness, outer and inner, birth and cessation, transferring and not transferring, changing and unchanging, darkness and light, former and later, edge and center, self and other; and likewise there is no turning from having taken up to having cast away, nor from having cast away to having taken up. Therefore space is unfabricated everywhere and in every way. This very thing should be applied likewise: therefore it is unfabricated.

"Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this. Space, for example, was made by no one. It has no maker. It did not arise from a cause. It was not produced by conditions. It was not born at the beginning. It will not be destroyed at the end. No beginning or end is observed in it. There is no attaining it and no not attaining it. There is no hope for it and no striving. Therefore space was made by no one, and because it is unmade, it has no remedy. Likewise this very thing too, because there is no maker, is unmade, and so it has no remedy.

"Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this:

Whoever seeks the limit of space
Will not find it by seeking.
Whoever knows that there is room,
That one has found space.
One who traces the mind to the very nature of mind
Does not find it, for it has no root.
Whoever knows it arises without ground or root,
That one has found the very nature of mind."

Mind seeks mind, yet mind does not find mind.
Who would search for the depth and root of mind?
Who would mark off the center and the edge of mind?
How could it be found by seeking?
How could mind point out mind?
Is mind said to have any substance?
By what could the substance of mind be made?
The awakening mind is no thing, yet emanates things.
What, who, is this great freedom from directions?
Being free of one direction, it is free of all directions.
It has passed beyond the domain of investigation.
If one realizes it as it is, that is suchness.

Mind seeking mind finds a certain warmth.
If one finds that warmth, it is the awakening mind.
It is anything at all, and whatever it is, it is fine.
That itself is the supreme meaning of the Dharma.
Therefore this nature of reality of saṃsāra
Does not exist as two;
The suchness of saṃsāra
Is taught in scripture to be the nature of reality.
The illusion of delusion in saṃsāra
And the illusion of utter purity and purification, these two,
Sentient beings and buddhas, though they are different,
Do not exist at all apart from suchness.

For example, from a single cloth as the material,
Transformed by the condition of dyes,
Though the colors are varied and different,
How could they exist apart from the cloth itself?
So these sentient beings and those with wisdom,
Ripened from false thought and from right thought,
From a single cause transformed by conditions,
Appear as two, awareness and ignorance.

The suffering of saṃsāra is like a dream;
As when, with a great breath, one wakes from a dream,
All is great bliss, the awakening mind,
Appearing as all-pervading and all-encompassing like space.
All that is imagined as coming from what is adventitious
Is conditioned; because it is unborn,
Suchness pervades and encompasses it all,
Great self-arisen wisdom, without effort.

The eighth section begins.

Lord of Laṅkā, of this very thing
There are seven roots that ascertain that it is so.
If one examines them and realizes it as it is,
That is the full ascertainment of it.
The continents and mountains, together with the encircling oceans,
Are seen clearly from the very summit of Meru.
Having found that, all the categories of vehicles,
The great suchness itself, are seen clearly.

The king of light in the world
Is the sun, majestic and bright.
Clearly illuminating all phenomena completely,
That itself is the great all-illuminating one.
Space, which gives great room,
As it makes room for the four great elements,
So by this very gathering of the secret
All the expressions of what is expressed are clearly explained.

Whoever obtains the secret of the word
Does not depend on any other person.
If one fully comprehends the secret essence,
One need not seek it in any speech or utterance.
The elements together with their continua
Arise and appear from the state of space.
Likewise all that arises as speech and utterance
Arises from that profound expanse itself.

All the categories of what is to be done for the aim
Are gathered into the gradual accomplishment of the result.
Though they are spoken of in great number and at length,
All of them are gathered into suchness.
Just as the clear heart of a mirror
Shows the reflection of a face,
So by that very mirror of the Dharma
The nature of everything is made clear.

Because the root of all is self-arisen,
Its nature is not contrived in any way.
Because the awareness that knows all never sets,
With the appearance of luminosity one sees in all directions.
Because everything is gathered into that itself,
It comes down to the essential nature of no thing.
Because all are one in suchness,
There is no convention of making them two.

Because the cause is primordially complete as the result,
It is free from both hope and fear: that is suchness.
Because there is no thing to be discarded,
How could there be anything to be taken up?
Already seen, already trained, already done,
Desire already fulfilled, conduct already practiced;
Likewise, since things are already accomplished,
It comes from the very finding of that.

The imagined appears when everything is void;
By being applied to everything, it is able to perform its function;
With doubt cut off, it appears in harmony.
In brief, it is just three;
In general, it comes down to just two;
Definitively, it is nothing but one.
Everything is gathered here, and arises from this.
If one knows this root of all,
Everything too is uncontrived, just as it is.
That suchness is the sūtra of all.

Everything appears as an illusion, without any basis.
Being without attachment, the mind is not disturbed.
In the state of space there is no intrinsic nature.
Because it cannot be examined, it is free from things.
There is nothing other than the essence itself,
Which shines forth as a stream of manifold things.
Appearing as a lamp, it dispels darkness.
It has arisen from the wish-fulfilling jewel itself.
Being without birth and cessation, it is free from fear.
Not fixed as anything at all, it is undaunted.
When one comprehends it, one is free from all effort and striving.
In the domain of understanding there is no journeying.

In each and every word of the great suchness
The intent of all the Victors is transmitted.
Though one cut the three roots elsewhere,
The secret meaning arises from itself alone.
Whoever seeks it somewhere else
Has entered a path that goes astray,
Obscured by the obscuration of the darkness of conceptual thought.
Whatever is not aware is what is obscured.
Conceptual thought itself is the obstructor;
Obstructors arise from that very thing.
For one in whom conceptual thought has not arisen,
How could there be what is called a māra?

If one asks from what the māras arise,
They arise from constant striving.
From non-striving itself, māras do not arise.
"Why not strive?" That itself is the māra.
Likewise faults, deviations and obscurations
Arise from seeing them as equal to good qualities.
If there is no seeing them as equal to good qualities,
How could there be faults, deviations and obscurations?
That itself is fault, deviation and obscuration.

If that itself has not become manifest,
Then whatever one keeps that has come from the secret meaning,
Like a sick man keeping medicine without knowing how to use it,
One wanders in bad states, with no sure means.

Objects, signs and things,
Memories, thoughts, and all that is heard and seen,
Just as they move in the mind,
So in that very instant they appear.
Not abiding permanently, momentary,
Never setting, unattached, is the mind of great bliss.
Since apart from the expanse of mind
There is nothing whatsoever called other,
How could there be any deviation or obscuration to bring about?
Who would pursue deviation and obscuration as a goal?
By whom, and how, could deviation and obscuration be found?
Deviation and obscuration too are that very expanse.
By seeking, that too comes to rest in that.
One seeks elsewhere, yet it arises from that.
By looking, what is seen is not realized;
It is just like a rainbow in a dark cave.

Through the distinction of seeing suchness,
Whichever of the four actual modes of conduct one engages in,
One is not bound; it becomes a means of liberation.
All of it, trained therein, is the supreme state of the path.
Those who pursue the meaning
And wish to cut off deviation and obscuration completely,
Yogin heroes who would eliminate the extremes,
Seize whatever side they are attached to,
And by holding to that view of center and extremes
They are firm in their certainty about ritual.
All of them are indeed yogins,
Yet they are not joined in union with the genuine meaning.
Their conduct may be joined in union,
But with the genuine meaning they are not joined at all.
They are attached to partial views,
But to the ultimate meaning they are not attached at all.
They taste the flavor of adventitious actions,
But they do not taste the flavor of the nature of reality.
They speak of a selflessness free of sides,
But they are not free of partial views.
Through ascetic discipline they may vanquish thoughts,
But the thoughts of apprehended and apprehender they do not vanquish.
Yogins who conceive of center and extremes
Seek the gold of the genuine Middle Way
By the lamp of verbal expression.
Seize this monkey of conceptual desire,
And tie up the thief, the cat.
Block every gold hole and skylight,
Tear down the piled clutter,
And if you open the king's treasury,
You become the owner of all that wealth complete.
One who seeks the path of the genuine Middle Way
Should abandon the five faults of conceptuality:
The mountain of coarse thought,
Signs, movement, sensation,
And the noticing of arising: these five kinds.
They are to be examined as follows.
Thought too is of two kinds,
Coarse and subtle.
Likewise signs are of two kinds, large and small;
Movement of two kinds, long and short;
Sensation, clear and unclear;
The noticing of arising, quick and slow.
These, obscuring the meaning,
Are great conceptions, great obstructors.
Why? Because the center itself is not seen.
By obscurations like darkness, overcast,
Thick gloom, banks of cloud,
And fog,
The center is not clear and is not seen.
These too are each of two kinds:
Darkness, and great pitch darkness,
As with utter gloom;
Complete overcast, and veiling;
Gloom, great and small, two kinds;
Thick great clouds, and wispy ones;
Fog, dense and patchy, two kinds.
The three lower states and the half-lit state,
The happy states of the four of desire,
The abodes of meditative concentration and the abodes of the four streams,
And falling into the state of śrāvaka, pratyekabuddha, or bodhisattva:
The pitch darkness that has become obscuration
Appears as the six distractions of signs:
Intrinsic nature, outer, inner, signs,
Attention, and negative tendencies.
These are held to be very clear.
Where the view of the Middle Way itself is vast,
The faults that obscure the path of the Middle Way
Arise in five kinds that obscure:
Through the force of the noticing of arising, of sinking and dullness, of grasping attachment,
Of rigid permanence and slack diffuseness,
There are the precipice, the gloom, cessation,
And nothing but perpetual nonexistence.
"These are faults of the Middle Way":
So the heroic yoga of the Middle Way holds.
Seeking the certain path of the Middle Way,
Keep to solitude continuously in a hermitage,
And with a mind that measures by the two kinds of valid cognition,
Cut off the trail of conceptual thought.
From what did earlier thoughts of existence and nonexistence arise?
On examination, thought arises from marks.
The mountain of thought is pacified,
And thereby one does not go to the three lower states.
If one asks from what thought arises,
It arises from signs themselves.
If signs are fully known as illusion,
The phenomena of signs are pacified,
And thereby one is not in the last of the desire realm.
And how is it with signs?
They too arise upon what occurs.
If mindfulness knows them as faults and as peace,
It dispels the faults of movement,
And one is not in the second of the desire realm.
Movement too, whatever it is, is likewise;
It too arises in that same way.
If it is known in that way,
It dispels the faulty sensation,
And one is not in the third of the desire realm either.
If it is scrutinized there in that way,
Whatever it is, it is only the outer and that itself.
If that is fully known as a fault,
Where could there be cessation and birth?
That is not in the fourth of the desire realm.
And as for where that arises from,
It comes about from the phenomena of mind.
If mind itself is fully known as illusion,
The phenomena of mind are pacified.
The aggregates are like reflections,
And mind is held to be like a mirage.
Freed from both extremes of existence and nonexistence,
One who knows this is not in the fifth of the desire realm.
Because one does not view mind itself as an existent thing
Or as something called nonexistent,
One does not fall into wonderment.
Because one has not become sunk in dullness,
It does not become a fault of meditative concentration.
Because one does not follow after perceptions,
One does not come to the abodes of sentient beings.
Because one sees the origin as the all-ground itself,
One is not in the state of peace either.
Because one does not view peace as peace,
How could one be in the state of a pratyekabuddha?
Because it is not mere cognition either,
One is not a bodhisattva there either.
In the Middle Way of emptiness and equality,
Because there is no division into separate, distinct things,
One does not train through the sequence of the grounds.
The Middle Way itself is beyond partial views and extremes.
On that very absence of intrinsic nature,
Within mind itself, instant by instant,
One should be mindful, unwavering and continuous.
When one rests in equipoise on it in this way,
The experiences of the signs of progress arise as follows:
Warmth, signs of progress, miracles,
Higher perceptions, and wisdom arise.
Warmth is stirring and attainment;
The signs of progress are arising and stability;
Miracles are arising and completion;
And where all awareness is clear,
There all aims are fulfilled.
It is like darkness clearing,
Light dawning, and the sky,
Seeing everywhere, and everything being clear.
Therefore, through its distinction,
The darkness of attachment to anything is dispelled.
Just as the wind sweeps away the clouds,
By a yoga of a single instant
That realm of darkness is swept away.
Just as when a lamp dispels the darkness
That very place becomes illuminated,
So if one knows it in an instant,
The darkness of long-held attachment is dispelled.
Those who know it
Have opened the door to suchness itself,
And likewise, since they possess that eye,
Seeing and hearing are composed into four chapters of words.
Since they stand on that ground, look to that.
If one does not possess that eye,
Then with words without limit or end,
Whatever conventions one may state about it,
One cannot distinguish the focus of the certain and the uncertain.
Wild fools whose minds follow after words
Are not the ground or path of training.
Whatever hundreds of thousands of words they may speak,
This is not to be spoken to others.
If anyone speaks it,
That is false speech, a thief of the vajra word.

He falls into the vajra hell from which there is no release.
If deceitful pretenders and hypocrites,
Without having opened the strength of a mind of certainty,
Led along by words and letters,
Teach a path that is uncertain,
Then, more than taking the lives of all living creatures,
Heavier still is teaching an uncertain Dharma.
Therefore, in the equality of extremes and center,
Whoever enters that bliss all at once,
Whether the time of abiding in yoga is long or short,
Whether there is radiating out or not,
Whenever nothing is left outside equality,
If one rests there in it just so,
Suchness itself appears immediately.
If one does not stir from it,
One does not go to the city of appearances.
If one is not attached to it in that way,
How could one go to non-appearance?
Because suchness itself has become manifest,
They are not in the state of peace.
When the extremes of existence and nonexistence are cut,
That is the very yoga beyond extremes.
"Here is the supreme certainty":
Those who make such a claim
In that which is without hope or fear
Surely appear as deviation and obscuration itself.
Why? Because they distinguish center and extremes.
If there is a center, extremes too arise by their nature;
If there is no center, extremes too are naturally absent.
Within that very distinguishing of center and extremes
Is suchness itself, free of extremes and center.
Not abiding, not taking up, not distinguishing,
It is nothing whatsoever, without seeing or hearing.
Distinguishing nothing, free of anything to be done,
It is free of the conventions of appearance and non-appearance.
The inconceivable nature of reality:
Rest in it as unthought suchness.
All inconceivable phenomena
Are nothing other than unthought suchness.
The grounds, the liberations, and so forth,
The dharmakāya, awakening, the side of the white,
All of these, with respect to the inconceivable,
Have become great deviations and obscurations.
The austerity of the great one hard to surpass
Is just unfabricated suchness alone.
It is hard to surpass for countless victors.
Whatever grasping there is regarding the formless is a deviation.
This ground of the Great All-Pervading Expanse
Is primordially untraversed, naturally arrayed.
There is nothing whatever not encompassed by this ground.
Whoever here arranges grounds and traverses them deviates.
Likewise, in the primordially arising awareness of that,
Whoever wishes distinctions to arise in stages
Is simply deluded here,
And has surely parted from the self-arisen.
In what has no object of thought and no reference,
The yogin of Great Equanimity,
Although it is simply just as it is,
Secludes the body, contrives the mind, and enters the place of deviation.
In that great total liberation,
Although it is certain there is no bondage or liberation,
Whoever here is bound by austerity and conceptions
Deviates from great liberation itself.
From the very beginning it is simply unfabricated;
Whatever abides is just suchness itself.
How could there be a place of deviation in it?
Deviation and obscuration too are just suchness.

When this had been discussed, the Lord of Laṅkā and the other beings of inferior bodies were completely freed from whatever bonds they had, and their doubts of hope and striving were cut off.

This concludes the fifty-fourth chapter, the teaching of the mirror of all phenomena, from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then the Lord of Laṅkā, the bodhisattva, the Great Being, asked the Lord of Secrets fully about the question of the root: "O Lord of Secrets, what is that which is called the root of all phenomena? How should it be fully understood?"

When he had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said: "O Lord of Laṅkā, this excellent question is good; it is very, very good. That which is called the root of all phenomena: the root refers to bodhicitta. It should be fully understood as follows: it should be known as the nature of reality of the minds of all buddhas and the nature of reality of the minds of all sentient beings. It appears as the two, darkness and light.

"Lord of Laṅkā, it was like this. When the Blessed One, the Lord of the Supreme Secret, stayed for an instant in the secret abode called Endowed with Lotuses, the emanated womb of Uṣṇīṣa Universal Monarch, in the palace of the supreme secret, at that time, on that occasion, countless holy beings were cutting their doubts about the supreme secret of suchness, making their requests, hesitating to ask, and departing again with their doubts cut off. Having gathered in that assembly, I said this to the Blessed One: 'Blessed One, we have not seen and have not apprehended that root of all phenomena called the heart of awakening. We have not truly found it, and we have not known it with certainty. Since it is not reached by traversing, where is it? Where should it be sought? What is it? What is it called? Where is it lost? Blessed One, please teach it in this very instant of time. Sugata, please teach it.'

"When I had asked this, the Blessed One said: 'Great Being, that root of all phenomena called the heart of awakening is not seen because of obscuration, is not apprehended because it is nonexistent, is not found because it is profound, is not known because it is difficult, is not reached because it is swift, and is not encompassed because it is vast. It exists in the realm of darkness. It is to be sought in the three cities. It is the limbs, the sūtra, and the root. It refers to the essence, suchness. It is lost in the realm of darkness. Therefore, Great Being, in order to dispel this darkness, you should become skilled in its intrinsic nature. Likewise you should become skilled in its essence, its characteristics, its limbs, its duration for as long as it lasts, its actions, the synonyms of darkness, its examples and so forth, and the means of dispelling it. You should apply yourself diligently to this. It should be known as being of one part with those who belong to the realm of darkness.'

"When he had said this, I said: 'Blessed One, from what did this darkness arise? What is the intrinsic nature of this darkness? What is the essence of this darkness? What is the characteristic of this darkness? How many limbs does this darkness have? What is the root of this darkness? How long does this darkness remain? What does this darkness obscure? How many varieties of this darkness are there? With what is this darkness concordant? Where does this darkness gather thickly? By what is this darkness dispelled? Why is it called darkness? Why does it thicken? Where is the net gathered? Please explain this. Sugata, please explain it.'

"When I had requested this, the Blessed One said: 'Great Being, the root of all phenomena comprised by darkness and light should be known as gathered without basis and without root.'

The sūtra that teaches this is as follows:

The heart from which the awakening of the buddhas
Arises and is brought forth is this very thing.
Like the heart of a lotus in bloom,
Its root lies in the mud of saṃsāra.
The rootless root
From which all roots arise
Is the root of all phenomena.
These three realms are gathered in it.
This Dharma is profound and hard to see,
And it cannot even be realized;
Even a being who dwells on the final ground
Does not see it.
This nature of reality, empty because it is nonexistent,
Is not apprehended as an existent thing either.
Apprehension and non-apprehension: the path of words is cut off.
It is nonconceptual and unborn.
Even if the clear-sighted, for endless time,
Searched everywhere, pervading all without remainder,
A thing to be called "this"
Would not be found and would never come to be found.
Even if the learned, for endless time,
Examined it with utmost intensity in every way,
Nothing whatever here is seen.
Even if the swift, for endless time,
Ran until they were utterly exhausted,
Still they have gone nowhere
From this very expanse; there is no going.
On the trunk of the unborn Dharma
Hang branches of four kinds,
And it ripens into the heart of unsurpassed awakening.
That is the tree of awakening, the origin of all.
Engaging with objects is the desire realm;
Grasping without manifestation is the form realm;
Without appearance yet uninterrupted is the formless itself.
That which comes from intention becomes action;
Whatever becomes the object is engagement itself;
Whatever is engaged, those are cognitions.
Though that object has become the realm of darkness,
In the realm of darkness that very thing appears.
Appearance, and the light of appearance arising,
Is the very heart of the root of birth and arising.
Knowing sides, one does not know it:
It is that very absence of essential nature.
Just as gold is lost in a dark cave,
So in the darkness, the pitch darkness of saṃsāra,
This bodhicitta, which is like precious gold,
Comes to be lost.
Those who have become of the realm of darkness
Arise as the very perception of darkness.
Whatever those of the realm of darkness know,
Even down to the merest subtle part,
Whatever the small-minded and childish
Deliberately discuss and speak of,
Speak of this to all the intelligent,
And know it to be for the sake of the foolish.
This too appears fully as follows:
The limbs, the sūtra, and the root
Are opening, releasing, and bringing forth.
What are renowned as the six limbs,
The six of seeing, hearing, feeling, experiencing, touching, and knowing,
Are the limbs that cut off arising and engaging.

Lord of Laṅkā, if you ask what the four sūtras are, they are these: the sūtra of the ground, the sūtra of reference, the sūtra of appearance, and the sūtra of engagement. That too is as follows:

The four sūtras, of ground, of reference,
Of appearance, and of engagement, are explained according to scripture.

Lord of Laṅkā, of these, the sūtra of the ground is as follows:

The childish hold the ground to be like this:
Formless, clung to as a name,
The very realm of nonexistence, simply that.
It arises as the essence of all cognition;
The knowable itself appears as the object.
The cause that brings all things forth
Is primordially and naturally unborn,
Unfabricated, luminous of itself,
Uncompounded because it has no cause or condition.
Uninterrupted, toward every object
It arises together with cognition, yet unclear.
All-pervading, equanimous, insubstantial,
Nonconceptual, free of analysis.
In the very suchness of that nonconceptuality,
However many earlier and later moments there are,
Like a river with its continuous flow,
Is a continuum arising, with birth and cessation.
Not cut off into a single thing,
It does not appear as separate classes;
So how could there be any distinguishing of particulars,
Saying "this" and "this"?
Phenomena comprised by the two conventions
Are each the whole of everything:
Going to all, entering all, joining with all,
Aware of things in their variety, yet not knowing them as divided.
It is movement itself and arrangement,
Full experiencing, cutting off, and grasping of that.
For one endowed with the five, present before it,
It is equal toward all.
Whatever clings grasps that and lets nothing go.
Like space that gives room,
It gives room for all phenomena to abide and arise;
It is not generating and the rest, and it is taking up.
Conditions are not endowed with two;
In that very uncertainty alone
It abides in everything.
Conditions are whatever meets with it.
While phenomena are one at the root,
By the force of their continuum there are two kinds,
Known as the ground and the support,
In which the distinction of delusion and knowing appears.
In the ground, going everywhere, deluded about all,
There is no dwelling place for clinging to aspects.
The support is appearance joined with the ground,
And the changeless, utterly pure abode with nothing to be sought.
Each of these has two, cause and effect.
Whatever is each earlier one
Is the cause of the ripening of each later one.
Thus it arises in saṃsāra everywhere.
By the force of the continuum of the five supports,
There appear five yogas, five antidotes,
Five examples of ripening, likewise the examples of application,
Five occasions, five paths, likewise the grounds,
Five sets of five dwelling places, five times,
Five abodes, and five kinds of culmination.
Divided here, they are fifty-six;
Because they appear as complete, they are explained as five.
Because it has become the ground of all phenomena,
Through mistaken conditions it appears as saṃsāra.
If the meaning of the intrinsic nature just as it is
Is seen with certainty, that is nirvāṇa.
From the all-ground and whoever knows it
The heap of phenomena, the three existences, arise.
From its arising the root takes on variety.
It is known as the expanse, the source, and the cause,
Emptiness, and without characteristics,
Suchness, the unmistaken,
The unfabricated, that which is not other;
It is known as samādhi, as thought, as emptiness,
Insight, calm abiding, liberation,
The Middle Way free of extremes, without anything apprehended,
Without apprehender, without annihilation, without permanence,
Unborn, unceasing, without thought,
Without conceptuality, without signs,
Likewise without self and without other,
Without going and without coming,
Stainless, without coming together or parting,
Without aggregates, without elements, without sense sources,
Without objects, without karma, without cause and effect,
The unborn heart, without awakening.
Buddha, Dharma, and bodhicitta:
That is unborn and unceasing;
That is like space.

The expanse of phenomena itself is the dharmakāya;
It is known as nonconceptual wisdom.
Unarisen, from the very beginning it appears as intrinsic nature.
However long there is existence that takes up rebirth,
Whatever is cleared away, whatever arises and ceases,
Because the continuum of suffering is exhausted and burned away,
It definitely emerges through five occasions of change.
Formless and unseen, it is not existent.
If it existed, it would have become an object of seeing.
How could what has not become an object of seeing
Rightly be called existent?
The cause that arises without limit or end
Is insubstantial, the ground and support, essential nature itself;
Therefore distinct variety appears.
If this expanse did not exist, it would contradict everything.
If there were a single nonexistent thing,
Then grasping at sides too would be like that,
And one would never be liberated:
The knot of bondage would be there.
Whoever grasps at limits and views extremes
Does not see the limitless meaning.
Grasping at limits is like measuring height in the sky.
Liberation is taught to lie in limitlessness itself.
One who grasps at limits and wishes for liberation is not liberated.
Though sand is cultivated, it does not ripen into grain.
Though a crystal ball is transformed, it does not acquire depth.
Though a glass bead is transformed, it is not a jewel.
Though earth and stone are mixed, they cannot be made one.
In the ground of the lightning flash there is no darkness.
Vermilion dust and refined gold can never be one.
That a person should make offerings to himself is impossible.
The earlier and later currents of a river do not meet.
Where phenomena accord, their kinds are infinite;
Therefore it is praised as suchness.
If that itself did not exist, then in all things
How could there be any taking up of it?
If that itself did not exist, then once the continuum was blocked,
When, at some point, it rises again,
That from which it arose is from nothing.
If what is absent and that which is absent are not two,
From what would it rise, and what would it not become?
Likewise, when it comes to cease through something,
However many moments, it rises from there.
Therefore that itself is the ground of all,
Just like the body, and poison and medicine.
The five engagements are like dreams.
That being so, is this one,
Or is it something distinct?
Free of the essence of one and distinct,
Whether as shape or as color,
It is like turbid water and the water in milk.
So then, is that itself the stronger,
Or is this ground of it the stronger?
Unmoving and unchanging, yet moving and changing;
Unarisen, and likewise arising,
Just like the arising of space.
Beginning and end: no time is apprehended.
Unmistaken, unfabricated suchness,
The expanse where intrinsic nature appears everywhere,
Unarisen intrinsic nature, clear from the very beginning.
What is called the ground, or its continuum,
Is the potent root of the birth and arising
Of all streams of cognition, set as the ground,
That very cause from which variety arises.
Clouds that move in a clear sky,
From the time that even a tiny measure has arisen,
Are called mist, vapor, banks of cloud, and overcast;
Before the moment of their arising,
It is called the clear sky itself appearing.
Likewise, that itself is set as the ground;
Because they are alike, it appears there,
And the particular distinction of each is only that.
From that very movement of it
The hosts of engagements appear as its display.
Because the potent root increases,
In whatever and wherever, whatever is afflicted
Appears as signs, grasping, moving, stirring, agitating;
In whatever appears of itself, that is clearly aware.
Wherever it looks and whatever it grasps,
From that continuum this arises there.
Whatever feels, whatever knows, whatever knows that,
Is the very thing that is frightened, terrified, afraid.
A mirror, a burning glass, and the arising of fire, these three:
Whatever they meet, that arises accordingly.
A mirror, waves on a lake, and waves of earth, these three:
However long that continuum lasts, there are the noticings of arising.
Through that very cause there are five natures,
Like tongs that hold fire, that hold that, that hold a leaf.
Through that itself, in that continuum,
Whatever is clung to, it does the grasping of that.
White cloth and the like:
They gather under the power of whichever door.
That continuum depends on that itself;
It is just whatever has been imprinted.
Snow, grime, and the like:
Through "the like," they appear as those very things.
Whatever arises in that continuum,
Those too are just those very things.
From milk, water, and seed,
Because they are unlike, that is the sign.
From that continuum of that itself,
That too appears in that way.
Whatever those things are that abide,
What arises there is just that.
Quick ripening and timely ripening are not two times;
It is the increase and decrease of large and small seeds.
Just as clouds, haze, and darkness
Obscure a substance so that it is not seen,
So by the overcast darkness of its continuum
The very thing to be seen is not seen.
In the first, the second, and the later,
Clarity, cessation, and distinctness appear.
Because that itself goes to all,
The one takes on distinct variety.
From the display of that itself come the six engagements,
And re-engagement too is from that itself,
Just like a stream of rain or a mirage.
Through the power of an eye disease, with respect to objects,
They are not distinct; they are mere essence.
From its mingling as one with whatever,
The magical display distinguishes it and takes them up.
Arisen from that itself, it distinguishes them.
Though whatever joins here does not appear,
Through the power of something, distinctness appears.
From that, that arises; from that itself,
Likewise those arise.
A foul smell arises: whose smell is it?
Clinging to what is absent in something,
From that itself, unlike and not one,
That itself is other, and that continuum other.
That whose essence is one
Also changes according to place and occasion.
For one whose habitual tendencies are utterly pure,
Just as everything is clear through clarity,
Whose continuum is cleared, all-pervading and vast,
"That itself is the utterly pure Middle Way":
So those whose domain is darkness hold.
Darkness too arises in that itself.
The intrinsic nature and essence of darkness,
Its characteristics, limbs, and root,
Its abode, obscuration, and particular phenomena,
Where, by what, for what, and what:
These should be definitely distinguished.

Lord of Laṅkā, these are called the sūtra of the ground. The phenomena of darkness were taught with a view to those whose domain is darkness, who are swollen with karma.

Lord of Laṅkā, if you ask what the sūtra of reference is, it is this:

The form realm, grasping that, is a single abode.
Grasping that, that itself looks at that.
Grasping that, making it that, it has darkness.
Grasping sides, sideless, aspect and one;
Uncertain, going in general, not apprehending two;
Without variety, it grasps that as self.
Stainless, without grasping, with nothing else like it,
Its engagement without support, without distinction,
In the three times there is no grasping of it.
Through grasping that as that in that,
There is the realm of attachment to the two, form and name.

Lord of Laṅkā, if you ask what the sūtra of appearance is, it is this:

The desire realm, engaging that, following that, abiding:
It is the third. Those are
That itself arising, its support, and immediately after it:
The five objects of desire and the two phenomena.
All activities, and always one;
The three classes, and the meeting, holding the classes;
Engaging that is seeking, cutting, and bringing down.
Unceasing, wherever, the continuum pervades and engages.
In the three times and three occasions there is no engagement.

Variety itself is planted in that.
Its engagement, too, is of two kinds:
The five, and the common and the particular.

These are knowing objects, knowing characteristics, knowing time, knowing the continuum of moments, engaging in actions, and fully experiencing their results.

Six, two, five, three, twelve, and one:
All of these bring about engagement in that.

Lord of Laṅkā, it is like this:

The Lord of the Supreme Secret,
In the delight of the supreme secret,
With his intention explained this domain at length.
First, conceiving the seven conceptions:
Having signs, and being signs,
Seeking engagement by its own nature,
Discriminating, and afflicted,
And conceptuality other than these.
Second, the three proximate minds:
Of the right kind, not of the kind, and neither.
Third, the conditions that delude and that embolden:
Whatever arises here becomes the support of the faculties.
Of the consciousness that arises from a faculty,
Concordant with it, and concordant with its arising:
Weak, and unaccustomed,
Sharp, and beyond measure.
Fourth, wandering of the life force, agitation of the heart,
Imbalance, fear and fright,
Being struck in the vital points, and possession by another.
Fifth, the onset of illness, weakness,
Fatigue, excess, and gloom,
Fixation, habit, and change through other conditions.
Of five and two, the first is what arises outward:
Signs, and whatever here is cleared away,
Whatever, when released, has all its entanglements exhausted,
Destroyed through the condition of planetary influence,
And changing through the conditions of others.
Ceasing in a single moment and its clearing:
That too, engaging there, is just that.
The second, engaging in making two:
The preliminaries, thinking "This is it,"
The seventh, generating and applying effort,
Sets all arisings in motion.
The third, bringing about freedom from attachment:
Weary of the object and place of attachment,
Separated by others, and interrupted,
It is brought about by correct attention.
The fourth, degenerated through attachment,
Abiding firmly through deluded views:
Here, slight habituation,
Aspects, indications, and signs,
Because the mind that does that degenerates and so forth, there is change;
Taking it up correctly and engaging therein,
Being obscured, and being seized by others.
The fifth, cutting off the continuum of meaning:
Having the strength of clarity and of wrongness,
Finding companions concordant in enjoyments,
Those who go on without breaking their continuum,
Not cutting off or turning back continually:
The wrong continuum persists and the continuum of meaning is cut off.
Of five and three, the first is linking,
Endowed with the continuum of meaning that turns back from that.
The second is of linking to rebirth:
In the phenomena of death there are two,
Timely and untimely. Of these,
For some there is untimely death,
Where those conditions are exhausted, or through other conditions.
Then, though there is a cause for long life,
The conditions for remaining long are incomplete.
The karmic conditions of not avoiding danger,
Excess, and what is unsuitable,
Pressing on what has not been cleared,
Not expelling what has long accumulated,
Blocking the flow so that it ceases,
Not relying on the respective antidotes,
Not knowing what suits through lack of habit,
Going at the wrong time, and faults of conduct.
Then one is endowed with three perceptions:
Endowed with meaning, with wrongness, or with equanimity.
Whatever meaning one is endowed with, there one goes.
Having relied on it before and remembering it,
Even if it is made clear by others,
It arises coarsely until then.
When perception moves subtly,
Turning back from that and endowed with equanimity,
What was relied on before arises;
Others cannot dispel it,
And even when urged one cannot remember.
Whoever is endowed with that goes to a happy destiny,
And does not experience
Even unbearable, fierce feelings.
At that time, whatever is seen,
What was never seen anywhere is seen.
Just as, through the condition of sunlight,
Light gradually arises,
So from the realm of darkness it becomes light.
Forms appear like a dream,
And various delightful things are seen.
Knowing all of that,
Here one leaves from the lower part;
The warmth fades upward,
And whatever emerges at the crown is happy.
Where one goes wrongly,
It is just the reverse of the above.
Through unbearable, fierce feelings,
Extremely sharp, unbearable pains,
Moving, stirring, unpleasant sounds, and grahas,
When one sees the appearances of delusion,
Those who were loving and the like
Appear as executioners and other unwanted figures.
Seeing terrifying forms of every kind,
Dust arises, the hair stands on end,
The limbs flail and clutch at the sky,
Excrement and urine are released, and the eyes roll back.
From the realm of light one has come into darkness.
Knowing all of that, one arises and falls;
Whatever arises at the end is unhappy.
Equanimity: either it is not clear through that,
Or it has not been made clear by others;
Unclear in both ways, it is equanimity.
Whatever one is endowed with, wherever one goes,
It is certainly just as one has become habituated.
Inclined toward habit, that is powerful.
Whatever one is unaccustomed to, even if made clear,
Ceases through the force of non-attachment.
If one is equally habituated to both,
Then whatever is clear first,
Or what has been made clear by others,
Does not turn back, and the other does not engage.
Some equanimity changes;
Some does not change, and completion does not arise.
At the time when all transfer,
In the intervals before the occasion has come,
When the movement here is unclear,
Over a long time, through habituation,
Whatever one is attached to arises.
From that, through the power of that,
Knowing that it will cease to be here,
Aversion arises toward this very thing,
And one comes to engage with what is to come.
Overwhelmed by the fire of aversion,
The continuum of this abiding ceases,
And that attachment to what has not yet arisen,
The sevenfold intermediate state of the three existences,
Is the support for bringing about rebirth.
At that time the yogin
Likewise has great purpose in habituation to that.
At that time attachment does arise, but
Through the power of the meaning to which one has become habituated,
That attachment is overwhelmed,
Just as a person with skill
Overpowers those who have no strength.
In the yoga of the knowledge holders of awakening,
Whatever one is attached to does not arise.
The unbearable suffering of the vital points,
Except for the high and the low,
Occurs for all others,
Because there is no extreme danger or joy.

Only the two, great suffering and small:
Whatever ceases, ceases either all at once or by stages.
Having become habituated to that very thing, whatever has not ceased,
Being foremost, is clung to there and held dear.
The third of the three existences is the being of the intermediate seven:
Born miraculously, possessed of faculties, moving by the power of karma.
It is seen by those of like kind, and is not seen by others.
Great bliss, bliss, and suffering,
And so forth: wherever it is going to be born,
It takes on the shape of that, and it is unobstructed.
As for its body or its sphere of activity,
Those who have become stained
Are like black things in the black darkness of night;
All of them see all the others.
Those who are stainless
Are like white moonlight and white things;
These two are seen with the pure eye of their kind.
Its size is of the smallest measure:
An emanated form, the size of a five-year-old.
Until it has taken up a place of birth,
Its eye is like the eye of that state:
Unobstructed by anything, it sees those of equal fortune.
It is not like that for all.
The places of birth of those of equal fortune,
And their very abodes, it comes to see.
For those who are stained, the eye is clear downward;
Going head downward, they do not see others.
For the stainless it is clear upward; the others likewise:
In whatever direction each faces, that alone appears.
The rest are just as above.
The one with the core of the being of the intermediate seven,
Until it finds its birth,
If it is delayed at the longest, remains for seven days.
If it is endowed with the conditions, there is no fixed time:
It transfers after one, two, or three days.
If it is not endowed with them, then after that
It remains there again for up to seven days.
If it exists but is not endowed with them,
Up to the end of seven weeks,
At each seventh day it changes,
And it remains there until that is complete.
When its stay there is exhausted,
It takes up a stay elsewhere;
However long that holds sway,
Once the seven sevens are complete, from then on
How could it possibly fail to take birth?
In the interval between what sets and what appears,
Because it abides for a moment, it is the intermediate state.
Being of seven days and of the nature of setting,
It is also called the setting existence.
Because it abides for up to seven days,
It is called the being of the intermediate seven.
Because it follows after scents and feeds on scents,
It is also called the gandharva, the one who approaches.
Because it arises in dependence on that,
It is also called the one arisen from that.
Because it holds the shape and form of its birth,
Having the manner of the former and the later,
And because it goes before birth,
It is also called the one manifestly produced.
Because it always hovers close around food,
It is also called the one who hovers near.
Because it is the interval between the two,
It is called the existence that links.
Because it possesses form,
It has no place in the formless realm.
The existence abiding there,
Toward the existence that ceased before,
Through habituation to attachment, aversion, and clinging
Toward the three, that, what is not that, and what is regarded with equanimity,
Just as it formerly clung to whatever it clung to,
At that time it delights in that.
The places where good and bad deeds were done in the former life,
Whatever lies in their valleys, that itself appears.
Seeing these like a dream,
What it sees becomes manifest as objects.
At that time, as when seeing illusory forms,
Omens arise through which its future is shown.
For one who is not endowed with the conditions for happiness,
If it dwells there and sets there,
The omens of arising in a lower state are these:
Of insects, worms and the like,
It sees the kind in which it will arise.
Sounds of clamor and thickets of reeds,
Dense forests and the like,
Groves of shrubs and various smells,
Mansions, pools, and massing clouds:
Signs of entering within arise.
For one who is pure here,
The omens of seeing its kind are these:
Peaceful places and pleasing sounds,
Delightful satisfaction of the senses,
Mansions and celestial palaces,
And pleasure groves in its field of vision:
Signs of arising appear.
Universal monarchs and the like,
Pure through purity, it sees that kind.
The core abiding there,
Because it clings with attachment to desire,
Follows wherever a scent arises,
To the place where scents of its own and not its own arise.
Clinging to that, it becomes satisfied,
And at the union, aversion arises toward it.
Through this, the continuum of the intermediate seven is cut off.
While it clings, it is exhausted there,
And through that very thing it is nourished;
From the core where it was nourished,
Various kinds of embodied beings appear.
Those born from the condition of warmth and from moisture take on that kind;
In a realm without that, it does not arise.
It is not absent, but is like motes in space.
The objects with which it is connected,
However far they may be by measure,
Its unobstructed eye reaches what it sees and feels.
Because it goes by the power of that,
It arrives at the very moment of knowing.
For it there is no convention of far.
Why? Because it is all-pervading and immaterial,
Not the sphere of embodied beings.
Some, through the power of what they cling to,
See those of a kind that appears concordant
Wherever they find what is compatible.
Right there, roaming about in that place,
Thought and analysis take hold around it,
And through clinging, the nature of clinging rises up.
Through its power, deluded there,
It sees things in an inverted way,
And they appear mistakenly, like an illusion.
Wherever that person finds a compatible meeting,
There that appears clearly to it.
If it takes the side of staying,
Desire attached to arising comes about.
The reverse of that is likewise:
It wishes to change its abode and be parted from its place.
Arising is likewise:
Where it is not compatible, it sees that.
For it, likewise, by the power of that,
Apart from the mere signs of abiding and arising,
Nothing else appears.

It sees that, and having become caught up in it, Displeasure toward it arises. Through that its continuum ceases, And, just as whatever seizes whatever, Through clinging it takes hold of that existence. It is guarded by its own materiality. The being of the intermediate seven bound for a lower destiny, Because habituation to clinging to killing has piled up, When it dwells in whatever place it is, Killing and the like appear to it. As soon as it sees that, Recognizing it through its former habituation, It runs there thinking, "I must kill." Seeing the birthplace in the place of killing, Because it undertakes the deeds of killing and the like, It is caught up in them and aversion arises. Through that the continuum of the intermediate seven ceases, And taking on a lower body, it experiences the afflictions. Thus the being of the intermediate seven Of the various realms in which it will arise, In the place where it is to arise, Goes toward the objects it was habituated to as they appear. Because it is caught up in conduct of aversion toward them, By the power of aversion its continuum ceases. When that appearance has arisen, it takes up what had not arisen. For the four birthplaces there are four ways of entry: Entering, dwelling, and emerging with full awareness; Likewise entering and dwelling aware, but emerging unaware; Entering aware, but dwelling and emerging unaware; And entering, dwelling, and emerging all unaware. The one standing near, mingled with grasping, Takes birth when the three places are in accord. If, through the birthplace, the seed, and karma, There are not the three obstacles of faults, Everything ripens in the womb. Moreover, at the time when intense attachment Of the one source for the other arises, At the climax the essence that is held comes forth. Then likewise, from both of them, A single drop of essence each Comes forth without fail. That very thing mingles there, And it gathers as warmth settles; The all-encompassing one enters there. That which has no essence, Why does it grasp at an entity? It is just as ḍākinīs, by the power of desire, Hold to an image drawn with the juice of the earth: It holds to an image of attachment to what does not exist. By the power of that all-encompassing one, Without wavering, the substance ripens and is nourished. In whichever direction those stages of the embryo lie, Its very entry there is the support of the heart. Right after it abides, it is endowed with equanimity, And at that juncture it becomes two. In the phases when the six sense bases are desired, At the time when it is clear and easy, It has become well nourished. Likewise, at the time it is curled up, it is shaped, And it enters from the cord, and is nourished by that. Whatever five there are, it is produced by these elements; What it relies on as one is the elements themselves; That itself, having entered the support, is the core. In the last two the faculties and elements arise. Like the elements, it grows, becomes solid, gathers, ripens, and holds together, And the limbs separate and come forth. Through whatever causes, its complexion changes. If it is the abiding kind, like the side that accords, it is on the left; If the arising kind, wrinkled and still, facing the other way, on the right. While it abides, the time of abiding is eight, And as with the early embryonic stages, the limbs become firm. For that reason, then, the elements, The faculties, and the sexual organs mature. At that time of complete perfection, The wind of the weeks arises, Which brings about its affliction. Because the wind belonging to the elements arises, It becomes restless and moving, And it is turned around, according to the way existence arises. The time they remain there Is thirty-eight weeks; By that, all is completed. When its six roots and four are complete, It emerges without fail. The fifth: the four principles of arising, From which comes the very source of yoga; These are four sets of four, Renowned as the sixteen principles, In which all of birth and self are gathered. These are births brought about by karma. That all of them do all of this Is karma, slow and swift: So it is seen by those whose sphere is darkness. For those obscured, entangled by karma, With that intent it was thus taught in the sūtra. Lord of Laṅkā, these are to be known as the Sūtra of Appearance. Lord of Laṅkā, furthermore, what is the Sūtra of Entry? It is as follows: That which desires abides in three ways; It opens space, and one stream produces another stream; It divides into separate classes and exercises power; Endowed with that itself, it is just that; It is mere arising in the nature of whatever; It is not anything at all: that is the middle. Totally uncontrived, its nature simple, Three, three, two, and the three conditions, with time: That is the complete desire realm. Lord of Laṅkā, these are called the four sūtras; one should know that through them that is completely unravelled. Lord of Laṅkā, what then are the three roots? They are these: the root of entities, the root of expression, and the all-encompassing root. Lord of Laṅkā, of these, the root of entities is that from nothing whatsoever anything at all appears. The root of expression is that from nothing whatsoever it becomes anything at all. The all-encompassing root is that from nothing whatsoever there is awareness itself of anything at all. Lord of Laṅkā, these are the roots; through them everything is brought forth. Lord of Laṅkā, these are as follows: What the King of Dharma taught, Through mind, through mouth, and through answers to questions, the three, Called meaning, scripture, and tantra, Is renowned here as three. Those who have thoughts about this, Thinking, "How does the single speech of the King of Dharma Appear as three, called tantra, scripture, and the ultimate?" Whoever has such thoughts and asks this, He himself knows, and to make it clear, With that intent he taught the three distinctly. That is the context and what follows from the context. What abides in the mind of the Person, Not having come forth from the mouth, is the ultimate itself. When that is indicated through the transformation of speech, It is the scriptural word of the Person, the king. When answers to questions unfold in subtle detail, It is the tantra word of the Person, the king. Great in means, small in hardship, immeasurable in meaning, Easy to practice, hard to fathom, the profound instruction; That very certainty, just that, Uncorrupted and determined just as it is, is scripture. The tantras of action, with their bases of rules and narratives, And the tantras with the great conceptions, their branches and connections: They appear as three, yet come down to one. In the nature of the single indivisible mode, The expressive power and the manifold appearances Are one, entirely without aspects. Thus that supreme secret explained. For those who are entangled by karma, Whatever side of darkness is explained here, Don the armor and make it known. From the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, this is the fifty-fifth chapter, teaching the sphere of darkness of the root of all phenomena. Then the Lord of Laṅkā, the bodhisattva, the Great Being, again put a thorough question to the Lord of Secrets: "O Lord of Secrets, with regard to the root, what is the sphere of the totally pure appearance of what?" When he had asked this, the Lord of Secrets said, "Lord of Laṅkā, that question of yours about total purity is excellent! It is most excellent!"

Lord of Laṅkā, moreover it is like this.
