"If you ask why: Lord of Laṅkā, it is because it does not depart from that which it intends; because it is no one's domain, and everything arises from nonexistence; because its wisdom is fully arrayed; because it explains whatever is certain in this way as yoga; because here everything is drawn in; because the excellent ones have fully comprehended it; because it is arrayed without mixing anything; and because it definitively arose and arises in twelve circumstances.

"Lord of Laṅkā, if you ask what it means that the sūtra is gathered into ten: it is gathered into just ten points, as follows. One should grasp the basis. One should unravel the tantra. One should open the door. One should bring it to completion. One should gather everything together. One should cross over the pass. One should decide the ground. One should find the experience. One should keep the hardship small. One should make it a single sūtra.

"Lord of Laṅkā, know that the basis of all dharmas is samaya. The abiding of samaya is like this:
That there is nothing to guard and no guarding
Is the supreme samaya of dharmatā.
Since everything is itself supreme,
One should not go against anything at all.
Whoever abides in this supreme samaya
Is on the ground of a supreme vidyādhara.
Toward the objects that are to be guarded,
To be without deceit, pretense or guile is itself supreme.
In this profound, clear, vast and supreme teaching,
The moral disciplines proclaimed in this way
And all the many activities of the ritual manuals
Are all here guarded as samaya and completely pure.
This samaya is greatly marvelous;
Because it is not shared by all,
For all those of small aspiration
One should set the boundaries of what is to be guarded.

The utterly secret teachings of the Great Vehicle
Should be guarded, from those who are not fit vessels,
By the means of concealment
Outer, inner and secret.
Those vessels that are not a proper place for the teaching,
The varieties gathered into four kinds,
Are explained so that the excellent Subhūti of the future
May examine them and keep this secret.
This wish-fulfilling jewel of the great secret
Is not flawed or at fault;
It is for the benefit of those of small intellect
That it is kept utterly secret through compassionate means.
Worldly people, followers of the lesser vehicle,
Those of perverse craving, attached to wrong positions,
Faithless, dull-witted, distracted and torpid,
Who strive at nothing, are like darkness.
These are perverse, their samaya broken, turned away from this;
The rope of their awakening is cut at its depth;
The sprout of their mind is burnt at the root;
They lie face down in the swamp of the lower realms.
Though they speak of the secret meanings in words,
They do not know this hidden secret.
To crooked companions who fancy they know,
They speak above all of what ought to be kept secret.
On the four branches themselves
They hear the sounds, without the meaning, and do not practice.
Those who cannot keep what is entrusted to them
Leak away into saṃsāra without essence.
Though they speak in words of the three supreme treasuries
Of view, samaya and conduct,
They have not come to abide where clinging has no limit.
Tormented by guile, deceit, jealousy and avarice,
Fixed in their grasp of the dharma of non-action,
Yet without that very thing in themselves,
They call yogins inferior;
Their sacred bond is defeated and they sink into saṃsāra.
Feigning good conduct, making guesses, bringing out contemptuous words,
Smooth-tongued, double-tongued, twisting things around,
Deludedly cursing anyone, these oath-bound ones
Are fit only for the sake of arrogance in this one life.
Thieving, guileful, deceitful, cheating and enticing,
Clinging for material gain, delighting in words,
Conceited, eager to talk, hypocritical,
Speakers of falsehood, venomous, they fall in the end.
To such defeated and bad vessels,
The great secret, that very secret,
Being profound and its depth hard to fathom,
Even if taught, becomes a cause of defeat and downfall.
Therefore it is not to be taught.
If it were taught,
Whoever teaches, speaks, or listens,
Would be ruined in this life and the long term: both would burn.
Toward a yogin learned, lucid and meaningful,
Whoever, not knowing yoga,
Under the sway of arrogance or out of jealousy,
Gives rise to whatever kinds of thoughts,
For as many eons as their number
Will experience unbearable results.
If he would afterward attain happiness,
He must first pass through that many eons.
Even one learned, lucid and meaningful,
If he definitely thinks as above,
He too takes on a bad state,
And the learned cannot protect him from it.
If toward an excellent one who has come forth in the way of yoga
One stirs him up with words like weapons,
And speaks ill of him with exaggeration and denigration in song and talk,
One will fall into the vajra hell.
Even the incidental ripening
Will come about exactly as it is.
Yogins of inferior fortune,
Whose root is broken and whose nature is flawed,
Possessed of bad causes, anger and ferocity,
Carry out unbearable and malicious deeds.
Those of ignorant disposition,
Childish in nature and weak of mind,
With an extremely inferior view,
Look to pretenders.
Toward those endowed with the most excellent of the excellent,
Of loving manner, always abiding in patience,
Who likewise seek auspicious aims,
Such people have no aspiration.
While behaving with samaya like a monkey,
A buddha who attains the essence of awakening
Will never come forth;
I have never truly seen one come forth.
Toward a yogin who has become an heir of the victors,
Skilled in showing the path and removing faults,
Some, under the sway of arrogance,
Reject him and by such means bring him to ruin.
That is the bottom stone of saṃsāra;
Never will it rise to the surface of liberation.
Ignorant and deluded yogins
Do not follow
Those who go definitively to the truly excellent,
But follow whoever looks to the desire for material things,
And long for that;
They are travelers on a wrong path.
For the view of yoga and the gain of awakening
They give no thought even if the rope is cut;
For the desire for material things and the gain of guile and trickery,
However small, whatever they pursue, they stake their lives.
One with the nature of a supreme yogin
Is like a lamp inside a vase:
Though he possesses the meaning of the treasury of awareness,
Like the claws of a thieving beast,
He reveals the meaning of what is hidden within,
Yet here the fool sees nothing at all.
For example, it is just like this:
A man who carries treasure on his body
And a man who has become a fool,
Staying in one place, look alike, with no difference between the two.
They speak in praise of this secret,
But do not pursue the secret's meaning.
They speak in praise of samādhi,
But do not pursue the meaning of samādhi.
They speak in praise of the true,
But do not pursue the meaning of the true.
They speak in praise of samaya,
But do not pursue the meaning of samaya.
Just as some beings
Say, "A precious jewel is like this:
All desired qualities come from it,"
And extol its qualities at length;
And when other beings ask them,
"Do you have even a little
Of that which brings forth what is desired?"
They have none, and so reply,
"I have merely mentioned a few
Of the qualities that fulfill needs;
That thing itself I do not have";
Likewise, those who do not strive in yoga,
Being devoid of the dharmas in which one should train,
Though they speak in praise of the excellent,
Have nothing excellent in them.
Such enjoyments as these
Rarely meet with excellence itself.
Whoever, holding the dharma of yoga,
Regards those whose yoga has degenerated, who have perverse craving,
As voices that destroy the secret,
And, as if seeing their evil,
Censures them and binds the ground,
That person is an heir of the excellent.
For example, when someone
Transgresses the king's law,
One who does not let him go as he pleases but applies the rule,
Correcting by command what is hard to straighten,
Becomes renowned and known to all
As "excellent, pleasing to the king's mind."
If a poisonous tree grows in a garden,
As soon as the gardener recognizes the poison,
He tears it out by the root and destroys it.
Why? Because if the poison spread
Among the garden's food plants, people would die.

Those who have degenerated, those poisonous ones,
Should be firmly shunned by the yogin who keeps the discipline,
Whether in dwelling place or for a span of years.
Those who do not shun them but act as their protectors
Are samaya-demons of the secret.
In truth, any yogin who,
Toward the profound oaths and precepts,
Keeps an unwavering intention to guard them,
Even if, in the root and branch commitments,
He should break one without noticing,
Though there will be a ripening, his root is not broken.
Just as the elephant Enaya,
Though it dies and becomes a corpse,
Still serves a purpose through its bezoar,
So even if a yogin degenerates and becomes a corpse,
He becomes something supreme for others
And is able to accomplish their aims.
Even so, all such as these
Should not be seen even in dreams.
Why? Just as a little milk
Going bad spoils all the milk,
A single degenerate yogin
Makes all yogins unruly.
All those of perverse yoga
Who let saṃsāra win the battle
The yogin certain of the ultimate
Should all resolutely cast out.
If they are not cast out and behave perversely,
Saṃsāra will win the battle,
And the power of the blessing of truth will grow small.
Everyone will say, "Practicing virtue does little good,"
Will grow weary of the true aim,
And will incline toward perverse dharmas.
Because everyone engages in perverse actions,
All kinds of unpleasant things arise.
Therefore, whatever the punishment of the command
For both exaggerating and denigrating,
Those possessed of wisdom, by the nature of their view,
Never do any harm at all;
But the wicked and mighty ones
Who dwell in their retinue,
The moment they see the faults
Of yogins who practice perversely, cut them down with punishment,
And all fall headlong into the lower realms.
There is one who is supreme against harm to practice:
Unlike these, complete in all things,
Possessed of lineage and of meaning, of good conduct,
His mind attached to nothing, endowed with emptiness,
Having unlocked the bolt of the mind of delusion
With the key of wisdom and liberation,
Possessing the eye of the King of Dharma,
The excellent domain free of extremes and partisanship,
Knowing how to enter the unerring path,
Thoroughly tamed, like a great tree in its trunk,
Just as is taught in the scriptures of the oaths and precepts:
That excellent sage of austerity
Is the most excellent of the excellent,
And for him one should open the door of the secret.
Just as austerity is mastered by the sage,
So it is a wish-fulfilling jewel:
From it come delightful wishes and happiness;
It is the mother of all vidyādharas and victors.
Likewise, this excellent austerity,
By abiding in it, is the ground of support;
It is also the life force of all austerities;
It is the supreme wealth of all the excellent.
This excellent one is a refuge from fears,
The supreme guide on the path traveled by the excellent,
The boat that ferries across the river of the excellent;
Likewise it is the supreme siddhi.
It is the best of samaya companions;
It is the guide of those blind to samaya;
It is the army that conquers opposing factions;
It brings victory in the battle of saṃsāra.
Therefore it is the excellent proof.
In such a way that one's three doors,
Holding to the limit of the three purities,
Do not here spoil the four empowerments,
One holds the three doors together with non-harm:
These are the four key points of being wholly unimpaired.
Brought to completion, with the four kinds of seals and signs,
The key points are counted in mindfulness and dedicated.
The children of small aspiration,
Possessed of the continuity of discipline and bound by the precepts,
Should diligently guard
Everything down to the subtlest of the subtle.
Of the vajra body, speech and mind,
There is speech, conduct, the delightful,
Not abandoning, the knowable, accomplishment,
Nonexistence, openness, oneness,
And spontaneous presence: ten kinds.
From this view a hundred thousand arise.
It is the supreme wealth to be practiced
By every yoga of yoga.
To the child who practices after definitive examination
From the family lineage of excellent yoga,
This scripture is taught, definitive for the mind
Of the profound and supremely perfect bliss.
Its preliminary is samaya:
Before the profound and all the rest,
In order to guard the narrow and dangerous paths
Of the fierce vajra oaths,
If the general and particular samayas
Are made firm through the definitive meaning,
That is a great vessel of treasure.
Thus, having taken one's stand in the excellent vow,
On every occasion and at every time,
To be able to guard what must be guarded,
The samayas of disciplined conduct,
Comes first among all samayas.
Having abandoned all that does not serve the aim,
One should practice thus, in order to accord with all.
The one that cannot be crushed is the lion,
For it is the king of beasts of prey.
A casket is the king's treasury;
It is not fitting to throw poison into it.
The tender shoots of precious sprouts
Should not be cut, severing the stream of their growth.
Even ordinary melted snow water
The wise should not drink.
The anthers of the lotus should not be opened;
Unless by moonlight, it should not be opened.
Into an unexamined vessel full of holes
Medicine of amṛta poured will leak away.
Those with meaningless and artificial signs of conduct
Should not be kept or kept company with.
Precious eggs of white crystal
Should not be sunk into the mud of a swamp.
The milk of the white lioness
Should not be poured into a vessel not made of gold.
The precious wish-fulfilling jewel
Is not to be burned.
The great garuḍa's wings, spread in the sky,
Are not to be broken.
The fiery bolts of meteoric iron
Are not to be hammered.
The deeds performed in the play of tigers and lions
Should not be mimicked.
The foundation packed with great vajra rock,
How could it be torn down?
The fence of the vajra enclosure,
Which is to bind, is not to be torn apart.
The lamp that burns the firewood
Should not be shaken by fierce scattering winds.
The flowing streams of vajra words
Are not to be cut off at random.
The king's edicts and seals
Should not be erased carelessly.
The fortress packed with vajra diamond
Should not be overturned from its foundation.

The wish-fulfilling jewel on the crown
Should not be taken down from the top of the victory banner.
Distinguished by the means of yoga,
It is the samaya of those of skill,
The supreme wealth of the fortunate.
The supreme wealth of constant training
Is the meditative concentrations of vajra activity.
With the vajra armor and stake of great wrath,
The enemy that obstructs life,
The dense afflictions, should be destroyed.
What has become the basis of idleness and laziness,
The mountain of pride that boasts of mastery,
The essence that inflames the senses and weakens them,
And the conditions that make one swagger should also be restrained.
Apart from the secret mantra of the Great Vehicle,
Whatever signs there are, the words of the childish,
Should not be uttered even in part;
Everything should be done as that very nature alone.
Just as people who are to be put to death,
When the executioner is about to kill them,
Strive at the means of escape,
So one should strive diligently, slaying the enemy of laziness.
To remove the obstacles that obscure yoga,
Those bound by the knot of austerity and vows
Should abandon attachment to sensual pleasures and discord,
Being not one here with what is one,
Being two-faced, outer and inner, and given to harmful deeds,
Intoxication with desire, dullness, torpor and greed,
Faithlessness, broken roots, haughtiness and distraction:
All the wise should abandon these.
These are obstructors of yoga;
They interrupt yoga.
For one who has definitively entered yoga,
The māras that interrupt its continuity
Are great conceptual jealousy;
Indifference and laziness toward everything;
Possessions, busyness and distraction;
Harsh words, those sharp weapons;
And anger, ferocity, short temper and great turbulence:
These are the five māras of the practice of yoga.
They obscure the true aim;
With the antidote, the supreme mind of awakening,
Turn them back into the state of great peace.
The enemies who steal the wealth of yoga
Should be tamed by one skilled in means.
Whoever contrives the nature of yoga
Falls away from being a sage;
Through the notoriety of his misdeeds,
That is the enemy that makes them a laughingstock.
Through an inferior view and coarse, careless conduct,
They habitually behave against this;
By going against the command and engaging in malicious deeds,
That is the enemy of corruption, which does not serve the aim.
Transgressing the vajra austerity and samaya,
Not having become the expanse, they are corrupt through laxity;
Like a tree whose roots have rotted,
That is the enemy that, breaking, falls into the six.
As for applying oneself to the genuine itself,
If one has no definitive words about it,
The aims are not accomplished,
And it does not become a result: that is the enemy of deviation.
These enemies that steal the supreme, excellent wealth
From yogins who practice yoga,
The four enemies of fault, one should turn back
With intelligence skilled in means.
These fields that appear as the three existences
Are definitively pure fields;
Since the grounds are purified all at once,
The fields are the most excellent of all.
These gatherings of sensual pleasures as offerings
Are the supreme of great requisites;
They are the marvelous offerings
That please all the excellent wisdom minds.
These places of saṃsāra, these charnel grounds,
Are the places of great bliss:
The unerring, uncontrived dharmadhātu,
The ground of effortless spontaneous presence.
The five great poisons, the root of all,
Are the five great wisdoms themselves;
Since the all-knowing self-awareness is self-arisen,
The yogin possesses the eye of self-awareness.
Lord of Laṅkā, these
Are the supreme secret samayas.
In these words of instruction from the excellent,
Whoever is a definitive yogin,
Just as from seeds planted
In reliance on moist ground,
Only the excellent is fit to enter.
Through yearning, whatever its very nature,
Yearning, one enters the domain of the excellent.
Lord of Laṅkā, how is it?
If you ask what it is like:
On one occasion that I saw and heard,
When my excellent spiritual friend
Was teaching this path at length
To great assemblies,
Gathered in this retinue
Were goddesses, nāginīs, and women of low caste;
Since a retinue of six maidens had gathered,
The six fruits came to ripen.
Just as that which holds the life of the living,
Since it has become the basis of all dharmas,
Abiding in this samaya,
Through its ripening into unsurpassed awakening,
Is the excellent holder of the life of virtue.
If it should degenerate,
The places of rebirth for punishment, the eight great hells,
With unbearable and fierce suffering,
Turn like a wheel of weapons.
Lord of Laṅkā, if you ask
What degeneration from this is:
Great total degeneration,
Degeneration of the root, degeneration of the branches,
Likewise degeneration of recitation, and incidental degeneration: these five
Arise constantly in a continuous cycle.
The means by which they are to be restored
I shall explain as five kinds:
Action, substance, aspiration,
Samādhi, and suchness itself.
Action restores in five ways:
For the first, one hundred thirty thousand;
For degeneration of the root, fifty-three thousand;
For degeneration of the branches,
One should complete five thousand three hundred;
For degeneration of recitation, one hundred fifty-three;
For incidental degeneration,
Restore it with fifteen, one and three.
Substance is with the five kinds of precious things:
Whatever samaya of the families has been broken,
Toward whichever gurus of the five families
Whatever oath was destroyed, one makes amends with that wealth.
Aspiration is with faith and clear trust,
With a cry of intense longing,
Lamenting constantly without ceasing;
Even while one undergoes life in a great hell,
It becomes the supreme antidote:
That is excellent restoration.
The great means of samādhi is this:
Though fire is needed to burn the world,
No fire is needed to burn habitual tendencies:
Since the fire of the wisdom of awareness arises,
All the firewood of obscurations is burned.
Though wind is needed to scatter the world,
No wind is needed to blow away signs:
Since the wind of the wisdom of awareness arises,
All the dust of signs is swept away.
Though water is needed to wash the body,
No water is needed to wash the mind:
Since the water of the wisdom of awareness arises,
All the grime of habitual tendencies is cleansed.
The river of emptiness is the bathing pool;
From the sky of suchness
It is burned in the eon of blazing, radiating fire:
That is the most excellent drying.
As for suchness itself, its supreme means:
For any excellent yogin,
When the punishment for degeneration arises,
Likewise, though suchness itself is unmoved,
There is no protector or refuge other than it;
There is no convention of degenerated and not degenerated;
Restored or not restored is beyond the path of words.

"Lord of Laṅkā, these should be known as the deliberation on fully grasping the basis." When he had said this, the lesser ones, the Lord of Laṅkā and the others, came to enjoy fully the wealth of the excellent.

This is the sixty-sixth chapter, called "The Supreme Secret of Samaya," from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again the Lord of Secrets said: "O excellent friends, the full liberation of the mind-stream depends upon the excellent one. And what is that? It is as follows:

The condition that ripens the unsurpassed Essence,
The one of great intelligence to whom questions are put,
The guide for those who have lost the way, the spiritual friend and protector,
The precious lamp that dispels darkness,
The eye faculty that sees, the mental consciousness,
The boat that ferries across the fordless river,
The great fortress against fear, the castle to rely on,
The supreme physician who draws out the chronic sickness of saṃsāra,
The captain who brings back jewels from the islands of the ocean:
That excellent guru
Should first be known with certainty.

The treasury of the collections, the great house of treasures,
The twelve keys of commentary,
The excellent definitive sūtras and the ten principles,
The ten limits, the branch sūtras and the root:
Whether one here possesses the ten topics in full,
Whether the extremes of faults have been exhausted
And one has crossed to the far shore of the ocean of qualities,
Whether or not one has reached the measure of an excellent one,
When looking to such a one as spiritual friend and protector,
Whether he is consummate and perfectly complete:
Since what one knows oneself is not the measure of this,
With methods and their branches
One should examine him through others.
If, on examination, he is endowed with all that merits dedication,
One should not go over to another.
If the five requisites of the excellent one are complete,
It is not a matter of lacking the lesser requisites.
If he is surely Samantabhadra himself,
The object of his gaze is not the base.
A lamp does not hold steady in a raging gale;
It is not found in the lowly, loose in desire and loose in speech;
How could it be in the foolish, the dull and the deluded?
Just so with the faults of worldly people:
However those excellent ones may be,
If they are surely guides skilled in the path,
However hard it may be to bear,
All of it, all of it, should be borne.
This excellent one is the guide;
Since he is my protector,
All worldly conventions
Are surely, every one of them, illusion.
These mere trivialities here,
For whom are they a hindrance to the excellent one?
Toward this that is seen for a mere instant,
"If it is like that, then it is like this";
One simply bears it for so long as it lasts.
Come what may, overcome it and practice.
Will not this perverse grumbling
Be turned to the excellent aim?
Reflecting thus, one should hold him as excellent.
Focusing on the great long-term aim,
Generate one's understanding afresh.
Toward the excellent one of supreme bliss,
Because of these momentary conventions,
How could one possibly be indifferent?
When here I wander without a protector,
In all these directions,
Where is anyone of the kind from whom help comes?
Apart from the excellent one there is not a single one.
Though here there are many helpers and protectors,
And whatever ties one may have with the lowly,
They are experts in a mind bent on wrong practice,
Advisers in leaping over a precipice;
They have no thought of holding one back for one's own good.
Why, without a spiritual friend as refuge,
Do all go off to the city of the low-born?
How could they be fit objects of a trusting, loving mind?
For one wandering here in darkness,
The one who shows the path of the light of supreme bliss
Is that great refuge, the excellent one;
No other is able to protect.
Therefore, to the excellent ones
One should attend with utmost devotion, drawing near.
When here the way of light dawns,
Pray with longing to the excellent one himself.
Once one has obtained light from the excellent one,
Of acts that would go against that excellent one
Do not do even the measure of a particle.
Do not break the seal, do not cast aside the transmission;
From the first longing until one is fit for the work,
One must rely on him.
Whoever here has not recognized him
And passes the time in mere indifference,
For him there is no excellent aim,
Because he lacks that condition.
Turning from the objects of training, consumed by doubt,
Through laziness not striving toward the object,
Through slackness showing him no respect,
The knowable is veiled by masses of darkness.
As the seeing of the sky is blocked by thick clouds,
As a knot is one that a child has not the strength to open,
So the binding of knots arises from this:
How could the mind-stream possibly be liberated?
One who is poor in the five excellent requisites,
If that destitute one would free others,
They too become poor.
Being bereft of excellent sustenance,
Destitute of great enjoyments,
They fall into a very wilderness of famine.
Once such a lowly person
Has been taken as one's excellent one,
Whether he is surely excellent or not,
How could breaking his word be proper?
If one's seal with him is breached,
One goes to the Vajra Hell
And experiences unbearable suffering.
The dog that guards the hell, known as Lude,
Like a lion in form,
With fangs like long awls and sharp points,
Sets upon one and chases one along a path of weapons.
Into the forest of swords with razor-sharp edges
One runs here and there, without refuge.
In the fordless river
Of boiling molten bronze and scalding ash one sinks.
Into a dark pit of blazing fire,
Filled with tridents and great arrows,
Needle-like sharp points and long awls,
And sharp knives and swords, one falls.
From those successions of suffering
There is no chance of release.
Having become a disciple, what person with a mind
Would do wrong to that excellent one?
Having wronged all the buddhas,
They always go headlong downward.
Whoever transgresses the excellent one himself,
Who is joined in equality with all the excellent ones,
The excellent yogins who have gained power,
All in turmoil, drink his great blood.
The arranged syllables of the excellent letters
Dawn within the great breath,
And a nine-headed iron scorpion
That has become rākṣasas and yakṣas
Kills him and sends him on into the great hell.
Harmers and robbers of wealth,
Obstructors, and the poisoned points of evil
Do harm to that degenerate one.
Being a sinner, he dies.
Royal punishment and the like,
The eight fears, befall him.
Toll collectors, great beasts of prey and the like,
He is killed by yakṣas and goes to misery.
Why? Because of the eight classes of gods and demons,
All the fierce males, fierce females and oath-bound ones
Are protectors of the Dharma, servants of the command,
And never let the moment pass in carrying out the command.
Therefore the children who practice the Dharma
And enter upon the excellent
Should never disparage his feet,
Nor break even a corner of his word.

Whoever single-mindedly guards the Dharma,
The guardians who protect the Dharma,
The fierce ones of great power as well,
Will always protect him like a son.
And what is it to guard the Dharma?
Whoever is not deluded about any phenomena
And has certain knowledge
Becomes skilled in guarding the Dharma.
Whoever is unskilled in everything
And holds firmly to a deluded view
Cannot be changed by any attempt to change him,
And will never guard the Dharma.
If at all times
That excellent one and he are one,
Yet through a weak character, weariness and sloth
He goes against him, it is a cause of downfall and obscuration.
Therefore do not cut off the scripture of the excellent one.
The scripture of the excellent one, established as valid,
Is all of it spoken with hidden intent,
Applied in keeping with its audience.
And what is that like?
It is as when those with perverse craving ask,
"Who is it that guides the world?"
"O you who bring delight to the world,
If you know how to teach all things as delight,
And, having shown the meaning of delight as I do,
Know the fruit beyond doubt,
Then you are called omniscient."
What was taught to those of such inclination was this:
"If you kill, destroy and utterly vanquish
Father, mother, brahmins and kings,
They are forever the supreme of bliss."
One who was taught this and practiced it
Came to possess the bliss of supreme bliss.
Likewise, in the hidden intent of the Word
There are sūtras and matters of great importance;
So if you trust the one you have taken
As great refuge and excellent one to be valid and excellent,
Hold his way too as excellent.

Lord of Laṅkā, at the time when
The Lord of the Supreme Secret
Commanded me in these words,
"Vajrasattva, Vajradhara, listen!
Those who attain buddhahood over a hundred thousand eons
Should know the master as one held in awe.
Why? Because the buddhas of the eons
Arose in reliance upon masters.
Without relying on masters
Buddhas do not arise;
I have never truly seen them arise so.
Were they to arise, it would contradict the way of the excellent.
Before the excellent one who is endowed with the meaning,
At all times of the three times,
When all the sugatas and victors
Dwelling in the realms of the ten directions arrive,
They pay homage with mantra and with longing.
Beings, gods and humans,
Likewise nāgas and yakṣas,
Rākṣasas holding wrong views,
And all the vidyādharas gather together,
Scatter emanated flowers everywhere, and say:
'Guru, excellent spiritual friend,
You are our father.
Guru, excellent spiritual friend,
You are our mother.
Guru, excellent spiritual friend,
You are our protector.
You are the counselor of those whose minds are ignorant and deluded,
The guide of those who have lost the way,
The lamp of those who wander in dark caverns,
The jewel that dispels torment.
To your sun face we bow and pay homage;
To your sky mind we bow and pay homage;
To your moon body we bow and pay homage;
To your lotus feet we bow and pay homage.'
Thus with joy and reverence
They pay homage with mantra and with longing.
Therefore, since all the victors
And the sattvas and vidyādharas
Arise from the excellent of the excellent,
Just as a mother gives birth to a child,
So should you know the excellent one.
The nature of that excellent one, moreover, is this:
A trustworthy and tamed mind,
An intelligence measureless and vast,
Withstanding conditions, patient, upright in all things,
Honest in conduct and without deceit;
To all beings who are in danger
He gives by skillful means fearlessness of every kind;
Heroic, powerful, of great diligence;
In every object of knowledge
He proceeds just as he wishes;
Though questioned to the end of existence, he knows no exhaustion;
When examined, his qualities appear more and more;
He speaks the truth and his word does not change;
He does just as he has said;
Toward good and bad deeds
He has no conceptual grasping at liking and disliking;
Like the lion, king of beasts,
Splendid and majestic, he is not drawn in by objects;
He has given up busyness and base activities;
Free of wandering and distraction wherever he is, always;
Like a lion he dwells alone,
Keeping to the wilderness and solitary paths.
One endowed with these is worthy to be the excellent one,
An object of veneration in every way excellent."

Even if he lacks that nature,
If he holds the treasury of the excellent and its stewardship,
Take him as excellent and treat him as excellent.
Even if he does not know or realize all phenomena,
By greatly honoring and serving
The guru who has become one's excellent one,
One will cross the ocean of saṃsāra.
Why? Just as seed sown in a fine, moist field
Grows and multiplies,
So in the supreme field of excellent merit
Not a single sesame seed is lost or fails to increase.
There are four excellent spiritual friends
Who protect from the extremes of saṃsāra:
Source, scripture, empowerment,
And the measure of certain understanding: these four.
From them arise four antidotes:
The power of generating the mind, the power of regret,
The power of action and the power of focus;
They overcome the powers of wrongness.
One crosses the four rivers,
All mind-streams are fully liberated,
And one becomes the excellent of the excellent."
Having spoken thus, he said nothing more.

Therefore, Lord of Laṅkā, here,
The intelligent, with joy and reverence,
Before those known to have become excellent ones,
Should show respect three times in their presence
And touch their feet with the crown of the head.
Toward those who are excellent, or not yet certain,
Gathering the excellent Dharma and the rest,
In order to overcome faults,
One should show worldly courtesy and respect.
When his word comes, whether in reproach or in encouragement,
Respond again and again with attentive deference.
Marking the pauses with words of courtesy,
Recall all of this as is fitting.
On occasions of asking after his ease, receiving, rising,
Carrying out tasks, making requests and the like,
With a pliant mind, courteous and respectful,
Without obstinacy, act boldly and diligently.
In every way keep a modest bearing and remain conscientious;
Speak honestly and without deceit.
Why? Because one who acts toward one who has become excellent
Unreliably and deceitfully
Deceives all who have become excellent,
And falls into the Vajra Hell.
Those diligent and persevering, of good disposition,
Able to bear blows and to keep secrets,
Elegant, of pleasing countenance, with graceful manners,
Of good conduct in the three doors, restraining the three extremes,
Should act in harmony with the excellent one himself,
And with clarity and purity discern what they understand.
When sitting at ease among the excellent,
Do not become the subject of everyone's talk.
Regarding anyone's good or bad conduct,
Do not disparage them behind their backs.
Speaking of good and bad about oneself,
Telling all one has seen, saying all, exaggerating and denigrating,
If one wishes to draw others into talk:
Cut off talk and one will find ease.
In the places where the excellent dwell,
Do not speak in anger, harshly, or idly.
Do not act in a careless manner.
Do not speak without decorum, improperly,
Or without awe.
Do not boast of daring, ability, or all one has seen,
Nor make empty brags and the like.
Words that cut others down with contempt,
And taking sides, should not be engaged in.
To one who has become the abode of the excellent,
Offerings that are suitable and appropriate,
Wealth and riches, the finest garments,
The finest elixirs, food and the like,
Should be offered and presented to please him.
Thereby illness, poverty and the like,
And the sufferings of hunger and fever,
Will never strike.
Making requests, making offerings, being ill,
Waiting in his presence for his word,
Not understanding the conduct of the excellent:
Staying in one place with the excellent ones,
It is never right to be ignorant of attendance;
Not to act in this way is a fault.
Do not step on the shadow of his body,
And do not tread upon his footprints.
His clothes, cushion, supports and the like
Should never be stepped over.
When the excellent one's word comes, carry it out as it names.
If one is not able and cannot do it,
Without pretense or the like, give up the post.
Guard the excellent one's property like your life.
His close attendants, regard as the excellent one;
His retinue and servants, regard as relatives;
Always look on them with an even mind.
A throne, a bed, a comfortable seat, the manner of a guru,
Disrespect and other improper ways
Are unseemly in the presence of the excellent one;
If one does these, courtesy and samaya decline.
Covering the head, carrying a parasol,
Leaning on the hand, leaning the body, likewise baring the upper body,
And here, coarse and disorderly ways,
Should not be done within his sight.
The ways of commoners, caṇḍālas and low-caste women
Are never fit to come before the excellent one's gaze.
The conduct of the wanton and the mad who roam everywhere
Is not for the presence of the excellent.
Act on whatever arises in his visible instruction;
As the act arises, so the action arises.
Act gently and slowly, as though wary of being heard.
Within sight of the excellent one's conduct
Do not behave with great carelessness.
Having trained, adopt a graceful manner,
And remain conscientious.
Whatever the body must discharge
Should not be within his sight or hearing.
Visible bodily functions
The intelligent do not perform there.
Crowding together in idle gatherings
Is not fitting in the excellent one's presence.
Restless, distracted, greedy and unpeaceful ways
Should not be shown within his range.
The bodily functions that arise from the elements,
Hiding, concealing, screening or covering them,
Do all things softly, quietly and gently,
In whatever way is seemly before that excellent one.
From whatever side the excellent one moves his seat,
Be attentive on one's own part and show courtesy.
Moving, folding, pressing and airing, prepare his seat;
From whatever is lacking, arrange it for his comfort.
Make effort beforehand, and when his word comes, gather courtesy;
And when it is completed, do likewise.
When the intelligent engage in the four modes of conduct,
Apply courtesy and respect in the same way.
Disrespect and indifference
Should never be shown at all.
Courtesy, honor and service:
Before a task and at its very time,
Include courtesy and go about at ease.
Ask after his ease and the like with courtesy.
In the very presence of true austerity,
Wherever one is and whatever the occasion, offer courtesy.
Never call him by his name;
Saying "excellent one," "guru," "spiritual friend,"
Add a word of respect at the end.
Make distinctions of respect in everything.
When his word comes, say, "As you command."
Receive his speech with palms joined.
Follow the excellent one's word as it comes.
All of this is what courtesy signifies.
Adopting a graceful manner, with reverent longing,
Request permission three times.
With humble pliancy free of obstinacy and pride,
Do all attendance mindful of his intent.
With the conduct of a newly ordained monk,
Give up flirtatious and similar behavior,
And the wearing of ornaments and bearing of weapons.
That intelligent reverence itself
Let one examine in oneself,
And give up grounds for others' censure.
Whatever person all agree is good,
Act as he does.
Whatever base one all agree is not praiseworthy,
Give up his conduct.
Maṇḍalas and the like, fire offerings,
Accepting disciples, and teaching the Dharma
Should not be undertaken without his word;
In the excellent one's domain this is not fitting.
If one works for the benefit of others,
Do not use the requisites received for it;
Offer them all to the excellent one,
And take what remains after the excellent one has taken.
Even making disciples of the excellent one's disciples
Is not fitting in the excellent one's presence.

It is not only honor, courtesy and the like:
Whatever there is, the excellent one himself possesses.
Whatever the excellent one bestows out of compassion,
The intelligent should receive with both hands,
Going forward with courtesy to meet it.
Free of dullness, with clear mindfulness, well composed,
Do not transgress your own deportment.
That sun among the intelligent,
In order to worship the excellent one like a deity,
Should offer in full to that excellent one
The eight requisites that please him:
Faith without weariness,
Humble pliancy without obstinacy or pride,
Material goods without miserliness,
Courtesy without hesitation,
Uprightness without guile or deceit,
One's own being without flattery,
Wisdom without delusion,
And stainless superior intention: these eight
Offer in full to that excellent one.
When the bounds of the training enjoined by the excellent one
Are completed in full, the mind-stream is freed,
And one moves unobstructed through all that can be known.
The wisdom that unties the knots of one's own mind-stream
Moves like a bird in the sky.
At the end, the stream untied and objects abandoned,
One's kingdom and one's own body,
Sons and wife, wealth and riches,
The supreme wealth one most cherishes and delights in,
Offer to that excellent one.
Even one's own body, as is fitting,
Redeem with wealth for whatever it is worth.
Then, just as it is, wherever one is at ease,
Enter into resting in genuine yoga.
The excellent ones of old, the excellent,
Follow in the footsteps of the sugatas.
The sugatas, the excellent ones of old,
Having pleased the excellent captain,
Abandoned everything and went to the edges of the wilderness;
By this the excellent one himself is pleased.
Therefore, from the excellent captain
Never be separated at any time,
Just like a body and its shadow.
When the intelligent ask, they become excellent.
Just as a thoroughbred steed
Bound by a restraining fetter,
Once released, comes out with ease
And goes unhindered anywhere,
So this thoroughbred faculty of mind,
Bound by the bonds of ignorance,
When the excellent one opens the door of liberation,
Frees all its mind-streams and goes unhindered.
This is the untying of the knotted bonds
Of beings in the stream of ignorance;
To become accustomed to the training
That frees the mind-streams is most precious.

"Lord of Laṅkā, these should be known as the thorough deliberation on the bounds of the training that fully frees the mind-stream." When he had said this, the sattvas of lesser body came to know that the countless buddhas had been trained by excellent masters.

This is the sixty-seventh chapter, called "The Supreme Excellent Honoring," from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again the Lord of Secrets said to the bodhisattvas of excellent body: "O friends, the opening of the doors is as follows:

Upon the essence ascertained
As suchness just as it is,
By way of awareness and unawareness,
In what appears in two ways,
As all-knowing conduct and as all-pervading delusion,
It becomes many and diverse.
Delusion appears in two ways:
Ignorance and wrong knowing.
Ignorance is twofold: the slack and the Lokāyatas.
Wrong conception is the Extremists and the tīrthikas.
These, moreover, attached and with perverse craving,
Are called 'the world' and 'the worldly.'
The wrongness of the tīrthikas and the wrongness from other conditions
Are also described as 'arising incidentally.'
These two kinds of ignorance, appearing as the basis,
Those of inferior lineage and of uncertain lineage,
And those of severed lineage who hold perceptions:
These are darkness thickly gathered on every side.
The four with perceptions: the attached,
The distracted, and the timid,
And those known as Endowed with Inclination,
Appear as fields that hold the seed.
This wrong conception is a wondrous thing:
Three hundred and sixty-two,
Twenty or ten, five and two,
It appears in many diverse forms.
From partisan holding that lacks the requisites come
The view of the perishing collection, extreme views,
Wrong views, holding views as supreme,
And holding one's own discipline as supreme:
These are known as the five classes of discordant kinds.
Wrong by nature is wrongness itself.
Wrong from other conditions: practicing without subtlety,
Relying on scripture yet holding only to its signs,
Practicing artificially and speaking nonsense.
Relying on a mere fragment of scripture,
Going astray on the wrong side, corrupted by adulteration,
And deciding from a partial standpoint: these are known as the four kinds.
The artificial are only two:
Those changed by another and those changed by this.
Endowed with five, place, retinue, requisites, wealth
And enjoyments, they are changed by another.
Eternalism from recollection, holding to dependence as the cause,
The extreme of nonexistence, knowing the infinite,
The view of total non-distraction,
The view asserting that consciousness exists after death,
Likewise the view of nonexistence,
The view that this arises from that,
The view that it is neither existent nor nonexistent,
And 'when I die, this life is nirvāṇa.'
Here, relying on a mere fragment
Is simply not being what it is not.
What has become going astray on the wrong side
Is sixfold: each separate and distinct;
The general view that whatever is known is faultless;
The view of the cause, the view of purity through loving-kindness;
The view of purity through contrived livelihood;
Contrived ornaments, shoes and clothing,
Matted locks and adornments;
And sixth, relying on austerities.
What is corrupted by adulteration:
Relying on it, the view of self, conceiving supports,
Attachment to supports, attachment to blessings, auspicious signs,
And wrong refuge: these are utterly corrupt.
What is deciding from a partial standpoint?
Outsiders and the genuine,
Secret mantra practitioners and those of the great secret.
Here the outsiders are of three kinds:
The Vaiśeṣikas, the conventionalists,
And those who decide one-sidedly from their own appearances.
The genuine are subtle and profound,
Vast and spacious, suchness itself.
Secret mantra: the Vedas, conduct of awareness,
And union of families, deciding from a partial standpoint as excellent.
The great secret: great practice of discipline,
Those who overthrow kings and those of royal methods.
These that arise incidentally
Are complete in ten limitless classes;
Whoever has reached the limit holds the signs
And is an attendant of all the buddhas.
Lord of Laṅkā, moreover,
Just as it came from his mouth,
After the lamp of the world has set,
Those of diverse inclinations
Arise incidentally in the same way.
All-knowing conduct is also of two kinds:
The gradual and the simultaneous.
Gradual entry is of three kinds:
The unmistaken itself,
And the three of accomplishment, conduct and realization.

This too, by way of two kinds of distinction,
Was taught by the excellent one in threes, threes and twos:
Do you not know it?
Therefore great and small, two, three and six.
Accomplishment is twofold, profound and subtle:
The greatly intelligent, the profound, and the lesser.
Conduct is of vast waves and partial,
Vast, and practicing with signs;
That is explained as the secret of the great secret.
Realization is of two kinds, entering outer and inner.
Seizing on the crux of the discordant side,
The wish for the two parts separately arises.
The outer are two, the Vaiśeṣikas and the conventionalists;
From one of the two parts dispute arises.
Entering inward: the mind's appearance without center or edge;
Holding to the side that joins outer and inner as one,
Here definitive decision arises;
Distinguishing extremes and center, definite entry,
And establishing arise in the same way.
Holding to the side of one's own view,
Through debate doubt arises,
And distinguishing center and extremes, definite entry,
And establishing arise in the same way.
For the yogin of simultaneous entry,
Action, the means of action, and non-action,
The born, the unborn, and no birth or cessation:
All this is unmixed and perfectly complete.
Unmixed: planets and stars spread across the sky;
Perfectly complete: all-pervading, spread out like the sky;
Like a rainbow in the sky, unmixed and one.
The deluded, the gradual, and the conduct of simultaneous entry:
Abandoning attachment to objects of desire,
Transforming conduct, realizing through knowledge, mastering conduct.
Knowledge: worldly, conceptual and noble;
Conduct: that of the intelligent, the means of mastery.
The vessels for this, too, he saw as four:
Those of the lineage of attachment, clinging and perverse craving,
And to the vessels possessed of intelligence
What is to be taught was spoken in four ways:
Words that lead, conditioned by their wishes, provisional and indirect;
Hidden intent; and the ultimate, definitive: these four
Are matched to the conditions of the vessels.
All this, on the one ground of the knowable,
Is seen differently according to one's wishes.
Thus, just as the doors of seeing differ,
In the array of manifold appearances,
One who enters and looks into them all
And brings them down to one is the excellent one.
Just as a bee in search of a hole
Goes into every one and comes out again,
And then, whichever is its own crevice,
Enters it surely and stays there,
So the yogin who seeks the meaning
Gathers, following, whatever doors of conduct there are,
Enters the doors of view, examines and compares them,
And arrives at the very secret of the definitive ultimate.
In order to track down that meaning itself,
Comparing all the doors throughout
Is the method in which to train;
To become accustomed to it is most precious.

"Lord of Laṅkā, these are the full opening of the doors that appear as arising later; they should be known as the thorough deliberation on the bounds of training in comparing all." When he had said this, the sattvas of lesser body came to know the doors of all phenomena as self-appearing.

This is the sixty-eighth chapter, called "Fully Opening the Doors of All Phenomena," from the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas.

Then again the Lord of Secrets said to the sattvas of lesser body: "O friends, what is it that is to be brought to completion? It is this: the aspect of the inconceivable is to be brought to completion as the unthinkable. The unthinkable is to be brought to completion as the suchness of mind. The suchness of mind is to be brought to completion as the suchness of the gathering of the excellent intentions. Why is that? If one is skilled in the suchness of the gathering of the excellent intentions, one becomes skilled in the suchness of mind. If one is skilled in the suchness of mind, one becomes skilled in the unthinkable. If one is skilled in the unthinkable, one becomes skilled in the aspect of the inconceivable. If one is skilled in the inconceivable, that is omniscience, and it should be known as the very gathering of all the intentions.

"Friends, therefore, the aspect of all inconceivable phenomena is fully explained by three kinds of yogin. What are the three? The yoga of action, the yoga of performing action, and the yoga of non-action.

"Friends, the yoga of action, moreover, should be known as threefold. What are the three? Outer yoga, inner yoga, and outer and inner yoga. Friends, among these, what is outer yoga? It is this: the great yoga called earth vajra, and likewise water vajra, fire vajra, wind vajra, space vajra, form vajra, sound, smell, taste, touch, and the great yogas called dharma vajra, brought to completion and made supreme in the suchness of the excellent intention.

"Friends, inner yoga is this: the great yoga called mind vajra, and likewise the yogas called the vajra of whatever is grasped, conception, analysis and the like, brought to completion and made supreme in the suchness of the excellent intention.

"Friends, what is outer and inner yoga? It is this: the aspects of all, of white, of black, of the certain, of the uncertain, of the lesser, of the excellent, of what arises, of what does not arise, of the made, of the unmade, of the prescribed, of the unprescribed, of the common and of the particular, brought to completion and made supreme in the suchness of the excellent intention. Friends, these should be known as the yoga of action.

"What is the yoga of performing action? It is this: support, conduct, the eye, distinction, the unchanging, suppression by prowess, and total awareness. Friends, these too are brought to completion and made supreme in the suchness of the excellent intention. Friends, moreover, there are these: union, non-abiding, moving, transforming, prowess, arising, entering, door, knowing, abode, awareness, path, entering, primordial wisdom, and pervading; these aspects too are brought to completion and made supreme in the suchness of the excellent intention. Moreover, the doors of seeing, non-distraction, meaning, word, mantra and letter: these aspects too are brought to completion and made supreme in the suchness of the excellent intention. Friends, these should be known as the yoga of performing action.

O friends, what is the yoga of non-action? It is this: ground, training, application, examination, transgression, the unerring, certainty, intention, primordial wisdom, accumulation, practice, accomplishment, meditative absorption, the great, primordial wisdom, the mode of arising, greatness, play, boldness, splendor, power, miraculous display, and complete perfection; these aspects are to be brought to completion and made supreme in the suchness of the excellent intention. How is that? Friends, it is like this:

Just as one who climbs to a lofty mountain's peak
With diligence, never growing weary,
Sees everything clearly from that high place,
And so knows well the paths, all laid out in view,
So too, if on the lofty mountain of wisdom
One surveys everything with excellent diligence,
Wisdom reaches what is supreme over all,
And the primordial wisdom of the skilled is beyond measure.
One should become skilled in the meaning;
This is the supreme training.
To become skilled in that very excellent intention
Is most precious.

Friends, these should be fully known as what is to be brought to completion.

When he had said this, the heroic beings of lesser bodies became supreme over all and attained forbearance toward the suchness of the excellent intention.

This is the sixty-ninth chapter of the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, called Teaching the Inconceivable.

Then again the Lord of Secrets spoke to the excellent ones: "Friends, the bringing of everything together is accomplished through these: through activity, through intrinsic nature, and through transformation. Friends, among these, activity is through the activity of gathering. Intrinsic nature is through the nature of reality. What is to be tamed through transformation is this: cause, time, measure, renown, hindrance, sign, and attainment.

"Friends, among these, the cause is simply the five great requisites. The time is the completion of the ten limits, close inquiry, the gathering of those of the right family, the attainment of clarity, becoming fit for action, the appearance of signs, many mantra practitioners, the finding of meditative experience, and the completion of the activity.

"Friends, the measure is this: the guide, the omens, and ripening. Friends, what is the guide? It is this: familiarization, awareness, gathering, attainment, and completion. Friends, the omens are these: seeing, prosperity, capacity, and hearing. Friends, ripening is this: the fierce rite and the unshakable.

"Friends, what is renown? It is this: the lineage of the four rivers in which one has arisen, those above whom one is exalted, what one possesses, what one is skilled in, and what one has attained.

"Friends, what is hindrance? It is this: attainment, non-attachment, being unstained, and burning. What are the signs? They are these: renown, transformation, firmness, and arising.

"Friends, what is attainment? It is this: attainment through conferral and attainment through prowess. Friends, it should be understood that this is attained as follows:

Here, to the objects of compassion,
Whatever blessing may arise,
To yogins of aspiration
Twelve volumes should be given.
To whoever is a yogin of application
The sun and moon both should be given.
The yogin who gains great reassurance
Will surely obtain the seven precious things.
The yogin who has attained the great prophecy
Obtains the eight svastikas.
The yogin of perfected great prowess
Obtains that very distinction of the ten prowesses.
The excellent master of all
Obtains the unsurpassed blazing emptiness of thought.
This great secret, the Gathering of All Intentions,
If, without having heard it, one explains it presumptuously on one's own,
Then even striving in all the boundless teachings,
One will never come to see the excellent.
Just as one born in a royal lineage,
Installed by the many as sovereign lord,
Gains dominion over all
Through the precious wheel and the other treasures,
So one born in the lineage of yoga,
Empowered as King of Dharma,
Through the blazing emptiness of thought and the rest,
Goes on to become the distinguished, excellent master.
This is the distinction of the master:
To become familiar with the means of training,
Traversed with prowess, by which one surely comes to the throne,
Is most precious.

"O friends, these should be known as the full deliberation on bringing everything together."

When he had said this, the lesser excellent ones attained the warmth of the teaching of the excellent scripture.

This is the seventieth chapter of the Great Sūtra of the Gathering of the Intentions of All Buddhas, called Explaining the Excellent Yoga.

Then again that Lord of Secrets spoke to the heroic beings of lesser bodies: "O friends, what is to be decisively settled is this: going onward without turning back, following the armor one has donned. Thus, just as the multiplicity of things follows things as they are, and just as the elements follow space, so mental events follow mind itself, and all phenomena follow the nature of reality. Likewise, since all that is spoken and all that is done follows the excellent intention, wherever one practices, it is just as the Gathering of All Intentions has explained. O friends, that is like this:

Equal and unequal, unequal and equal,
Wholly unequal and perfectly equal:
The distinctions of Secret Mantra conduct, too,
Are to be practiced as equal in meaning with whatever it may be.
Conduct through blessing,
Or conduct through the means of mastery,
Is like space or a king,
Or like a mass of fire or a river.
These, too, through the greater or lesser reach of their domain,
Exert their blessing according to their distinctions.
Just as that king, too, by his distinction,
Has degrees of greater and lesser
In the realms over which he holds sway,
So the yogic way of mantra appears.
Entering the conduct of great means, too,
Is entering directly, as befits one's measure.
It is like an elephant entering water:
The great elephant heeds neither ford nor slope.
Yet by greater or lesser prowess and vigor,
Some cut straight across,
While some go the way the water flows.

Thus, though those who enter yogic conduct
Cut straight through to the fruit, the actual meaning,
Because, through the differences in the high or low reach of their prowess,
They practice according to the measure of their ability,
Grounds, phases and primordial wisdom,
Obscurations, deviations and conceptions,
Conduct, samaya and meditative absorption,
Time, accomplishment and culmination,
And the distinctions of view are transformed;
And although all of them are nothing but one,
The many renowned enumerations and divisions,
Matched to the capacity of the vessel,
Are taught with every kind of distinction.

Moreover, through the distinctions of prowess,
If lesser yogins, from the level of the beginner
And upward, abandon their own respective roots,
And practice the way of the great yogins,
Of the yoga of perfected great prowess and the rest,
Desiring its aim,
Then, though a frog may act out the deeds of a garuḍa,
The object it seeks is beyond its reach.
One whose rank in the trade of jewels is low,
Abandoning the measure of livelihood that is his lot,
Though he sets his aim on the great wealth
Of a king, high in rank through his means,
Does not obtain it and is left without a living.
These aims of the fortunate
Are hard for those of weak fortune to accomplish.
Therefore, in the Gathering of All Intentions,
Here, for the sake of lesser yogins,
The practices of healing and of purifying
Are taught as suited to the lesser ones.
This rite of healing and of raising
Is practiced in keeping with those who conceptualize.
Healing cures the illness of being seized by conceptual thought
With medicine for lapses in view and conduct.
In this rite of raising the dead,
The sick of the three realms of saṃsāra,
Dead in the extremes of permanence and annihilation, are taken from the charnel ground;
Taking up the corpse, the great carcass of delusion,
In order to raise it into non-arising and non-ceasing,
One should strike the corpse with the mantra
Of the wish-fulfilling jewel and press it down.
Having oneself become the antidote of the corpse,
Recite the corpse mantra without interruption.
When the splendor of the corpse arises,
Make diligent effort without a break.
When that corpse begins to stir,
Anoint it with the appropriate elixir of transformation.
Having bound all the corpse's limbs,
Do not interrupt the fierce recitation.
When it rises up there,
It displays a host of terrifying miracles.
The person who keeps the yogic discipline
Then cuts off the corpse's tongue;
If he strikes the corpse with it, it turns into gold.
Likewise, if it is transformed by the condition
Of whatever is wished for, it becomes just that.
The tongue is said to be a sword, a weapon,
And with it one cuts off the corpse's head;
Whatever substances one wishes are accomplished.
Taking the skull as armor
And wielding the tongue as a sword,
One turns back even the battle ranks of the asuras.
The fleetness of foot that comes from accomplishing the vetāla
Is beyond all measure:
If a man endowed with prowess,
Looking to the four directions, all at once
Shoots arrows, then before they fall to the ground
One retrieves them at the limits, circling the world in an instant.
Before the man's hand has even dropped,
One has at once arrived back at the place,
Gaining speed as swift as a moment.
These and endless other things,
All that one wishes, come from the corpse.
When, practicing on the corpse as a corpse, the corpse is accomplished,
The substance of the corpse no longer appears,
And since it appears as whatever it was practiced to be,
How could there be conceptual thought about the corpse?
Just as one is resolved by knowing
That the corpse has no intrinsic nature of a corpse,
If one likewise practices conduct in equality,
The excellent ones call it "view and conduct are equal."
"This is the conduct of the excellent nectar
Of raising the dead,"
So the Gathering of the Excellent Intentions has proclaimed.

Friends, these should be known as the complete practice of the rite of raising the dead.

Friends, what then is the practice of purification? It is this: purifying the thorn of conceptual thought, and thoroughly purifying the mind-stream. Of these, purifying the thorn of conceptual thought is purifying the things of conceptual thought. Friends, thoroughly purifying the mind-stream is purifying through the activity of arrangement and purifying through the activity of fire. Of these, what is purifying through the activity of arrangement?

At the start of the undertaking, the beginner,
When entering the ultimate meaning,
Should be purified by the five supreme means.
In the vajra approach at its appointed times,
In close accomplishment here,
And in the application of great accomplishment,
Numbers, times and signs are to be set.
At this door of the command that delimits the yoga,
The secret door is not opened, for they would be terrified.
Why? Because they are beginners:
Childish ones whose minds have not yet unfolded,
Uncertain, turning back and entering,
Would stray from the meaning of the Great Vehicle.
The childish ones who enter this place,
Even when drawn out of the six worlds,
Are placed on the ground where, subject to change, they are not yet certain,
And gentle means are applied as is fitting.
The five clusters of the great vajra means,
Endowed with the sixteen hosts of Vajradharas,
The vajra maṇḍala of the expanse,
Is to be drawn, or else visualized,
Or else used as speech, actual objects or symbols.
Through the religious customs of the lords of the world,
Having generated devotion and mindfulness,
And drawing benefit from the natural objects,
When one pays homage, makes prostrations and so forth,
With the lesser vajra substances blended into elixir,
With the great requisites and the rest,
Make offerings to the assembly of vajra means.
Singing the songs of vajra yoga,
Pleasing them, the activity accomplished and the count completed,
Once they are pleased, one should aspire to purify the mind-stream.
To disciples who are fit vessels,
Through such acts as knowing their faculties,
Grant the mode of entry, empowerment and accomplishment.
With the five kinds of vajra oaths,
Having given them, as appropriate, the responsibilities of training
In the form of prowess,
They swear the vajra oath of great importance.
Then the greater and lesser practices,
As they wish, with earnestness,
The austerities of vajra yoga, are to be done.
With vajra words in which such things as "unable" have no place,
One should urge them on firmly.
For the sake of the bewildered, one rouses their minds
And transforms their perception with wondrous words.
To those with the eye of wisdom, the objects of knowledge,
All of them, should be taught in sequence.
For those who have warmth and great breadth of mind,
Open the secret door into inner yoga.
To the coarse in manner, the wild and unruly,
Show the perilous defiles and the distractions of saṃsāra.
To those who have the words of vajra prophecy,
Confer empowerment according to their capacity.
For those without prowess, who are unable,
Only the benefits are praised.
Here, toward the clear and the unclear,
Neither cast down light nor cast down weight.
To those whose minds have the vajra as their object,
Give the counsel: "Train in great prowess."
In the empowerment rows of the vajra assembly,
Unfold the great bundle of vajra meaning,
And distinguish prowess and levels.
The mudrās of the respective families
Are given either in common or else one by one.

Furthermore, the assembly of the dhāraṇīs of wisdom,
Just as with arranging the assembly of means:
Those who are endowed as yoginīs,
Vajradhāriṇī and the others,
Having been arranged as an assembly of goddesses,
Here, entry, joy and the rest,
Concealed in the signs of the mudrās of means,
The dhāraṇīs of vajra equality
Are to be pleased with offerings of equality.
With dance, song, verses and the rest,
Accomplish the vidyādhara yoginīs.

Furthermore, if one knows the four times as equal,
How could it be fitting to call them "times"?
If one fully knows the four times as equal,
That itself is the nature of equality.
The vajra goddesses, the assembly of the times,
The queens of equality who hold sway
Over the portions of past and present,
Future and undetermined,
In destruction, birth, ripening,
And becoming empty:
In these, the vajra liberations, the immeasurables,
Mind, and likewise the wisdom of equality,
The retinue and the excellent center,
In their due order, with vajra
Songs, dances and other requisites,
Offerings and modes of entry as they should be,
Not letting the portions pass beyond their times,
On the days of the deities.
Great bliss and the rest,
The clusters or else the mudrās,
Are to be applied in common or one by one.
Creating the assembly of liberations for the past,
And for the present and the future
Creating immeasurable minds and liberations,
One becomes the king of equality.

Furthermore, the yogic mind of awakening,
Perfected in sixteen like the waxing moon:
When compassion and primordial wisdom dawn,
Like the sun rising in the sky,
The yogin perfected in the excellent
Is one who stands on the ground of true certainty.
To traverse to the thirteenth,
Apply the continuous assemblies of the vajra times.
Just as the full moon amid the host of stars
Is completed in sixteen days,
Since the thirteenth ground, of perfected great prowess,
Is completed through the sixteen assemblies,
By the sixteen moons that have changed,
That bliss, beautifully adorned
Upon the mudrā of the sun's clear light,
Brings about the perfection of the sixteen assemblies.
In perfection itself there is no reference point;
Because it has been purified into suchness,
In what has become empty through equality,
Everything is perfect awakening as the dharmakāya.
Suffering and awakening are not two;
Having generated the marvelous mind, perform the great activity.
When the subtle has become warmth,
If one practices the excellent, the sun rises.
If at this time the continuity is interrupted,
It is hard to turn back even with pleasing words.
If an interruption occurs, perform the rite of fulfillment and restoration.
Just as the bright moon in the sky grows full in its horns,
On earth, practice yoga so that it grows full.
When this has grown full, acts of merit
And of great dedication are to be done.
Through that, from dwelling on the ground of the vidyādharas
Who travel riding the sun and moon, onward,
Great bliss is accomplished on the thirteenth ground.

Furthermore, what is to be done continually:
All phenomena are the nature of the Victors,
Appearing as the Victors' body, speech and mind.
The mudrās of body, speech and mind
Are complete in the garland of letters itself.
The primordial wisdoms of the letters and mudrās
Are to be practiced in an unbroken stream.
Through that, one obtains unbroken meditative absorption.
Without cutting off the unbroken stream,
At the vajra hours of night and day,
Generate and apply the assembly of letters,
And likewise apply offering and approach together.
This is the path close to the ultimate,
For it brings about the excellent.
Here, through that unbroken assembly,
That itself becomes the most excellent of the excellent.

Furthermore, the expanse of the vajra net,
This secret, the great secret of the mind:
Since vajra is vajra,
Through the connection of vajra faculties and objects,
Everything arises; therefore it is called the expanse.
Vajradhāriṇī together with the perfected holder,
Their embrace in equality is likewise:
The images abide as the nature of meditative absorption.
If one applies them in due order,
Then just as one wishes,
It is taught to be equal, just like the desire realm.

Furthermore, the maṇḍala of the excellent great secret,
In which whatever is connected wherever it may be:
This nature of all phenomena,
Linked to one another as in a net,
From the space-like nature of emptiness,
Koṭis of emanations appear as anything at all,
Linked in the manner of a nondual net.
That very domain of the great yoga:
For one endowed with prowess who stands at the secret door,
For such a one the secret door should be opened.
The children who have entered this secret,
Having great intelligence and possessing means,
Are not afraid; joyful wonder arises in them.
This maṇḍala of the great secret:
The Vajra Kings together with their queens,
Five times two, embracing in pairs,
Are arranged in the manner of embrace from right to left.
The right holds the vajra of awakening,
Each one's own emblem blazing.
The left holds the echoing bell,
Clearly beautified with insignia,
Radiant with swaying and bowing,
In a manner passionate and moving,
In a manner moving like great lions,
On great lions, bulls and the rest,
On thrones that are the bodies of lesser beings,
Seated in perfect majestic poise,
Blazing with light of five colors,
Graceful in the thirteen kinds of attire:
Generate them gradually or all at once.
Here the faculties that are attached and clinging
To the desire realm are to be purified in stages.
Apart from that, not even father and mother are taken as a reference.
The retinues of the secret assembly, too,
By their nature of transformation,
The mudrā kings together with their queens,
Eight times two, are arranged in entwined, embracing pairs:
Kṣitigarbha, Ākāśagarbha, Avalokiteśvara, Vajrapāṇi,
Maitreya, Vajra, Samantabhadra and Mañjuśrī.
The inner and outer requisites
Are applied by the four pairs to give delight,
And so too visualization and the rest.

Together with the vajra gates,
The secret assembly in vajra embrace,
Through the application of great yogic enjoyments,
Is offered to in sequence, to please them and to bring union.
Through the secret mode of entry and the rest,
The secret aims are to be accomplished.
With entrance, conduct and view in harmony,
All having become one,
Endowed with causes and conditions in perfect abundance,
With the condition of loving harmony and with blissful union,
Deities, vidyādharas and yogins, all of them,
Gathering without exception, practice this Dharma.
An instant, a moment, a short while,
A moment, a single day, each month,
The auspicious days of the year,
The great festivals when their time has come:
These are the great occasions of blessing.
Whatever requisites one can afford,
Distinguished as pure and impure,
Clean food and the like,
And the nectars that conquer the illness of conceptual thought,
To the assembly of the Victors and to the bhūtas,
Offer earnestly, as one's wealth allows.
Acting for oneself, for others, for both,
And what does not serve the purpose of the Dharma:
Abandoning these four faults, apply the means of reversal.
Toward yogins, as toward one's own heart and eyes,
Establish the attitude of tender affection,
And provide for their ease as they wish.
To please the deities,
To pacify the obstructors,
To accomplish aims, and to revive the dead,
Performing this rite is most precious.
These are fit to be told
Before newcomers of small understanding.
All substances and requisites, too,
Should be combined with prowess, according to whatever will tame.
Therefore this rite in particular
Builds accumulation upon accumulation and purifies obscurations;
Having grown familiar with the approach,
Through accomplishment the fruit is brought to culmination.

Thus did Vajrasattva
Speak to the great assembly.
The whole assembly, having seen the meaning,
Addressed Vajradhara:
"Great Being, we have
Certainly seen that the nature of all phenomena,
The arrangement of means itself and wisdom itself,
The all-equal and the sixteen portions,
The assembly of letters and the expanse of arising,
Their linking in a nondual net and the rest,
Appears as the images of the meditative absorption of intrinsic nature."
When they had said this, he replied:
"All of you who are gathered here in this assembly,
It is just as you have seen.
Primordially unfabricated, just as it is,
The maṇḍala of intrinsic nature is suchness itself.
The excellent intention, spontaneously present,
Is itself the excellent maṇḍala of meditative absorption.
Since mind itself is their root,
Sentient beings are clear as the maṇḍala of images.
Into the maṇḍala of the meditative absorption of intrinsic nature,
All have primordially entered, been arranged, and been completed.
Beyond that, especially for fortunate people,
There are images that have become their sphere of experience:
On fine, soft cloth, well proportioned and the like,
In five colors or in twenty-five,
They are depicted so that the bewildered who have not seen may rely on them.
With unimpaired marks, they become delightful.
Relying on them, in a gentle shrine,
Depict the forms in blazing colors;
If their mudrās are unimpaired and they are beautiful,
They can display miraculous signs.
In gold, silver, copper, iron and the like,
Casting images of the Victors,
If they have unimpaired mudrās,
They too become supports for signs.
In crystal, stone, conch and the like,
If one makes images of the Victors in relief,
And likewise makes them unimpaired and full,
They become supports from which signs arise.
In plakṣa wood, bodhi-tree wood and the like,
With images carved by a skilled craftsman,
If their mudrās are unimpaired and all complete,
These too become supports for signs.
Making the mudrās in clay and the like,
And giving them beautiful colors,
They are supports from which signs arise:
Through signs, one knows signs to be liberated.
Those who make the shapes of the Victors from clay,
Finely formed in every way,
If they beautifully adorn them with the five pigments,
They become supports for knowing liberation.
Since whatever is the image of whomever
And that one's nature are one,
There is not the slightest difference here;
Through this one comes to know liberation.

"Whatever palace of the Sugatas
One makes in the manner of a maṇḍala:
In a pleasing, well-qualified, secluded place,
Making it beautiful and even all around,
Provide it with eight lines,
Beautified by the activity of the means of attraction.
Then encircle it with a single line.
When it has taken the form of a maṇḍala,
Inside, with eight even lines,
It becomes nine sections.
Having divided the periphery into thirds,
Within them all, three
Fine lines beautifully adorn it.
Having divided it into ninths,
Make the gates and gate-frames a third.
Beautify it with eight pillars,
And within the jewel pillars
Make the buddha palace with its pinnacle.
The gates of the means of attraction, the immeasurables and the liberations,
Beautiful in three tiers,
Are adorned with archways and steps as well.
The central palace and its sections,
Wheels, śrīvatsa knots, lotuses,
Eight-faceted jewels and the rest,
Draw them as is fitting.
The ornaments are various jewels,
pañcarakatvarana,
Adorned with garlands and with nets,
Fringes and hanging tassels,
Bells, small bells and little pendants hanging.
Many sons of the gods, sounding music,
From the sky within and without,
Draw them presenting various offerings.
Richly adorned with great supporting beams,
With jeweled cornices and stūpas,
And jeweled victory banners blazing with light,
The celestial palace, beautifully adorned,
Just as the palace of the Dharma is,
Draw it so as to represent exactly that.
Whatever is placed in the celestial palace,
Let all of it beautify and finely adorn it.
All offerings, surpassable and unsurpassable,
Offer earnestly, as one's wealth allows.
Kingdoms together with their riches,
Together with song, dance and music,
Medicines that destroy poison, and nectars,
Offer to Victors as numerous as the ocean.
By applying the offering of equality,
All purified mind-streams become equal."

When he had said this, the whole assembly,
All of them, their mind-streams purified, knew liberation.

Friends, these should be known as the thorough purification through the activity of arrangement.

Friends, what here is thorough purification through the activity of fire? It is this: in order to purify all phenomena into the nature of reality, all things were blessed as the nature of fire, and so the whole gathered assembly saw all phenomena appearing as the nature of fire. Utterly overwhelmed, they praised him in wonder:

"How wonderful! Sattva, great sattva!
Greatest of the great, great in blessing!
Great in meditative absorption, great in illumination!
Great in miracles, great in variety!
The supreme means cannot be measured:
All phenomena appear as a blazing mass of fire.
That very expanse of clear primordial wisdom,
Having become blazing, radiant and clear,
Burns all things, both dark and bright;
That all be perfected in the expanse of great bliss
Is what you, O Hero, have done.
Great blaze, a la la!
