The names of several realms and mountains (Utterly Pure, Summit Arrayed with Various Jewels, Lofty Beryl Light, Lofty through Dharma, Possessing the Essence of Awakening) are translated rather than given in Sanskrit, as no standard Sanskrit forms could be confirmed. The lists of designations and the "five bases" are highly technical and terse; they appear to enumerate the stages and paths of the vehicles, including the five yogas of Anuyoga (aspiration, opening the great family, drawing the great breath, attaining the great prophecy, perfecting great skill). The Lord of Laṅkā, Matyaupāyika, is one of the "five of the sacred lineage" known in this tradition; the Sanskrit form is the conventional reconstruction. The answer to the first question is cut off at the end of the piece and continues in the next. "Eight and twelve years" renders ལོ་བརྒྱད་དང་བཅུ་གཉིས literally; it may be meant as a total of twenty years. རིམ་ལ་ཡ is read as Malaya (རི་མ་ལ་ཡ), the mountain in Laṅkā where, in this tradition, the five sacred ones gathered after the Buddha's passing. In the third answer, the line on "calm abiding, insight, and action, these three ends" appears to correlate the three baskets with these three, but the phrasing is compressed. ཚད་དེ in the sentence after the fourth and fifth questions is read as ཆད་དེ, "cut off," as in the parallel sentences. The mountain དྲག་ཤུལ་ཅན is rendered by its meaning, "Ferocious," as I cannot identify a Sanskrit name for it with confidence. The prince's name རྒྱལ་བྱིན is likewise translated by meaning ("Gift of the Victor"); it may stand for Jinadatta or Jayadatta. In the line མི་ལས་རྒྱལ་རིགས་དྲ་མ་ཆེ, the word དྲ་མ is obscure; I have read the line as "the great one of royal caste among humans." གནོད་འདུལ་རྒྱལ་པོ, "the King Who Tames the Harmful," is an epithet of Vajrapāṇi, the Lord of Secrets. In the line གཅོད་རྙིང་སྦྱང་བར, གཅོད makes little sense; I read it as གཅོང, giving "chronic illness." The word འཁྲས་ས in "the refuge of beings" is obscure; I take it as a place of shelter, in keeping with the fortress image. In བཀའ་སྒྲོམ་ཐར་རམ་གསང་བ the phrase ཐར་རམ is unclear; I render the sense as "the secrets locked within the casket of the Word." Here བསྒྲལ་བ has its ordinary sense, "to ferry across," not its sense in wrathful rites. "Heroic beings in the bodies of beings" renders འགྲོ་བ་ལུས་ཀྱི་སེམས་དཔའ, the sacred ones who took birth in various classes of beings. The phrase ས་རབ་སྣང་བ is taken as the name of a ground or stage, "Supreme Illumination"; it could instead refer to a place or person, and is uncertain. The world མེ་ཏོག་བཀོད་པ ("Array of Flowers") and the buddha མ་གྲགས་པ ("Unrenowned") are translated by meaning, since I cannot confidently identify their Sanskrit forms. In the line about objects of offering, བྲབ་སྒྲོགས is obscure; I have rendered it as "proclaiming scriptures." The sentence about rice and sesame describes an impossibility: relics in a definitive sense do not exist. ཚད་མ, "the Authoritative One," is taken as an epithet of the Buddha as the embodiment of valid authority (pramāṇabhūta). In ཡུལ་ཚད་མ་ཉིད་ཀྱིས the word ཡུལ is unclear and is not rendered separately. In གང་དུ་གྲོགས་པ, གྲོགས་པ makes no sense; I read it as གྲགས་པ, "proclaimed." The names of the six sages are not the usual ones (the usual list has Śakra for the gods, for example); I have given Vijaya, Śākyasiṃha, Vairocana and Dharmarāja for the standard renderings, and translated སྲིད་ཐུབ་འཛིན ("Holder of Mastery over Existence") and རྣམ་པར་གནོན་པ ("Subjugator") by meaning, since I cannot identify their Sanskrit forms with confidence. The world realms དྲི་མ་མེད་པ ("Stainless") and ཡོན་ཏན་འབྱུང་བ ("Source of Qualities") and the buddha name དྲི་མེད་སྒྲ ("Stainless Voice") are also translated by meaning. The many names of world realms and buddhas in this passage are translated by meaning, since I cannot confidently identify their Sanskrit forms; several may correspond to names known elsewhere (for example, སེང་གེ་སྒྲ to Siṃhanāda or Siṃhaghoṣa, ཡིད་མངོན་པར་དགའ་བ resembles but is not the same as Abhirati). The Lord of Secrets is still speaking, continuing the speech begun in the previous piece. Most field and buddha names are translated by meaning, as they have no securely identifiable Sanskrit forms. The phrase "proceeds to the ground of exhortation" renders an obscure line (skul ba'i sa la gzhes) whose sense is uncertain. In the list of births, "Eager to Enter" translates 'jug sred, an epithet I cannot identify with confidence (possibly a name of Viṣṇu or Agni); Nara (sred med), Balabhadra (stobs ldan) and Vasudhara (nor 'dzin) are tentative identifications. "Sheep" for lu 'de is a guess for an unclear animal name, and "one endowed with patience" (bzod ldan) and "wondrous being" (ya mtshan can) are literal renderings of unclear class names. "Neither frightened nor alarmed" renders mi skrag mi dbang gi, where the second verb is unclear and may be a scribal slip. The phrase sha stag pas bsngags kyi ngo bo nyid is read as "all of them ... the very essence of mantra" (taking bsngags as a slip for sngags). "That embodied being" (lus kyi sems dpa') presumably refers to the Lord of Laṅkā, to whom the speech is addressed. "Figurative designation" renders bla dgas (upacāra), a merely conventional or metaphorical label. "The Explanation of the Intention" (dgongs pa nges par 'grel pa) may be a title (cf. the Saṃdhinirmocana) or simply "the definitive explanation of the intention"; the sentence in which it occurs is syntactically difficult. "Delivers all beings completely" reads yo bsgral as yongs su bsgral. The phrase srog bton pa is taken as "rescue the lives" in parallel with "bring out to freedom"; it could also mean "take the lives," which would bear on the following statement about killing without actual killing. The heading "the account of the Complete Array of the Lamp ... the second part, showing how it came about" is a section colophon of the text's larger structure, and "the twelve statements about past events" refers back to an earlier list in the text. In this passage de bzhin nyid is rendered "suchness" and de kho na nyid "reality," to keep the text's distinction between them. "Desiring Inclusion" translates gtogs 'dod, apparently the name of a god listed among Īśvara, Brahmā, Indra and Gaṇapati, which I cannot identify with confidence. "Water wheel" for zo chu and "descending flame" for lce 'bab pa are tentative. The list of kinds of mind continues into the next piece. Several items in the long list are obscure and rendered tentatively or left in phonetics: ligri (lig ri) and khrimön (khri mon) are unidentified objects; jagak (bya gag) is an unidentified bird; "emanation" (sprul pa) may be a slip for sbrul, "snake," given the surrounding animals; "maternal uncle" renders zhang, which may instead denote an object; "mildness" renders sla ba among the tastes. "The great affair of the realm" renders yul gyi don srid chen po, apparently addressed to the Lord of Laṅkā as a ruler. "Those who dwell on the grounds, one after another" reads sa dang sa la gnas pa as the bodhisattvas on the successive grounds. The questioning of whether mind can see mind (the sword that cannot cut itself, the fingertip that cannot touch itself) parallels the Kāśyapaparivarta. In the verse lines the text reads སེམས་དཔའ ("being"), which here evidently stands for སེམས་པ, "the mind"; translated accordingly. གྱིས་པ་རྣམས and གངས་པ་རྣམས are read as ཡིན་གྱི་ས་པ་རྣམས and གང་ས་པ་རྣམས ("those on the grounds"), matching the preceding passage. ཅི་ཉིད་ཀྱིས་ཀྱང is read as "you yourself" (ཁྱེད་ཉིད), as the reply in the first person requires. ཡེ་རྔམ ("awe, intimidating presumption") is somewhat uncertain in this context. The stages named here (aspiration or the beginner, opening the great lineage, great confidence, the great prophecy, perfecting great power) are the five yogas of the Anuyoga path, with their associated grounds. The boys' names Bliss Protector and Dumbur could not be identified with confidence: the first is translated, the second (དུམ་བུར) is given as written since its meaning is unclear; Tralita is spelled as in the Tibetan. The universal monarch's name རྣམ་པར་སྣང་བྱེད is rendered Vairocana, distinct from the buddha Vairocana (རྣམ་པར་སྣང་མཛད). The names of his five sons are translated from the Tibetan. The last line of the third verse, མི་གནས་བཅུ་དུས་ཆོ་འཕྲུལ་སྣང, is compressed; "the ten directions and the times" is a probable reading. "The Blessed One, that being of vajra essence" renders སེམས་དཔའ་རྡོ་རྗེ་སྙིང་པོ, taken here as an epithet of Vajrasattva, who is the speaker throughout, not the separate bodhisattva Vajragarbha. མངོན་པར་མཐོ་བ is read as མངོན་པར་མཐོང་བ ("directly seen"). In the clause "the holy beings who dwell on the tenth ground", ལས་བཅུ་ལ་གནས་པ is read as ending the previous phrase with ལས, followed by "dwelling on the tenth", that is the tenth ground. ཡོང་ at the start of the beings' question is taken as an interrogative ("how"). In the verse, བཤེས་གཉེན་དམ་པ་དེ་གཉིས་ལས is read as དེ་ཉིད་ལས, addressing Vajrasattva ("from you, holy spiritual friend"); as written it would be "from those two". གངས་པ་རྣམས is read as གང་ས་པ་རྣམས, "those on the grounds". ཡོད་སྟོན་པ at the start is taken as "but here is the Teacher". དྲ་མ་ཆེན་པོ in the opening simile is unclear; it is rendered "great personages", reading it as a term for nobles or eminent people. དབང་བཅུ here is taken as the ten masteries (vaśitā), not ten empowerments. The heaven of mastery over others' emanations (དབང་སྒྱུར) is Paranirmitavaśavartin. Lha ne in the verse on signs is read as lhan ne, "vivid." In the prophecy to Sattvavajra, bya bar 'gyur is read as the parallel zhes bya bar 'byung bar 'gyur ("will appear as the buddha called"). The second set of five families (Forbearing, All-Tormented, Six-Arising, Lineage-Holding, Abode-Holding) and the third (All-Illumining and the rest) are translated literally; I could not identify their Sanskrit equivalents with confidence. "Essence of awakening" renders snying po byang chub / byang chub kyi snying po, which also names the seat of awakening (bodhimaṇḍa); the text plays on both senses. The sentence contrasting those "swollen with doctrines of action" (las kyi chos kyis bsgyings pa) with those who "have not reached the supreme" is syntactically loose; it is read here as a contrast between long effortful practice and instant devotion to the secret. "Wedded to" renders bag ma pa (literally "bride-like"), read as intimate union with Vajrasattva as emptiness; the sense is somewhat uncertain. The final verse line's lhan ne is taken as "vividly present." To the question "do you wish for the essence of awakening?" the reply as written is "it is already fully ripened" (yongs su smin to), although the follow-up asks "why do you not wish for it?"; it is translated as it stands. In "We do not see them" (ma mthong legs so), legs so is read as the evident slip for lags so. In the reply about great compassion, the line "Because all is nothing but buddhahood" lacks the usual "they replied" but is evidently their answer. Several of the examples in the list (the heart of stone, what overpowers by its prowess, what enters into objects, what arouses delight) are given literally, as their exact referents are uncertain. The passage ends mid-list; the lines continue in the next piece. "Heavens of the Thirty-Three" renders lha gnas gsum (literally "the three abodes of the gods"); the referent is uncertain and may instead mean three divine realms. The phrase yum gtsug tor 'khor los sgyur ba'i lhums is taken as "the wombs of mothers of uṣṇīṣa wheel-turning monarchs"; it may instead refer to the deity Uṣṇīṣacakravartin. In the closing word analysis, "definitive" glosses nges pa in the title dgongs pa nges par 'grel pa, rendered as the Explanation of the Intention. The list "like beryl, like kaṅkari, like lac dye" contains two uncertain items: kaṅkari is left in its Indic form, and le brgan is taken as a red dye. In the list of manners, bdud pa'i tshul is taken as "the enchanting manner"; several of these manners (cam me, ma le, lhan ne, sal le) are expressive adverbs whose renderings are approximate. The opening reference to "four births" (skye ba bzhi) is translated as written, though the following exposition is framed in terms of four miracles. The two closing lists correspond to the seven factors of awakening and the eightfold path, given in this text's own order and wording. The arithmetic of the conceptions is obscure. The text first speaks of "ninety, or eighty-one" mountains, then calls the nine "the mountains of ten times nine" (ri bo bcu dgu pa), and then asks about "the eighteen and the one" (bcwa brgyad pa dang gcig pa), concluding "eighteen in general"; the numbers are translated as written, though they do not obviously sum to eighty-one. "The six continua" and "the six limits" (rgyud drug, mtha' drug) are rendered literally; their exact referents here are uncertain. The text announces "twelve splendors" but then lists twenty; the number is translated as written. In the seed-syllable list, "the unmoving" and "the infinite," standing beside Amoghasiddhi, likely allude to Akṣobhya and Amitābha among the five buddha families, but they are rendered literally. "Consideration" renders rtog pa, which may here carry the sense of a topic or section of exposition. In the seed-syllable list, the entry for kṣaṃ speaks of the "defining characteristic" of abandonment rather than its "nature of reality," as the other entries do; this is translated as written. After kṣaḥ, the Tibetan word ni is taken as a topic particle rather than as part of the syllable. In the run "the powers, the faculties, miraculous power," the word dbang po is read as "faculties" (indriya), among the factors of awakening, rather than as the name Indra. The second stanza on "perception" probably stands for "formations" (the fourth aggregate, which would follow perception in the list), but the Tibetan repeats "perception" and is translated as written. Likewise, "form is fear itself" may reflect the etymology of form as "that which perishes," with a near-homophone; it is rendered as written. The phrase rendered "beyond doubt" for the powers is the Tibetan idiom meaning "certainly, without fail." Several evident scribal variants are read in their plain sense: chan as chad ("cut off") in "when definitively cut off," bsnyes as brnyes ("found"), and gseng as gsang ("secret"). The phrase rendered "in the vajra essence itself" (rdo rje snying po) could alternatively be read as the name of the bodhisattva Vajragarbha as the one addressed; it is taken here in its descriptive sense. "The lesser, the bodhisattva, and the yoga of conduct" appear to name lower vehicles and approaches that have no place in the great maṇḍala. In one refrain the Tibetan reads "the speech of all perfect buddhas" instead of "the secret"; it is translated as written. The line on the origin of the tantras appears to name the classes of tantra in compressed form (atiyoga as "yoga free of activity," anuyoga as "concordant engagement," and caryā, yoga, upāya, and kriyā); this reading is likely but not certain. The scribal forms dra pa and gnos are read as dra ba "net" and gnas "abide." The "four things" in the monk's verse are traditionally understood in this narrative as the four root transgressions (killing, stealing, sexual misconduct and lying), which Black Liberation, the future Rudra, takes literally as license. The monk's name, Youthful Hard to Subdue, is translated from the Tibetan since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence; Keukaya and Denbak are given in phonetics for the same reason. In Liberation's complaint, the text's གཉིག is read as གཉིས ("both"). "The two measures" are presumably the two kinds of valid cognition, scripture and reasoning. Several animal, bird and insect names (nele bird, rutsé bird, puka worm) and the tree Nalabi cannot be identified with confidence and are given descriptively or in phonetics. In the hell list, "Lesser Heat" and "Blazing" render tsha ba chung ngu and 'bar ba literally, since this text's list does not match the standard eight one for one. "This is your very own karma" reads ཁོ་ན་ཉིད་ཀྱི་ལས as ཁོ་ཉིད་ཀྱི་ལས, his own karma. The name མ་ཟན་མ་ཏང་ཀ is given as "Mother-Eater Mataṅka": ma zan means "mother-eater," and Mataṅka is the Sanskrit-derived part of the name; it is the figure better known as Matram Rudra. The text's ལྟུང་བཤད་དག is read as ལྟུང་བ་ཤ་དག, matching the parallel phrases around it. The meaning of ཕྲུ་མ in the preta's boast is uncertain; it is rendered "domain." "The eon of the three eons" probably refers to the eons of famine, disease and weapons mentioned earlier in the account. The passage ends in mid-verse; the next piece continues it. The place description in Laṅkā (Tsoko Katangmar, Kinnara's Leap, Where the Sky Iron Is) is obscure; the names are rendered partly in phonetics and partly by meaning, and Kinnara's Leap translates བྱན་ལག་མཆོངས tentatively. The account of the duel with Mataṅka Runa uses archaic and unusual wording (ཛམས, ཇ་ཞ་མར་སད་པ, འུད་ནས, ཇེན) and is rendered by its evident sense. The corpse appearing as "the eight lands of the abodes of virtue" probably refers to the traditional eight great charnel grounds. Mataṅka Runa, the god-leader, is kept in phonetic form since it cannot be confidently identified. Karuna (ཀ་རུ་ན) is the god-leader called Mataṅka Runa in the previous piece; the verse spells his name differently and it is kept as written. Several phrases in Rudra's boast are archaic and obscure, notably ཧྲི་ཞེས་དབང་རྒྱུད་དུ་ཉན་ན ("crying hri, tried to bring me under his control"), ཐག་ཁོས ("cut the cord") and ཐམས་ཅད་འུབ་ལ་རྗེན་རྦན ("I have gathered them all in and laid them bare"); these are rendered by their probable sense. Here "mudrā" means the form of a deity arising from emptiness, as in Mahāyoga. བདེ་མཆོག in the epithet list is rendered "supreme bliss" rather than as the deity Cakrasaṃvara, since it is one of a string of epithets of suchness addressed in praise. ཡོན་ཏན་འབྱུང་གནས "Source of Qualities" stands in the position of the Ratna family (Ratnasambhava) in the five-family sequence; གདོན་མི་ཟ་བ "Amogha" stands for the Karma family (Amoghasiddhi). Read གཅོད་རྙིང as གཅོང་རྙིང "chronic disease". རྗེས་ཐོབ here reflects the stock formula "attained one's own aim" (anuprāptasvakārtha), not post-meditation. The palace is named ཆོས་ཀྱིས་མཐོང་བ "Seen Through the Dharma" in the verse and ཆོས་ཀྱི་མཐོ་བ "Height of the Dharma" in the prose; both are translated as written, though they are probably one name. རྡོ་རྗེ་གོ་འབྱེད་ཡོངས་སུ་བསྡུ་བའི་མཚན་མ is uncertain; rendered "the sign of the vajra opening that gathers all together". Several Old Tibetan or colloquial expressions in the narrative are uncertain: ཕྲུ་མ is taken as "encampment"; ཐའི་ཁ་བརྡབས as "retorted scornfully"; ག་ལ་ཇེན and རྗེན་པ as "come under your power / subdue"; གོ་ཟུ་སེ་མོ་དོ as "robe and necklace"; བུར་མེར་ཐོར་ཤུག as "scattered in confusion"; ཨ་ཙ་མའོ as a cry of pain, "Ah, the agony!". ཁོ་ན དགའ་ལྷ "his place of pleasure" is obscure; in the traditional account Hayagrīva enters Rudra from below and emerges at his crown, which the following sentence describes. The emanation called རྡོ་རྗེ་རྟ་གདོང "Vajra Horse-Face" is the Hayagrīva figure of this narrative; མགྲིན་བུ "Little Neck" appears to be Rudra's mocking name for him. "The four things" (དངོས་པོ་བཞི) is left literal; its reference here is unclear. ཆེ་མཆོག་འབར་བ in the last stanza, "the great and supreme Blazing One," may allude to the heruka Che mchog (Mahottara); it is rendered by meaning. In the final line, དེས་པ is read as ངེས་པ, matching the refrain throughout. བརྟན་གཡོར is taken as "stable and firm"; it may instead mean "stable and moving". The bodhisattva's name བསྐུལ་བའི་གོ་ཆ་བློ་གྲོས་ཐབས་ལྡན is translated by meaning ("Armor of Exhortation, Intelligence Endowed with Means"); I could not identify a Sanskrit form. The narrator ("I") is the Lord of Secrets, telling the story to the Lord of Laṅkā. Several rare words are uncertain: འཇོལ་ཉག ("unrestrained"), རྐོ་རྐ་ཐང་མར་རྒྱ་མཚོའི་དངོ ("whose slopes and plains run down to the ocean's shore"), མི་ངམ་སྐྱབས་ལིངས ("great haunt in the dreadful wilds"), བྱ་སྒྲོན ("bird-lamps"), ཡོ་སྒྲོལ ("sets right the crookedness"). In the list beginning "Vajradharma, the Wheel of Great Joy," it is unclear whether each line names a separate figure or gives epithets of the Horse-Face emanations; it is rendered literally. ཅང་ཤེས is taken in its usual sense of "thoroughbred steed" (ājāneya). The names of the four gatekeepers, the charnel-ground ladies and the Greedy Mistress of Desire are translated by meaning; I could not securely identify Sanskrit forms. སྨེ་བ་བརྩེགས is rendered Ucchuṣma, as the mantra confirms. Several lines are obscure: སྦོད་གཏོང ("ready to be unleashed"), བཤུད་བ་ལྔ (taken as "the five impure substances"), གཞོན་ནུ་སྣམ་ནས་བཏོན་པ ("drawn forth from the youth's lap"), and ལོན་འུན ("turned to"). The three lines given in romanization in the Mistress's speech appear to be in a non-Tibetan language, possibly Bruzha, and are left untranslated. ཐབས་གསུམ in "a sound resounding three times" is read as ཐར་གསུམ, as in the earlier "rākṣasa cry three times". The line "Avalokiteśvara with twenty-seven eyes" plays on the name, literally "the one who looks with eyes": twenty-seven eyes fit nine three-eyed heads. Several items in the opening description are uncertain: the word rendered "vermin" (smigs) and the implement "stira" (sti ra) are not securely identified. The phrase rendered "by the strength of their vajra stamping" (rdo rje stam pa na'i stobs) is obscure and may conceal a name. "Lord of Joy" (dga' dbang) and "the Mighty One" (mthu chen) are translated literally as names not identified with confidence; "Madana" renders myos byed, an epithet of Kāmadeva. Several phrases in the verse on Rudra's purification are obscure: "the mountain of the putra" (bu tra'i ri bo) and "purified him outright" (phyal du sbyangs) are tentative, as is "those with the branches" (yal gam can), which may denote a class of practitioners. In Rudra's lament, "your compassion is small" is a reproachful plea to be liberated quickly. Rudra's address "you attendants" (nye gnas) in the first speech is to his own retinue; in the lament the phrase "through the blessing of your compassion, my attendant" is addressed to Vajrasattva and is somewhat unusual; it may reflect the earlier narrative in which the deity appeared as Rudra's servant. The line "the worldly time will not stand empty" (mi stongs) is obscure; a reading "will not wait" is possible. "The great eightfold reviling" renders spyo ba'i brgyad chen po; the number may refer to the series of "surely the fault" verses, and the phrase is uncertain. The four steps of Rudra's fall (going against the command, piling up the mountain of the manifest, burning the wish-fulfilling jewel, engaging in the four acts) are translated literally; they allude to the earlier narrative of his transgressions. "Mixed practice" renders phra men, here in the sense of a hybrid or adulterated practice. The verse passage at the start continues Rudra's song begun in the preceding piece. "Black Throat" (mgrin nag po) and the rākṣasa king "Shatrang Sengbu" (sha trang gseng bu) are names not identified with confidence; the first is translated, the second given in phonetics because its sense is opaque. "Lord of Dance" renders gar gyi dbang phyug. "The host of those with perverse craving" renders log sred can literally; I could not identify it with confidence as a standard name for a class of beings. In Vajra Rākṣasa's reply, the repeated particle re after "you" could also be read as making each line a rhetorical question ("Is it the rudras that drive you round?"); I have taken the lines as statements. "The scripture of accomplishment" probably echoes the siddhi-yoga-āgama of the text's Sanskrit title. The five yogas named here (aspiration, opening the great family, great reassurance, receiving the great prophecy, perfecting great power) correspond to the five yogas or paths of Anuyoga. "The upāsaka called Black Excellent One" renders dge bsnyen legs ldan nag po, the form of Mahākāla known in Tibetan as Legden Nakpo; I have not given a Sanskrit form. The vajra names (Vajra Sprout and the rest) and the buddha name "Voice of the Lord of Ashes" are translated by meaning. In "pleasure grove of supreme bliss," bde mchog is read as "supreme bliss" rather than as the deity Cakrasaṃvara. In "so that it is hard to transgress," bka' bas is read as dka' bas ("since it is hard"). "Black Liberation" renders thar pa nag po, the name of the Rudra figure of this narrative (often left as Tharpa Nakpo); "Vajra Great Might" renders rdo rje mthu bo che, possibly Vajramahābala, which I have not asserted. "Savage Vajra" (rdo rje ma rungs pa) is taken as an epithet of the great being who tamed Rudra. "The four points of the body" renders lus kyi bag bzhi, whose exact sense is uncertain. In the last question, ya gyung is read as yang byung ("also arose"), as in the line it quotes. "Without fail" renders dmyags ma mchis par, which is uncertain. The four consciousnesses here (root, referential, all-appearing and engaging) are matched with the five wisdoms, the wisdom of the dharma expanse arising from the mind's nature of reality itself. The five lineages listed are vajra, elephant, jewel, lotus and youthful, which is not the usual list of five families. Gdung dang sde is rendered "lineage and class." The figures for atoms in a league render the Tibetan units as they stand: mi 'khrugs khrag khrig as "one akṣobhya of niyutas," bye ba khrag khrig 'bum phrag sum cu as "thirty hundred thousand koṭis of niyutas," bye ba drug cu phrag brgya as "a hundred times sixty koṭis," then thirty-two koṭis, five hundred thousand, and twelve thousand (khri nyis stong). The exact values of the higher units are uncertain. Several of the thirteen words describing shapes are rare, and their English renderings are approximate. "Wisdom like the vast" renders chen po lta bu'i ye shes, whose noun ("great ...") may have been dropped. The refrain ཕྱོགས་ཙམ་དུ་ཆོས་དེ་དག་མཐུན་ཀྱང་རང་རིག་པ་ཉིད་ཀྱི་དཔེར་ནི་ཕྱོགས་མེད་དོ plays on ཕྱོགས "part, aspect": the analogies match only in part, but self-awareness has no parts or sides by which it could be exemplified; rendered "there is no such part." The explanation of ཡེ་ཤེས "wisdom" glosses its two syllables: ཡེ "primordially" (how phenomena primordially are) and ཤེས/མཁྱེན "knowing." སེམས་ལས་བྱུང་བ, normally "mental factors" (caitta), is here rendered literally "what arises from mind," since the text uses it as one of three aspects of mind. མི་སྐྱོར་བ in the jewel list is uncertain; rendered "needs no support." The four bodies (emanation, perfect enjoyment, dharma body, body of essential nature) correspond to nirmāṇakāya, saṃbhogakāya, dharmakāya and svābhāvikakāya. In the answer on permanence the Tibetan is not parallel to the answers on existence and unity: it affirms permanence but negates impermanence and the two further alternatives, while the other two sets affirm all four. This is translated as it stands. The "power of the lotus" by which one who has passed beyond still benefits saṃsāra is not explained in the passage and is rendered literally. Throughout, utter purity, maturation, continuity, and suchness map onto the dharma body, perfect enjoyment, emanation, and essential-nature body as set out in the preceding passage. The final speech of the Lord of Secrets continues into the next piece, so its closing quotation mark is left open. པདྨོ་ཅན and མོས་པ་ཅན, the buddhafields of the secret and of continuity, could not be identified with confidence as standard Indian names and are translated by meaning ("the Realm Possessing Lotuses," "the Realm Possessing Devotion"). གཙུག་ཏོར་འཁོར་ལོས་སྒྱུར་བ is taken as the name Uṣṇīṣa Cakravartin. སྐྱེས་བུ་དམ་པོ is read as སྐྱེས་བུ་དམ་པ "sacred beings," and ཡང་གིས་འོད་གསལ as a variant of "luminous" in the standard triad empty, unfabricated, luminous. The final speech of the Lord of Secrets continues into the next piece, so its closing quotation mark is left open. "Actual basis" renders དངོས་གཞི, the original that a reflection indicates. The question about "those of great mind ... fashioning well" (ལེགས་པར་བསྐྲུན) is taken, in light of the Vajrasattva example just given, to refer to the making of images; the verb itself only says "fashion, construct." The letters named are syllables of the Sanskrit syllabary, each assigned a meaning; the speech of the Lord of Secrets continues into the next piece, so its closing quotation mark is left open. The last six "letters" (Hook, Boundless Light, Hope, Crescent Moon, Drop, Staff) are given Tibetan names rather than Sanskrit syllables; they appear to name diacritic or graphic elements of the script, and are translated by meaning since no Sanskrit equivalents could be identified with confidence. In the verse, the "glorious knot" (དཔལ་བེའུ, śrīvatsa) at the heart is the origin of the sounds, which then move through throat, tongue, tongue-tip, and lips, following the Indian classification of guttural, palatal, cerebral and dental, and labial consonants; ཐོར་ཏོར "at the crown" is taken in that sense (the cerebrals, mūrdhanya). The vowel ཨུ appears twice in the prose list as written. The verse continues the account of the letters' mode of origin, whose framing speech continues into the next piece. Much of this passage describes the shapes of letters and the vowel and diacritic strokes of an Indic script, with many rare or non-Indic words. Several comparison objects could not be identified with confidence and are given in transliteration or by their literal sense: amokari, thabura, tola, khalaktse, kartika, kuñcita, shanta, hāraka, naro, and litsaktsik; "a lion poised" renders the obscure gyalwa; "one eating with relish" for ṭa is a tentative reading. The names of the stroke elements (drenpa, öpak, repa) are kept in phonetics, since a literal rendering ("guide," "measured light," "hope") would mislead. "Gwar and cwang," "cwangkadeu" and "pwangkadeu," and "śata" and "gardha" look like terms from the text's Bru zha or Indic sources and are transliterated. "Six and thirteen" is literal; the arithmetic of the next passage (six times sixty giving three hundred sixty) suggests some figure such as thirty-six may be intended. The six seed syllables called the "root" are not listed here. The simile of "a single gourd" by whose blessing a tongue becomes a thousand renders ku ba literally; its intended image is uncertain. In the verse on holding the vajra essence, the lines about riding a chariot or horse and the Victors honoring "his mount" (rta stegs, literally a horse platform) are obscure and rendered tentatively. In this text the Middle Way (Madhyamaka) is presented as a lower approach surpassed by secret mantra. The last four verses correlate the four tantric activities (pacifying, enriching, bringing under control, and fierce subjugation) with the four eons of emptiness, abiding, formation, and destruction. The second chapter title reads "conduct" (spyod pa), though the chapter concerns activities; it is translated as written. This passage begins the account of the taming of the Black One, evidently the Rudra figure of Nyingma narrative; "the Black One, bound to saṃsāra by his own rope" renders nag po rang thag literally, and "the Black Captain" (nag po'i ded dpon) is the same figure with his retinue. Vajragarbha renders rdo rje snying po as a name here. The classes of beings (those given to conceptual rites, the low-born marked with moles, the vomit-eaters, those with perverse craving) are rendered by sense where no Sanskrit class name could be identified. The phra men ma are given as "sorceresses" per the house glossary. "Women who may be approached and who may not" interprets rgyu dang mi rgyu, which could also mean "moving and unmoving." This passage continues a narrative in which the converts of the Black One offer their antinomian "samayas," which the Blessed One then restates in nondual terms before showing that the beings' own versions are binding knots. The interpretive renderings are tentative in places: "the two kinds of action" (great and small, presumably virtuous and nonvirtuous), "soot-water" for sol khu, "the eight abuses" for spyo brgyad, and the verse on cutting the effigy's births and "joining that very one to that," which describes the liberation rite in compressed language. "The great self" renders bdag nyid chen po, the tantric "great identity." Several images in these verses are compressed and rendered tentatively: "great stolen bait" (rku ba'i rngad zas), "scent-eaters" for beings who live on smell (gandharvas or similar spirits), the line listing "sandalwood, venomous snakes, and the like," and the verse on "effigies of signs bearing ransom figures" whose holders are reborn as "great predators of the toll-gate places" (sho gam). The Blessed One's final speech continues into the next passage. "Dhāraṇī of suchness" renders gzungs; in this context the sense is likely the consort ("retainer"), mother, daughter and sister being reinterpreted as the nature of reality and wisdom. The phrase "the seven and the five of desire" (’dod pa bdun lnga) is obscure and is translated literally. The question "are these samayas not admitted within the Great Vehicle" renders ma gsol tam, which the answer ("not excluded") suggests means "not permitted/included." "Drew in their four limbs" renders bag bzhi bskums, an unusual phrase whose literal sense is uncertain. The plant lang thang tse is identified as henbane (Hyoscyamus). Several lines are obscure and are rendered closely. "As with the spray scattered by a sneeze" renders gang gi sbrid bsangs, whose sense is uncertain. The groups named in the "times past" verses (e.g. "false speakers who held sway over the practice of causes," "those in whom something other arose," "the corrupters through whom this was not passed on in between") are not identifiable with confidence and are translated literally. ཆར་ཀ་ནག་པོ is given as "the Black Carakas" (wandering ascetics), an uncertain identification. The exclamation ཨེ་མའོ is here rendered "Ah," since the context is a lament. The two yogins' names, ཚངས་པའི་འོད and གྲགས་ལྡན (later གྲགས་པའི་ཚུལ་ལྡན, "Endowed with the Manner of Renown"), are translated literally, as their Sanskrit forms cannot be confirmed. "Hair-splitters" renders སྤུ་བྱེད, whose sense is uncertain. "Brood" renders བརྣོས, also uncertain. The verse "looking at that itself, in that, as that" is deliberately cryptic in the Tibetan and is rendered literally. Most names in this passage are translated literally, as their Sanskrit forms cannot be confirmed: the eon Fully Arrayed, the buddha King of Manifest Arising, the yogins Endowed with Excellence, Stainless Conduct and Auspicious Bliss, and the woman Excellent Ornament. དཔལ་བྱིན is given as Śrīdatta. ན་ཞི་ན and པྲ་མ་ཏ are transliterated names that could not be identified (Nashina, Pramata). "Tamer of the Harmful" renders གནོད་འདུལ, apparently an epithet of the Lord of Secrets. "Act the part of cotton-clad yogins" renders རས་སྤྱོད, whose sense is uncertain. The phrase ཁོ་ན་ངོ་བོ་ཉིད་དེང་ཤིག is obscure; it is read as a command to leave ("get out now"), which fits the context. Several names are translated literally because their Sanskrit forms cannot be confirmed: Giver of Bliss (བདེ་སྦྱིན), Holder of Great Clarity (རབ་ཏུ་གསལ་འཛིན), Endowed with Light (འོད་ལྡན), Endowed with Love (བྱམས་ལྡན). པེ་ན་པ་ཡ is transliterated as Penapaya, unidentified. བློ་གྲོས and མི་འཇིགས are given as Mati and Abhaya. ལྷ་གནས་གསུམ is taken as the heaven of the Thirty-Three (Trāyastriṃśa), a probable but uncertain reading. In "Lord of Secrets, listen" for གསང་བདག་འཇིག་ཉོན་ཅིག, འཇིག appears to be a slip for ཉིད or a similar particle, and is not translated. The glossary rendering "fierce rites" is used for མངོན་སྤྱོད, but in this context, rejoicing in others' practice of the Great Vehicle, it may simply mean the "manifest practice" of the Great Vehicle. The names King of Virtue, Endowed with Light, Mighty Lord Endowed with Light, Bright Gift, Endowed with Virtue and Endowed with Clarity could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning; Kalya is given as transliterated. The line rendered "who pervade all with their minds" (བསམ་གྱིས་ཁྱབ་པ) may be a slip for "inconceivable" (བསམ་གྱིས་མི་ཁྱབ་པ). The closing colophon phrase "It arose again" (ན་ཡང་བྱུང) seems to be a heading word of this section, as the next piece also cites it. "Limit of action" renders ལས་མཐའ, which may mean the culmination or boundary of karmic action; the sense in this context is not certain. In the list of the bodies "shown as three," the Tibetan names four items (none, coarse, male and female, neuter); this is kept as written. The sequence ending "granting, and entering into ten millions, as many as ten millions" (གནང་བ་དང་། བྱེ་བ་དང་། བྱེ་བ་སྙེད་དུ་འཇུག་པ) is obscure; བྱེ་བ might instead mean "division" or "opening." In the passage on the door of the secret, "for the sake of appearance" occurs twice, as in the Tibetan. Several items in the closing list are rare or colloquial words whose exact sense is uncertain: "deceit" (འཐིང་སླད), "boasting" (ཁ་གསག), "insistence" (ནན་ཏུར), "cajoling" (བཞབས), "mire" (འདམ), "obscuring" (འཐིབས), and "prevailing over them" (ཐུབ་པས). In the envy verse, the line about the "all-restraining vajra" (ཀུན་སྡོམ) is kept as written though it contrasts with "all-agitating" in the next line. "Drinking them is the means for the uncertain" (བཏུང་བ་མ་ངེས་པ་ཡི་ཐབས) may rather mean "the means of drinking without fixed rules." Several buddha names here could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning: King of Manifest Emergence (མངོན་འབྱུང་རྒྱལ་པོ), Friend of All (ཀུན་གྱི་བཤེས་གཉེན), Glorious Excellent Ornament (བཟང་རྒྱན་དཔལ). The eon name Endowed with Perfection (རྫོགས་ལྡན) is the usual term for the Kṛta age. The line "The Fierce One gave the blessing and the prophecy" (དྲག་ཤུལ་ཅན་གྱིས་བྱིན་ལུང་བསྟན) is obscure; "the Fierce One" may be an epithet of the Lord of Secrets. In the "Renowned" prophecy, the relation between the learned brahmin woman and Tamer of the Harmful is ambiguous in the Tibetan. Ratnaśrī is given for དཀོན་མཆོག་དཔལ as the usual Sanskrit equivalent; King Protector of Bliss (བདེ་སྐྱོབ) and the place name Sale (ས་ལེ, possibly "pure gold") are uncertain. King Ja is the king of Nyingma tradition who first received the Mahāyoga tantras. In "the eight hundred forty million kings of complete awakening" the Tibetan could also be read as a single name. In the wind verse, ཁྱབ་འཇུག is read in its literal sense "all-pervading" rather than as the name Viṣṇu. The three vehicles of the definitive ultimate correspond, in Nyingma doxography, to the causal vehicles, the outer tantras of austerity, and the inner tantras of skillful means. "The vehicle of the awareness of austerity" (dka' thub rig byed) and "the vehicle of the means of mastery" (dbang sgyur thabs) follow this text's scheme of the outer tantras and inner tantras respectively; rig byed can also mean "Veda." In "endowed with yoga and samādhi" the Tibetan reads mar ldan, where mar ("butter") makes no sense; the general sense has been translated, and a reading such as gnyis ldan ("endowed with both," i.e. the dual tantra) is possible. "The universal mount" renders the obscure spyir chib, which is uncertain. The seven "eons" (famine, stupor, weapons, fire, water, wind, space) play on the eons of decline and destruction; "stupor" for rmongs pa is uncertain (it may stand for the eon of disease). The chapter title reads cho 'phrul sngon byung, "Miracles of the Past," whereas the vehicle in the verses is cho 'phrul mngon byung, "manifestation of miracles"; the title has been translated as written. The four verse sections beginning "Thus" play on the opening formula of the sūtras, "Thus have I heard at one time," read in four senses: the definitive meaning, the secret words, the reasoning of scripture, and words that serve a wish. In the line "Belongs to those of intelligence, yogins endowed with the qualities of practice," blo gros is taken here as the common noun "intelligence," not as the name Mati, since no such person is addressed in this dialogue. Several items in the lists of the bases (for example dong ba, "penetrating," and spyir phyin pa, "general completion") are terse technical terms whose exact sense is uncertain. In the branch of retention, pha ma pa ("the parental") is obscure and may be a scribal slip; it is translated as written. The verb jen pa, rendered "inclines," is uncertain. The lists of reasonings and of pure and impure proofs parallel the Yogācāra discussion of the four kinds of reasoning (dependence, function, proof, and the nature of reality) and of valid and invalid proof, but are translated here as this text gives them. The phrase phyogs kun bkod ("sets out all aspects") is a common phrase here, not the name Fully Arrayed. "The imputed," "the dependent," and "the perfected" render the three natures of Yogācāra (parikalpita, paratantra, pariniṣpanna). The three roots are here called "the key of opening," although the scheme set out earlier assigns the roots to drawing out; the text has been translated as it stands. The opening verses are a highly compressed summary list whose individual items cannot all be identified with confidence; they are translated literally. In the description of the mansion, rdo rjes kun du smras pa ("spoke everywhere with vajras") is taken as a slip for spras pa, "adorned everywhere with vajras." Several architectural terms (the reliquary ornaments in triple lines, the fringes, the vajra lattices) are rare and their exact sense is uncertain. The name of the tathāgata king of form, rnam mi rig, is rendered literally as "Imperceptible"; it could not be identified with a Sanskrit name. The two "Heard at one time" sections play on the opening formula of the sūtras, giving an outer, intentional reading and an inner, self-appearing reading; "the two are simply two expressions of whatever occurred" seems to refer to these two readings. The names of the buddha-fields (Endowed with Inclination, Treasury of Merit, Endowed with Lotuses) and of the ten secret beings (Swift Vajra, Great Vajra Rapid, and so on) are translated by meaning, as I could not identify standard Sanskrit forms with confidence. "Press forward to ask" renders zhu bar sdog pa, whose verb is uncertain. "Horns" renders ke ra go, an instrument I cannot identify precisely. Here nyi ma'i snying po is the "heart of the sun" in a simile and an image of the rising sun, not the bodhisattva Sūryagarbha named elsewhere in the text. Several names in the list of beings pacified are uncertain: 'jug sred ("Entering Craving") and sred med ("Craving-Free") may be epithets of Hindu gods (sred med kyi bu is a known rendering of Nārāyaṇa), but I could not identify them with confidence, so they are translated by meaning; dbang phyug is taken as Īśvara and stobs ldan as Balin. Lu 'de and me khyer ("fire-bearers") are unidentified classes of beings. The phrase ched du 'gro ba ched du brjod par bya in the definition of the branch of connection is obscure and is rendered literally. "Placed my tongue upon a lotus" follows the Tibetan literally. The opening line addresses the Lord of the Supreme Secret as "Vajra King, Lord of the Assembly" (rdo rje rgyal po tshogs bdag); in this context tshogs bdag is an epithet of the one being supplicated, the lord of the assembled gathering, not the god Gaṇapati. "Turned it five times, that is, in twelve ways, and changed it into two and five" follows the Tibetan literally; its ritual sense is unclear. These three chapters set out a hermeneutic of the text's own "branches" (yan lag) in extremely terse verse, and many lines, especially in the chapters on certainty and on the means of entry, can only be rendered literally; their exact technical sense is uncertain. Rendering "guiding" ('dren pa), "certainty" (nges pa) and "full unraveling" (yongs su dgrol ba) follows the plain sense of the Tibetan. In the four sūtras at the end, nges par 'byung ba is taken as "release" (renunciation, emergence). In the list of the qualities of space, several items are double negatives in the Tibetan (ma khyab pa med pa, mi 'byung ba med pa, ma chub pa med pa, stong pa med pa) and are rendered literally as "without anything it does not pervade," and so on. Chags pa med pa, between "without stirring" and "without abiding," is taken in the cosmological sense of formation (as in formation, abiding and destruction). The line "'Why not strive?' That itself is the māra" renders a terse Tibetan line whose sense is uncertain; it may instead mean "Why so? Because not striving is itself [free of] the māra." "The secret essence" and "this very gathering of the secret" may allude to the titles Guhyagarbha and Guhyasamāja but are rendered by their sense. "Ripened from false thought and from right thought" reorders the first line of that stanza for English syntax. The verses play on the word for yoga (literally "joining in the natural state"), contrasting yogins whose conduct is "joined" with their not being "joined" to the genuine meaning. The line rendered "Tear down the piled clutter" is obscure in the Tibetan and its exact sense is uncertain. In "The center is not clear," the text reads "troop" or "mass," evidently a slip for the similar-sounding word for "center." In "thought arises from marks," the syllable as written is meaningless and is read as "marks"; the reading is uncertain. In "Within mind itself," the text reads "illness of mind itself," evidently a slip for "within." The phrase "the abodes of the four streams" is translated literally; its intended referent is unclear. The doubled syllable in the line on the two kinds of valid cognition is a scribal duplication. The line "Since they stand on that ground, look to that" is uncertain. The six distractions listed correspond to the classical Yogācāra list (intrinsic, external, internal, distraction of signs, of attention, and of negative tendencies). In "Whoever enters that bliss all at once," the text reads "that māra," which makes little sense here; it is taken as a slip for the similar-looking word for "bliss," and the reading is uncertain. In "It is the limbs, the sūtra, and the root," the word rendered "sūtra" may here rather mean a junction or main trunk, set beside limbs and root. The opening line completes the sense of the preceding piece: one who teaches it wrongly falls into the vajra hell. The word glossed in the house glossary as Viṣṇu is here used in its literal sense of "all-pervading," describing the ground among a series of qualities, and is translated accordingly. "Luminous of itself" reads a syllable that literally means "again" as the similar "by itself." Several lines in the account of the ground, notably those on "the five" and on "generating and the rest," are terse and their exact sense is uncertain. "Fifty-six" renders the Tibetan number literally. This piece is extremely terse and many lines are open to more than one construal; the translation stays close to the Tibetan. The word glossed in the house glossary as Viṣṇu occurs in the final verse in its literal sense of "pervading and engaging," and is translated so. In "Because they are unlike, that is the sign," the text has a meaningless syllable read as the similar word for "sign." Several images are uncertain: "acquire depth" for the crystal ball, "made one" for earth and stone, "tongs that hold fire, that hold that, that hold a leaf," "snow, grime," and "haze." This passage is a terse enumeration, apparently of the occasions of death and rebirth, and many lines admit more than one reading; the translation keeps close to the Tibetan. The word listed earlier in the text as the name Balin occurs here in its ordinary sense, "having the strength of," and is translated so. "Signs" in "Signs, and whatever here is cleared away" reads a meaningless syllable as the similar word for "signs." "The sevenfold intermediate state" may refer to the seven weekly periods of the intermediate state. "Those who were loving" renders a word that is also the name Maitreya; here it plainly means loved or loving ones. The passage closely follows the Abhidharma account of the intermediate being (antarābhava), including its alternative names (gandharva, abhinirvṛtti, "the existence that links," that is, pratisaṃdhi) and the seven-day and seven-week periods. "The being of the intermediate seven" renders bar bdun pa, the text's own term for this being. The line rendered "an emanated form, the size of a five-year-old" is compressed and its reading (literally "of the final measure, emanation, source, five in measure") is uncertain; it is taken in light of the Abhidharma statement that the intermediate being is the size of a young child. ཉེ་བར་འཁོར་བ here is an epithet of the intermediate being ("the one who hovers near" food), not the name Upāli. ཁྱབ་འཇུག is taken here as "all-pervading" rather than as the name Viṣṇu, since the context is the intermediate being's unobstructed movement. The phrase rendered "scents of its own and not its own" (ང་དང་མི་ང) is uncertain; it may refer to the two parents, toward whom attachment and aversion arise at their union, as in the Abhidharma account. The verses on conception and gestation are highly compressed; several lines are obscure, in particular the lines on the position of the embryo ("If it is the abiding kind ... on the left; if the arising kind ... on the right"), which are rendered literally, and "it gathers as warmth settles." "Stages of the embryo" takes མེད་ནུར / མེར་ནུར as the first embryonic stages (mer mer po and nur nur po). "The all-encompassing one" (ཐམས་ཅད་པ) is understood as the consciousness of the intermediate being entering the womb. In "Whoever has such thoughts and asks this," the text reads གང་དྲུག ("whatever six"), which makes little sense here; it is taken as a slip for གང་དྲིས ("whoever asks"). In "In which all of birth and self are gathered," སྐྱེ་བདག might instead be read as Prajāpati, the lord of creatures, but the context does not support a divine name. "Totally Pure" is taken as the name of the buddhafield (ཡོངས་སུ་དག་པ་ཞེས་བྱ་བའི་ཞིང་ཁམས). "The all-gathering" renders ཀུན་འདུས, the short form of the text's own name, the Gathering of the Intentions of All. In "the subtle aspect that distinguishes extremes and middle," མཐའ་དབུང is read as མཐའ་དབུས, "extremes and middle." "The jewel on the crown of Indra" renders དབང་པོའི་སྤྱི་གཙུག་ནོར་བུ, taking དབང་པོ as Indra; it could also be read as "the jewel on the crown of a lord." In the line "Is a mighty lord," དབང་ཕྱུག is taken as a description of the practitioner rather than as the god Īśvara. "Holding to measures" reads ཚང་འཛིན as ཚད་འཛིན. "It becomes a fault" takes ཚིག་པར (literally "burnt") in the sense of a breach. The recurring closing formula ཞེས་ཐོས་པ་...བགྲོས་པའོ is rendered "This is how what they had heard was discussed"; its exact sense is uncertain. "That essence of space" renders ནམ་མཁའི་སྙིང་པོ, which here describes mind itself rather than naming the bodhisattva Ākāśagarbha. "Since in it there is no I or no I" renders ངའམ་ང་མེད་པས literally; the second term may be meant as "no non-I." The ten occasions of application and the closing lists are compressed enumerations, and the grouping of their items is uncertain. The verse sequence maps the stages of the mind of awakening (moving, aspiring, arising, applying, entering, joy, not changing, awareness, abiding, completion) onto visionary signs compared to stars, lamps, and the waxing moon by day of the lunar month. "Defiled meat" renders སྨེ་ཤ, a term for impure or polluted flesh used among the antinomian substances; its exact referent is uncertain. "Body of essential nature" renders ངོ་བོ་ཉིད་ཀྱི་སྐུ (svābhāvikakāya). "The Gathering of the Intentions of All" in the later verses renders the variant phrasing དགོངས་པ་ཀུན་ནས་འདུས་པ, taken as a reference to this sūtra; it could also be read simply as "the gathering of all intentions." The verse on discipline plays on the two syllables of brtul zhugs (vrata), "tamed" and "entered." The line "and even for one who is not such, once it is grasped" (གང་མ་ཡིན་ལ་ཟིན་འབྱུང་བ) is obscure and the rendering is tentative. ཉི་མའི་སྙིང་པོ, elsewhere in this text the name Sūryagarbha, is here used in a simile beside the jewel lamp and is rendered "the heart of the sun." The second of the six kinds of knowing is listed as ཡ་མཚན་ཅན ("astonished") but explained as ལོག་སྲེད་ཅན ("perverse craving"); both are translated as written. The verses on cause, action, enumeration and so forth are terse and their syntax is uncertain; the rendering follows the Tibetan closely. "Target" renders དམིགས་བུ, the aim or focus point of the gaze. This verse section is terse and many lines are obscure; the rendering follows the Tibetan closely. The ground names (Uncertain and Subject to Change, Foundation through Reliance, Conduct of Great Importance, Continuous Training, Treasury of Merit, Going Distinctively through Reliance, Arising of the Focused Result, Unchanging through Abiding, All-Pervading Nature of Reality, Mounted upon Completion) are translated by meaning. ནམ་མཁའི་སྙིང་པོ is here "the heart of space," an image of the vast expanse, not the bodhisattva Ākāśagarbha. "Wandering ascetic and youth" renders ཀུན་དུ་རྒྱུ་དང་གཞོན་ནུ, apparently examples of one thing known by different names. Several verses are obscure and the renderings are tentative, in particular "together with ordinary people in the four secret domains," the turtle simile, and "there is no protector or noble one for him apart from that; whatever is not held, the rope is cut" (མགོན་བཙུན་དེ་ལ་དེ་ལས་མེད། །དེ་གང་མ་ཟིན་འབན་ཐག་ཆད). "The wheel of time" renders དུས་ཀྱི་འཁོར་ལོ, which here appears to mean offerings made according to the cycle of the calendar rather than the Kālacakra Tantra. ནད་གདོད is read as ད་གདོད "now, anew." "The fourfold fire rite" is the fire offering for the four activities. In the simile of the sun, nyi ma'i snying po is here the core or orb of the sun examined by the eye, not the bodhisattva name Sūryagarbha used elsewhere in the text. The opening list of ten "correspondences" is terse; its items are given in the Tibetan order, and they correspond only loosely to the ten similes that follow. Several lines in the section beginning "For example, in the realms of darkness" are extremely compressed (notably the line on "dedicating the rising and the rest" and the lines with 'on pas ... 'on cing, here taken as "nonetheless"); the rendering follows the most plausible construal. "Danced their steps in pairs" renders the obscure dgyes pa chen po'i gnyis pas bstabs. The animal and warrior images in the first verse section are terse and in places obscure; lines such as "fierce and squat, a great trunk, terrifying and coiled," "the gold of the ocean," "a mind that devours, like jealous rivalry," and "mastering the land, taking a bride, moving in the royal court" render the Tibetan words as closely as possible without a commentary to fix their reference. "Root of disease" reads གཞི་རྩ for the list's གཞི་རྩལ, as the explanation that follows has it; likewise "guarding the regimen" reads བསྲུང for གསུང in the explanation, following the list. The sage's name ནག་པོ་ཀ་ལ is rendered "Black Kāla"; its Sanskrit original is not certain. "The tenth section, placed at the end" renders a division marker (Tibetan bampo, a unit of text length) inserted at this point; its exact wording is terse. The verse line "the heir who had gathered in that retinue, you yourself" addresses the listener directly, as the Tibetan does; who is meant is not made explicit. The eight alternative names correspond to elements of the Sanskrit title of the work, and "Vajra Array" reads dgod pa in the sense of "arranging" (vyūha) rather than "laughter." "The later outflows" refers to bodily excretions, the five substances of tantric samaya. The verse is terse and several lines admit more than one reading. "The excellent Subhūti of the future" renders rab 'byor dam pa, which could also be read as "the excellent ones, richly endowed"; the reference to Subhūti here is not certain. "Ruined in this life and the long term" renders mthong yun phung byed, taken as this visible life and the long future. In "they speak in praise of this secret" the first line reads sngags pa as bsngags pa ("praise"), as in the parallel lines that follow. "The elephant Enaya" renders glang po e na ya; the name is not securely identified, and the point is the bezoar (a prized medicinal substance) obtained from the elephant even after death. "There is one who is supreme against harm to practice" renders the terse sgrub pa'i gnod mchog gcig; the sense, from what follows, is the qualified disciple to whom the secret may be opened. "Fiery bolts" renders the obscure dur mo. The line "going against the command and engaging in malicious deeds" appears twice in the Tibetan, first in a shortened form; it is translated once as an evident scribal repetition. Several lines are terse and their exact sense uncertain: "the enemy that, breaking, falls into the six" (perhaps the six realms of rebirth), the lines on "yearning" ('un pa), and "lamenting constantly without ceasing" (rma ga chad pa). The figure for incidental degeneration, "fifteen, one and three," is given literally as the Tibetan states it. Throughout, "the excellent one" renders dam pa, here the holy master or guru. The glossary entry for bde mchog (Cakrasaṃvara) has not been applied: in this passage the word plainly means "supreme bliss" (a quality of the guru and of the path), not the deity. The name of the hell-dog, Lu de, could not be identified and is given in phonetics. The line containing "gyin da gyin da" is obscure; it has been taken as a colloquial expression for grumbling or petty complaint. Several other verse lines (for example "the object of his gaze is not the base" and "it is not a matter of lacking the lesser requisites") are terse and their exact sense is uncertain. The verses on killing father, mother, brahmins and kings illustrate teaching with hidden intent (abhiprāya): in the tradition such statements are read figuratively, for instance as slaying craving, karma and the like, not literally. The glossary rendering of bde mchog as Cakrasaṃvara has not been applied, since here it means "supreme bliss," not the deity. The exchange with "those with perverse craving" is terse; the attribution of the lines to questioner and answerer is interpretive. "Vajrasattva, Vajradhara" renders sems dpa' rdo rje 'dzin pa, which might also be read as a single epithet, "the vajra-holding sattva." Throughout, "the excellent one" renders dam pa, the holy master. This section is a detailed code of conduct toward the master, written in terse verse; several lines are obscure and their rendering is interpretive, notably "Not a single sesame seed is lost or fails to increase" (the Tibetan reads literally "a sesame seed is not lost, does not increase," taken here as an emphatic double negative), the lines on carrying out the master's word "as it names" and giving up the post, the list beginning "Covering the head," the line "As the act arises, so the action arises," and the line read as "In the very presence of true austerity." "Good conduct in the three doors" reads sbo gsum as sgo gsum, the three doors of body, speech and mind; the written form makes no sense. "The four modes of conduct" are walking, standing, sitting and lying down. "The excellent one" renders dam pa, the holy master. "Well composed" reads rab bsgribs ("thoroughly obscured") as rab bsgrims, which plainly fits the context. "At the end" reads mthar chib as mthar phyin. The chapter colophon has lés for las ("from"), an evident slip. The second half is a terse doxography of mistaken views; several lines are obscure and are rendered literally, notably "simply not being what it is not," the list of six kinds of going astray, the subdivisions of secret mantra, and "those who overthrow kings and those of royal methods" (rgyal dbog pa, rgyal thabs pa), which could not be identified with confidence. "Extremists" renders mur thug pa, a Tibetan doxographical label for a non-Buddhist school whose Sanskrit name is uncertain. The list of views includes items resembling the sixty-two views of the Brahmajāla discourse but is translated as this text gives it. The opening verses are a terse doxographical summary and several lines are rendered interpretively, especially "Holding to the side that joins outer and inner as one" (snrel zhir, of uncertain sense) and the line on the four modes of teaching, which appears to correspond to the familiar fourfold scheme of intentional, hidden-intent, provisional and definitive teaching. "Entering inward" reads nad 'jug as nang 'jug, and "most precious" reads ba tu as rab tu, both evident slips. The three yogas (action, performing action, non-action) may correspond to the classes of outer and inner tantra in Nyingma doxography, but the text is translated as it stands. The four yogins in the verse (of aspiration, of great reassurance, of the great prophecy, and of perfected great prowess) correspond to the four yogas of the Anuyoga path in this sūtra; the "yogin of application" is inserted among them as the text has it. The phrase rendered "the blazing emptiness of thought" (rtog stong 'bar ba) is uncertain; it parallels the cakravartin's precious wheel in the following simile and may refer to a blazing thousand-spoked wheel. Here dbang po is taken as "sovereign lord" rather than the god Indra, since it describes a king installed by the people. The "four rivers" in the definition of renown likely refer to the four great streams of transmission of this tradition. Bsnyen pa, nye bar bsgrub pa and sgrub pa chen po are taken as the stages of approach, close accomplishment and great accomplishment in deity practice. "The lords of the world" renders 'jig rten dbang po, here understood as the worldly gods whose customs of worship are adopted, rather than Indra specifically. The rite of raising the dead is presented as a symbol for raising beings from the death of extreme views into the unborn; it is translated literally as the text gives it. The line "shoots arrows, then before they fall to the ground" has one syllable fewer than its neighbors, and its sense is taken from context. The dhāraṇīs here are the female consort deities. The eight bodhisattvas at the end are given in the Tibetan by abbreviations (Earth, Space, Eye, Vajra, Loving, Vajra, Samantabhadra, Glorious); the identifications Kṣitigarbha, Ākāśagarbha, Avalokiteśvara, Vajrapāṇi, Maitreya and Mañjuśrī are standard, but the second "Vajra" is uncertain and is left as it stands. The line "in a manner moving like great lions" appears to be a duplicated line in the source and is translated as written. The sense of "hard to turn back even with pleasing words" (snyan pa dag gis) is uncertain. "Plakṣa wood" takes blag sha as the plakṣa fig tree; the identification is not certain. "The means of attraction" takes bsdu ba as the four means of attracting disciples, which with the immeasurables and the liberations are assigned to the maṇḍala's gates. "Cakrasaṃvara" renders bde mchog in the praise, following the house glossary; it may here simply mean "supreme bliss," an epithet of the fire as compassion. In "the wise should examine in the fire," the text reads mi la ("in the person"); the following lines on the sounds, colors and shapes of the fire show that me la ("in the fire") is meant. The line rendered "the corpse's face is displayed toward the four directions" (ro bzhir bstan) is uncertain. "Tally" and "register" render མཐའ and ལྟ in the lines on the white and black accounts of the deceased's deeds; the image appears to be of good and bad deeds reckoned like counted pebbles, with the black ones burned in the wisdom fire, but these readings are interpretive. རྒྱུད in "those whose mindstreams are completely pure" is taken in its sense of mental continuum, not tantra. The phrase རྨོག་བཏུད, rendered "helmeted," is obscure. དཀའ་གསལ is read as དཀར་གསལ "white and clear," མི་ཆོགས as མི་ཆག "cannot break," and མི་ངས is rendered loosely as "calamity." "Lamps" probably refers figuratively to buddhas, and "the self-arisen ones and those who remain after them" may refer to pratyekabuddhas and the elders or arhats who remain after a buddha, but the referents are not certain. "Corpses are turned into gold" renders རོ་དག་གསེར་འགྱུར literally; it may instead allude to an alchemical elixir that turns base metal into gold. "Those of lesser capacity" renders དམན་པ་རྣམས, the audience addressed by the Lord of Secrets. In the verse on the fearless, དེས་པ is read as ངེས་པ. This passage is terse and in places obscure. The lines on the boulder in the ocean, the line "the faded and the slack, are one in having an object," the couplet on "empty and existent, annihilation and permanence," and the lines on rivers, fire and gold are interpretive renderings of very compressed verse. The description of the four concentrations follows this text's own scheme, not the standard Abhidharma one. དགལ is read as དགའ ("delight"). Where the text says the illness is not overcome in stages (ནད), the word may be a slip for a similar-looking syllable, but it is translated as written. This passage is a list of dream and meditative signs and stages of yogic prowess. Many lines are terse, and the translation is necessarily interpretive in places, especially "wielding authority over Indra with a seal of command," "bringing the world down by a ladder," and the technical terms for the letters (nature, transformation, limbs). དེ་ཡིས་བོན is read as དེ་ཡི་ས་བོན ("its seed"). "Measure" renders ཆག་ཚད, taken as one's own bodily size. The "emblem (liṅga) of the expanse of space" here is not a ritual effigy but appears to mean a sign or figure visualized in space. ཆུ་ཁམས་རྟས་པ is rendered "the element of water congealed"; the sense of རྟས་པ is uncertain. The architectural terms in the description of the reliquary and palace (vase base, throne steps, joints of the wheels) follow stūpa terminology and are interpretive in places. The expression rendered "at ease" (gyin da, also in "effortless and at ease") is an uncommon, probably archaic word; its sense is inferred from its pairing with "effortless." In "to take a lesser body for the sake of taming beings," the word normally meaning "discipline" (vinaya) is taken in its sense of "taming, converting" beings, which fits the context of beings in lesser bodies. The line "the noble consort of the yoga of great meaning" is obscure; the rendering is tentative. "Were cut free by it": the Tibetan here appears slightly disordered (blo dang ldan gros kyis chod); the evident reading, that they gained insight and were held by means, is translated. The verse on aversion reads "is other than suchness" as written; doctrinally one might expect "is not other than suchness," but the text is translated as it stands. "All-Accomplished," "source of all qualities," "stainless sound," "perfect enjoyment," "secret body," and "continuous arising" are translated by meaning, as they could not be confidently identified as fixed Sanskrit names of particular buddhas. The names of the Blessed Ones in this sequence (Jewel Cloud, Source of Arrays, Lion's Roar, Beautiful Crest, King of Former Joy, and the rest) are translated by meaning, since they could not be confidently matched to fixed Sanskrit names; several may correspond to buddha names such as Ratnamegha or Siṃhanāda. "Cuts through the net of unknowing": the Tibetan reads "cuts the net that is cut of unknowing," where the first "cut" appears to be a scribal repetition; the evident sense is translated. As in the preceding passages, the names of the Blessed Ones are translated by meaning, since most could not be confidently matched to fixed Sanskrit names; Lord of Sages renders the common epithet Munīndra. "Luminous of itself": the Tibetan reads yang gis, evidently a slip for rang gis, "by itself." The name rendered "Mind" (yid dang) may be a slip for yid dga', "Joyful Mind"; it is translated as written. In "Suchness ... is the mother from which all arises," the phrase ma yin pas is read as "being the mother," which suits the identification with the perfection of wisdom in the following line; it could also be read as a negation. In the verse beginning "By knowing the one nature of all," the Tibetan reads thos pas, "by hearing," which makes little sense here; the evident reading thob pas, "since one attains," is translated. In the verse on the five senses, bde mchog is rendered "supreme bliss" rather than as the deity name Cakrasaṃvara, since the context is the sense of touch. "Being encompassed within the great reality" renders the uncommon 'ub chung, read as 'ub chub, "gathered in, encompassed." The lion's carcass alludes to the familiar image of a lion's body, which no other animal will eat and which is consumed only by worms from within. In the verse beginning "The stable and the moving," the Tibetan reads bsngags, "praise," which is taken as sngags, "mantra," fitting the series of maṇḍala, mudrā, meditative absorption, and mantra. The sentence after "a ho" is terse and its subject is not stated; it is rendered as describing the whole assembly passing beyond the tathāgatas into spontaneous great bliss, but the sense is uncertain. The sentence "Thus they extolled it" after the verse on the lamps lacks a qualifying word in the Tibetan. "The lowly ones" renders དམན་པ, referring as elsewhere in this text to the beings of lowly body (the Lord of Laṅkā and his companions) to whom Vajrapāṇi teaches. "Nothing at all remained perceptible" renders ཅང་མི་དམིགས་པར་གྱུར, which has no stated subject; it may mean that the Lord of Secrets vanished from sight. རིང་ལུགས་ཆེན་པོ, applied to Dānarakṣita, is an old title of uncertain exact sense (perhaps a ritual officiant or senior master); rendered "great master". The figure of 252 ślokas is given as the Tibetan states it, though it seems small for a text of this length.
